The Nibelungenlied

The Song of the Nibelungs — Siegfried's murder and Kriemhild's revenge, retold in modern English prose from the Middle High German

by Anonymous

c. 1200 2026

This is an AI modernization of The Nibelungenlied into contemporary English. The original is available from Bibliotheca Augustana (Bartsch's text of manuscript B). Also available as an epub.

Editor's Introduction

The Nibelungenlied, the Song of the Nibelungs, was written down around 1200 by a poet whose name nobody recorded, perhaps at the court of the Bishop of Passau on the Danube. It tells a story German-speaking singers had been reciting for centuries, and its oldest layer is history. In 436 the Burgundian kingdom on the Rhine around Worms was destroyed by Hun soldiers fighting for Rome, and its king Gundahar was killed. Attila the Hun died in 453 on the night of his wedding to a Germanic bride. Theoderic the Great, king of the Ostrogoths, ruled Italy from Ravenna and Verona until 526. In the poem they appear as Gunther, as Etzel, and as Dietrich of Bern, which is Verona, all alive at the same time and bound into one tragedy.

The poem has two halves. In the first, Siegfried of Xanten, a prince who has killed a dragon and bathed in its blood, comes to Worms to win Kriemhild, the sister of King Gunther. He helps Gunther win Brunhild, the warrior-queen of Isenland, by a deception, and years later the deception comes out in a quarrel between the two queens. Gunther's vassal Hagen of Tronje kills Siegfried. In the second half, Kriemhild marries Etzel, King of the Huns, and thirteen years later invites her brothers to his court.

It is a poem of 2,379 four-line stanzas, divided into thirty-nine episodes the poet called aventiuren, which this edition calls chapters. Its first readers were knights and court ladies, and much of it is ceremony: who greets whom first, who rides beside whom, what each guest is given. These scenes matter. Honor, loyalty and insult are measured in them, and every great killing in the poem begins with a slight at a feast. The poet also tells you, again and again, how things will end. This is not carelessness. Knowing that they are all riding to their deaths is how the story works.

The poem was forgotten for centuries until a manuscript was found in an Austrian castle in 1755, and in the nineteenth century it was claimed as Germany's national epic. The Nazis used it heavily; in 1943 Göring compared the army trapped at Stalingrad to the Burgundians in Etzel's burning hall. Wagner's Ring cycle is a different version of the same legend, built mostly from Norse sources.

This edition retells every stanza in modern English prose, translated from the Middle High German text of the manuscript known as B, with Daniel B. Shumway's 1909 prose translation consulted for sense only. The names are the poem's own: Etzel, not Attila, and Isenland, not Iceland.

Part One: Siegfried

Chapter 1: The Nibelungs

The old stories tell us many wonders: of heroes worthy of praise, of great hardship, of joy and feasting, of weeping and lament; and now you can hear wonders told of bold warriors' battles.

In Burgundy there grew up a girl of such noble birth that in all the lands there was none more beautiful. Her name was Kriemhild, and she grew into a lovely woman, and for her sake many fighting men would lose their lives.

She was made to be loved: bold warriors longed for her, and no one ever wished her ill. Her beauty was beyond all measure, and the young lady's graces would have been an ornament to any woman.

Three kings had her in their keeping, noble and powerful: Gunther and Gernot, two warriors of great renown, and young Giselher, a chosen fighter. The lady was their sister, and the princes had her in their care. These lords were open-handed, high-born, and bold beyond all measure in their strength, the pick of all warriors. Their land was called Burgundy. Later they did great wonders in Etzel's land.

They lived in their power at Worms on the Rhine, and the proud knights of their lands served them in honor until their final day. In the end they died wretchedly, through the hatred of two noble women.

Their mother was a mighty queen, the lady Ute. Their father was Dankrat, who left them his lands when his life was over, a man of great courage, who had won high honor in his own youth too. These three kings, as I have said, were of great courage, and the best warriors anyone has ever heard of were their vassals: strong, very bold, and fearless in the hardest fighting. There was Hagen of Tronje, and his brother Dankwart the swift; Ortwin of Metz; the two margraves Gere and Eckewart; and Volker of Alzey, a man of courage through and through. There was Rumold, master of the kitchen, a fine warrior; and Sindold and Hunold, who with him had charge of the court and its honors, the three kings' men. And they had many another warrior whose name I cannot give you. Dankwart was marshal; his nephew Ortwin of Metz was the king's steward; Sindold, a chosen warrior, was cupbearer, and Hunold was chamberlain. They knew how to keep up the honor of a great house. As for the strength of that court, the reach of its power, its high dignity and the knightly life these lords led with joy all their days: truly, no one could ever tell you the end of it.

In the midst of all this splendor Kriemhild had a dream. She dreamed she was raising a falcon, strong and beautiful and wild, and that two eagles tore it to pieces before her eyes. Nothing in this world could have grieved her more. She told the dream to her mother Ute, and Ute could explain it to the good girl no better than this: "The falcon you are raising is a noble man. Unless God protects him, you will lose him soon."

"Why are you talking to me about a man, dearest mother? I mean to live without any warrior's love. I want to stay as beautiful as I am until the day I die, and never have a man's love bring me sorrow."

"Don't swear it off so hard," her mother answered. "If you are ever to be happy with your whole heart in this world, it will be through a man's love. You will be a beautiful woman, if God sends you a truly good knight."

"Let it be, my lady," she said. "It has been proved in so many women how love is repaid with sorrow in the end. I will keep clear of them both, and then nothing can ever go wrong for me."

So Kriemhild shut her heart entirely against love. The good girl lived many a happy day after that without knowing anyone she wanted to love. Later, in all honor, she became the wife of a very bold warrior. He was the very falcon she had seen in her dream and her mother had explained to her. How terribly she would avenge him on her own nearest kin, who killed him in the end! For the death of that one man, many a mother's son would die.

Chapter 2: Siegfried, and How He Was Raised

In the Netherlands, meanwhile, there grew up the son of a most noble king. His father was Siegmund, his mother Sieglinde, and their home was a mighty castle, famous far and wide, far down the lower Rhine: it was called Xanten. The boy was named Siegfried, a quick and good fighter. His courage drove him to try his strength against many kingdoms, and his strength carried him into many lands. Ah, what quick fighters he would meet in Burgundy later on!

In his prime, in the days of his youth, there were wonders to tell of Siegfried: how honor grew on him, and how handsome he was. Later the loveliest women would lose their hearts to him. He was raised with all the care he deserved, and the virtues he took on came from his own nature. In time his father's lands would shine for it, because in everything he did he showed himself so truly a lord.

He was grown enough now to ride to court. People liked to look at him, many a lady and many a girl, and they hoped his heart would bring him there again and again. Plenty of them were fond of him, and the young lord was well aware of it. The boy was very seldom allowed to ride out without a guard. Siegmund and Sieglinde had him dressed in fine clothes, and men of experience who knew what honor was had charge of him too. So he was well on his way to winning both people and land.

He was strong enough now to carry weapons well, and whatever that called for, he had plenty of. With good sense he began to court beautiful women, and they loved bold Siegfried, honorably and well. Then his father Siegmund let his vassals know that he meant to hold a festival with his dear kinsmen. The news was carried into other kings' lands, and to strangers and to his own people alike he gave horses and good clothes. Wherever a boy was found whose birth and family entitled him to be a knight, he was invited to the land for the festival; these noble youths would take the sword alongside the young king's son.

There were wonders to tell of that festival. Siegmund and Sieglinde knew how to win great honor with their wealth, and their hands dealt out plenty of it; and so many a worthy man was seen riding into their land. Four hundred young men were to put on knightly clothes together with Siegfried, and many a beautiful girl was kept busy with the sewing, because they were all fond of him. The ladies set many a precious stone in the gold that they would work with braid onto the clothes of those proud young warriors. It had to be done. The host had seats set out for a great many bold men, for the midsummer when his son Siegfried would be given the name of knight.

Then many a rich squire and many a noble knight went to the cathedral. The older men were right to serve the young ones, as had been done for them in their day. There were pastimes, and the hope of great joy ahead. A Mass was sung in God's honor, and then a great crush rose among the crowd as the young men were made knights in the knightly way, with honors so great that the like of them may never be seen again. They ran to where many a saddled horse stood waiting. In Siegmund's courtyard the buhurt, the mock battle on horseback, grew so fierce that the palace and the hall rang with it; the high-spirited warriors made a tremendous din. From the seasoned men and the green ones alike came clash after clash, and the crack of breaking shafts rang up to the sky. Splinters flew out in front of the palace from many a warrior's hand: they went at it with a will. At last the host asked them to stop, and the horses were led away. Many a stout shield-boss lay broken there, and many precious stones had fallen on the grass from the bright shield-mounts, knocked loose in the clash.

Then the host's guests went where they were told to sit. Fine food in plenty took away their weariness, and the very best wine, which was carried to them in great quantities. Strangers and the king's own people alike were shown every honor. However much sport they had all day long, many of the traveling entertainers gave up any thought of rest: they performed for the gifts, which were rich there, and all of Siegmund's land was covered in praise for it. The lord had young Siegfried granted land and castles, as he himself had once been given them, and Siegfried's hand gave freely to the companions who had taken the sword with him. The guests were glad they had made the journey to that land.

The festival lasted until the seventh day. Mighty Sieglinde followed the old custom, and for love of her son gave out red gold; she knew how to earn her son the people's affection. Hardly a poor entertainer could be found there. Horses and clothes flew from their hands as if they had not one more day to live. I do not think any household ever showed such generosity. The festival ended with honor and praise. Later, the great lords were heard to say that they wanted the young man for their ruler, but Lord Siegfried, that handsome man, wanted nothing of the kind. As long as Siegmund and Sieglinde both lived, their beloved child would wear no crown. And yet he meant to be master over every act of violence that was feared in those lands, that bold and daring warrior.

Chapter 3: How Siegfried Came to Worms

Heartache rarely troubled the young lord. But he heard people telling of a girl in Burgundy, beautiful beyond anything you could wish for, and from her he would later gain great joy, and great hardship too.

Her extraordinary beauty was known far and wide, and her high spirit along with it, and so many a hero came to hear of the young lady that it drew guests in crowds to Gunther's land. But however many were seen courting her love, Kriemhild never once admitted to herself that she wanted any of them for her own. The man she would one day belong to was still a stranger to her.

Then Sieglinde's son set his heart on high love. Beside him, every other suitor's wooing was so much wind: he was the man who could earn a beautiful woman's love. And later noble Kriemhild did become bold Siegfried's wife.

Since he wanted to set his hopes on a lasting love, his kinsmen and many of his vassals advised him to court someone who was his equal. Then bold Siegfried said: "Then I will take Kriemhild, the beautiful young lady of Burgundy, for her beauty beyond all measure. I know this much: no emperor was ever so mighty that, if he wanted a wife, this royal princess would not be a fitting match for him."

The same news reached Siegmund. His people talked of it, and that was how he learned what his son intended, and it grieved him deeply that the boy meant to court that glorious girl. Sieglinde, the noble king's wife, heard it too, and she was badly frightened for her child's life, because she knew Gunther and his men well. People began to press the warrior hard to give the venture up.

Then bold Siegfried said: "Dearest father, I would rather go without the love of noble women for the rest of my life than court anywhere except where my heart has fixed its great love. Whatever anyone says, there is no arguing me out of it."

"If you will not turn back," said the king, "then truly I am glad of your resolve, and I will help you carry it through as well as I possibly can. But King Gunther has many arrogant men about him. If there were no one but Hagen, that warrior alone knows how to carry his arrogance so high that I badly fear it will bring us sorrow if we go courting that glorious girl."

"How can that hurt us?" Siegfried said. "Whatever I cannot get from them by friendly asking, my own hand can win by courage. I am confident I can wrest from them both their people and their land."

Prince Siegmund said: "I do not like this talk. If word of it were carried to the Rhine, you could never ride into that land at all. I have known Gunther and Gernot a long time. No one can win the girl by force," King Siegmund went on. "I know that for certain. But if you want to ride into that land with warriors, then whatever friends we have will be summoned at once."

"That is not what I have in mind," Siegfried answered, "that warriors should follow me to the Rhine as if on campaign. It would grieve me to force that lovely girl's hand that way. My hand alone can win her. I will go into Gunther's land as one of twelve, and you will help me with that, Father Siegmund." Then his warriors were given clothes of gray fur and fur of many colors.

Now his mother Sieglinde heard the news too, and she began to grieve for her dear child, for she was afraid of losing him to Gunther's men. The noble queen wept and wept. Lord Siegfried went to find her and spoke to his mother gently: "Lady, you must not weep on my account. Truly, I will be safe from every fighting man alive. Help me get ready for the journey to Burgundy, so that I and my warriors have clothes that proud heroes can wear with honor. For that I will be grateful to you, truly and with all my loyalty."

"Since you will not turn back," said Lady Sieglinde, "then I will help you on your journey, my only child, with the finest clothes a knight ever wore, for you and your companions. You will take plenty of them with you."

Young Siegfried bowed to the queen. He said: "I want no one on the journey but twelve warriors, and clothes should be made ready for them. I am eager to see how things stand with Kriemhild."

So beautiful women sat sewing night and day, and hardly one of them took any rest until Siegfried's clothes were finished. He would not hear of giving up his journey. His father had his knightly gear adorned, so that he could ride out of Siegmund's land in style. Their bright coats of mail were made ready too, and their strong helmets, and their shields, handsome and broad. Now the day of their journey to Burgundy drew near, and women and men alike began to worry whether they would ever come home again. The heroes had their weapons and clothes loaded on the pack-horses. Their horses were handsome, their harness red gold. If any men ever lived in higher spirits than Siegfried and his companions, they had no call to. Then he asked leave to ride to Burgundy, and the king and his wife granted it with heavy hearts. He comforted them both lovingly. "You must not weep on my account," he said. "Never be afraid for my life."

The warriors were downcast, and many a girl wept. I think their hearts told them the plain truth: that so many of their kinsmen would lie dead because of this. They had good reason to lament; truly, the grief was already upon them.

On the seventh morning the bold riders came onto the river sand at Worms. All their gear was of red gold, their harness finely made, and the horses of bold Siegfried's men moved at an easy pace. Their shields were new, bright and broad, and their helmets were splendid, as bold Siegfried rode to court in Gunther's land. No one had ever seen heroes so magnificently dressed. The points of their swords hung down to their spurs. These chosen knights carried sharp javelins, and Siegfried's was a full two hand-spans broad, with edges that cut horribly. They held gold-colored reins in their hands, and the breast-straps of their horses were silk. So they came into the land, and everywhere the people began to stare at them, and many of Gunther's men ran out to meet them.

High-spirited warriors, knights and squires, went up to the lords, as was only right, received the guests into their masters' land, and took the horses and the shields from their hands. They wanted to lead the horses away to rest. But bold Siegfried spoke up quickly: "Leave the horses standing, mine and my men's. We mean to be gone from here soon; I fully intend it. Whoever knows, let him not keep it from me: where can I find the king, mighty Gunther of Burgundy? Someone tell me." One of them, who knew well, answered: "If you want to find the lord, that is easily done. I saw him just now in that wide hall over there, among his heroes. Go in, and you will find many a splendid man with him."

By now the king had been told that fine knights had arrived, wearing white coats of mail and magnificent clothes, and that no one in Burgundy knew who they were. The king wondered where these splendid warriors could have come from, in their shining clothes, with such good shields, new and broad; and it annoyed Gunther that no one could tell him. Ortwin of Metz answered the king, and he had every right to, being both powerful and bold: "Since we do not know them, you should send for my uncle Hagen and let him look at them. He knows the kingdoms and the foreign lands too. If he knows who these lords are, he will tell us." The king sent for him and his men, and Hagen was seen striding to court with his warriors in lordly style.

Hagen asked what the king wanted of him. "There are warriors in my house whom no one here knows. If you have ever seen them, Hagen, tell me the plain truth."

"I will," said Hagen. He went to a window and let his eyes travel over the guests. He liked their bearing and their gear, but they were strangers to him in Burgundy. He said that wherever these warriors had come from to the Rhine, they might be princes themselves, or princes' envoys: their horses were handsome and their clothes very fine, and wherever they came from, they were men of high spirit.

"I will say this," Hagen went on. "Though I have never set eyes on Siegfried, I am ready to believe, whatever the truth of it, that he is the warrior striding there so grandly. He brings fresh news to this land. This hero's hand killed the bold Nibelungs, Schilbung and Nibelung, the sons of a mighty king, and since then he has done great wonders with his huge strength.

"Once, when the hero was riding alone, with no help at all, he came to a mountain, so I have been told, and found there, beside Nibelung's treasure, a great many bold men. They were strangers to him until he came to know them there. The whole of Nibelung's treasure had been carried out of a hollow mountain, and now hear a wonder: the Nibelung men wanted to divide it up. Siegfried the warrior saw this and it amazed him. He came so close that he could see the heroes and they could see him, and one of them said: 'Here comes strong Siegfried, the hero from the Netherlands.' Strange things he found among the Nibelungs. Schilbung and Nibelung welcomed the warrior, and with one voice the noble young princes asked the handsome man to divide the treasure for them. They begged him so eagerly that the lord agreed.

"He saw so many precious stones, as we hear tell, that a hundred wagons could not have carried them, and still more red gold from the Nibelung land, and bold Siegfried's hand was to divide it all. As his reward they gave him Nibelung's sword. But they were badly served by the service the good hero Siegfried was to do them: he could not bring it to an end, and they grew angry. Among their followers they had twelve bold men who were mighty giants. What good did it do them? Siegfried's hand killed them in his rage, and he subdued seven hundred warriors of the Nibelung land with the good sword called Balmung. Out of sheer terror of that sword and the bold man who held it, many a young warrior made the land and its castles subject to him. And the two mighty kings, he killed them both. Then Alberich put him in great danger: the dwarf meant to avenge his lords on the spot, until he found out what Siegfried's strength was. The mighty dwarf could not stand against him. Like wild lions they ran at the mountain, and there Siegfried took from Alberich the cloak of invisibility. Then Siegfried, that terrible man, was master of the treasure. All who had dared to fight lay dead. He had the treasure hauled and carried straight back to the place the Nibelung men had taken it from, and strong Alberich was made its keeper. He had to swear oaths to serve Siegfried as his servant, and he was ready for every kind of task."

So said Hagen of Tronje. "That is what he has done. No warrior ever had such strength. And I know one thing more about him for certain: this hero's hand killed a dragon. He bathed in its blood, and his skin turned to horn, so that no weapon can cut him, as has been proved many times over. We should receive this lord all the better for it, so that we do not earn the young warrior's hatred. He is so bold that we had better have him as a friend; he has done so many wonders by his strength."

The mighty king said: "You may well be right. See how boldly he stands there, ready for a fight, that bold man, he and his warriors. Let us go down and meet him."

"You can do that with honor," Hagen said. "He is of noble blood, a mighty king's son. From the way he carries himself, Christ knows, I would say it is no small matter that has brought him riding here."

The king of the land said: "Then let him be welcome. He is noble and bold, as I have heard, and he shall have the benefit of it in Burgundy." Then Lord Gunther went to where Siegfried stood. The host and his warriors received the guest so well that nothing was lacking in their courtesy, and the handsome man bowed to them, because they had greeted him so graciously.

"I am curious about one thing," the king said at once. "Where have you come from into this land, noble Siegfried, and what are you looking for at Worms on the Rhine?" The guest answered the king: "I will not hide it from you. I was told in my father's land that here with you were the boldest warriors any king ever had; I have heard it many times, and I wanted to know it for certain. That is why I have come. And I hear you yourself credited with bravery: that no king was ever seen bolder. People say so all through these lands, and I will not turn back until I know the truth of it. I too am a warrior, and I am to wear a crown. I mean to make them say of me that I hold my people and my land by right, and my honor and my head are the pledge. Now, if you are as bold as I have been told, then I do not care whether it pleases anyone or not: I will take from you by force whatever you have. Your lands and your castles shall be subject to me."

The king was amazed, and his men with him, at what they were hearing: that this man meant to take their lands from them. His warriors heard it, and anger rose in them. "How have I deserved this," said Gunther the warrior, "that what my father held so long with honor we should lose to the strength of any man? That would be a poor showing of our own knighthood."

"I will not give it up," the bold man answered. "Unless your land can keep its peace by your own courage, I will rule all of it; and if you win mine by your strength, then my inheritance shall be subject to you. Your inheritance and mine shall be staked alike, and whichever of us beats the other, all of it shall serve him, the people and the lands."

Hagen spoke against it at once, and so did Gernot. "We have no wish," said Gernot, "to conquer any land, and no wish for any man to lie dead at a hero's hands for it. We have rich lands that serve us by right, and no one has a better claim to them than we do."

His kinsmen stood there grim-faced, and among them was Ortwin of Metz. "This talk of peace sits badly with me," he said. "Strong Siegfried has challenged you without any cause. If you and your brothers had no defense at all, and if he had brought a whole king's army with him, I would still trust myself to fight so hard that this bold man would have every reason to drop his arrogance."

That made the hero from the Netherlands furious. "Your hand will not presume against me," he said. "I am a mighty king; you are a king's vassal. Twelve of your kind could never stand against me in a fight."

Then Ortwin of Metz shouted loudly for swords; he was every inch Hagen of Tronje's sister's son. That Hagen had kept silent so long troubled the king. Then Gernot stepped between them, that bold and cheerful knight. He said to Ortwin: "Put your anger away. Lord Siegfried has done us nothing we cannot still settle with good manners. That is my advice: let us keep him as a friend. It does us more credit."

Strong Hagen said: "It may well grieve us, and all your warriors, that he ever rode to the Rhine looking for a fight. He should have left it alone. My lords would never have done such a wrong to him."

Siegfried, that powerful man, answered: "If what I have said offends you, Sir Hagen, then I will let you see that these hands of mine mean to hold great power here in Burgundy."

"I will prevent that single-handed," said Gernot, and he forbade all his warriors to say a word in arrogance that might lead to grief. And Siegfried, too, thought of the glorious girl. "How would it suit us to fight you?" Gernot went on. "However many heroes lay dead for it, we would have little honor from it and you little profit." Siegfried, King Siegmund's son, answered him: "Then why are Hagen and Ortwin waiting? Why do they not rush to fight, with all the kinsmen they have here in Burgundy?" But they had to hold their tongues; that was Gernot's counsel.

"You are welcome among us," said Ute's son, "with the companions who have come with you. We will gladly serve you, I and my kinsmen." Then Gunther's wine was poured for the guests. The lord of the land said: "Everything we have, if you will accept it honorably, is at your disposal, and we will share it with you, our lives and our goods." At that Lord Siegfried's mood softened a little.

All their gear was taken and kept for them, and the best lodgings to be found were sought out; Siegfried's squires were made comfortable. From then on the guest was a welcome sight in Burgundy. For many days after that he was shown great honor, a thousand times more than I can tell you. His courage had earned it, believe me. Hardly anyone who saw him bore him any ill will.

The kings and their men kept themselves busy with sports, and whatever they took up, he was always the best at it. No one could keep up with him, so great was his strength, whether they were throwing the stone or hurling the javelin. And whenever the gallant knights, as courtesy demanded, passed the time in the company of the ladies, the hero from the Netherlands was always a welcome sight there, for he had set his mind on high love. Whatever was begun, he was ready for it. But in his heart he carried one lovely girl, and she carried him in hers, the lady he had still never seen, who in private often spoke of him with great fondness.

Whenever the young men, knights and squires, played their games in the courtyard, Kriemhild the noble princess very often watched them from her window. In those days she needed no other pastime. Had he known that she whom he carried in his heart was watching him, he would have had pastime enough for the rest of his life. And had his eyes seen her, I know it for certain: nothing in this world could ever have made him happier. Whenever he stood among the heroes in the courtyard, as people still do to pass the time, Sieglinde's son stood there so winningly that many a lady loved him for it with all her heart. And many a time he thought: "How is it ever going to happen that I see with my own eyes the noble girl I love with all my heart, and have loved for so long? She is still a stranger to me, and it leaves me standing here in sorrow."

Whenever the mighty kings rode out across their lands, the warriors all had to ride with them, and so Siegfried had to go too. The lady was sorry for that; and he, for love of her, often suffered great hardship. So he lived with the lords, it is the plain truth, a full year in Gunther's land, and in all that time he never once saw the lovely girl from whom so much joy would come to him later, and so much sorrow too.

Chapter 4: How Siegfried Fought the Saxons

Now strange news came to Gunther's land, carried by messengers sent from far away by warriors no one there knew, men who hated them. When the Burgundians heard the message, truly, it grieved them. I will name those warriors for you. One was Liudeger of Saxony, a mighty and proud prince, and the other was Liudegast, the king of Denmark, and for their campaign they had gathered many a splendid foreign fighter.

So their messengers had come into Gunther's land, sent there by his enemies. People asked the strangers what news they brought, and they were told to go quickly to court and stand before the king. He greeted them courteously. "Welcome," he said. "Who sent you here I have not yet heard. You should let us hear it," said the good king. But they were badly afraid of Gunther's grim temper.

"If you will permit us, King, to tell you the news we bring, we will not hold it back. We name to you the lords who sent us here: Liudegast and Liudeger. They mean to come looking for you in this land. You have earned their anger. Indeed, we heard it plainly that both lords bear you great hatred. They mean to march on Worms on the Rhine, and many warriors are helping them; you may take my word for that. Within twelve weeks the campaign must begin. If you have any good friends who will help you defend your castles and your land, show it quickly, for many a helmet and shield-rim will be hacked to pieces here by their hands. Or if you would rather negotiate with them, send word to them, and then the many companies of your strong enemies will not ride so close to you, bringing you heartfelt sorrow, the kind that costs many good and gallant knights their lives."

"Now wait a while," said the good king, "until I have thought it over better; then I will let you know my mind. If I have any loyal men, I ought not to keep this from them. Such hard news I must lay before my friends."

Mighty Gunther was grieved enough. He carried the message secretly in his heart. He had Hagen fetched, and others of his men, and he sent in great haste to bring Gernot to court as well. Then came the best men to be found there, and he said: "They mean to come looking for us here in our own land with strong armies. Take it to heart." Gernot, a bold and gallant knight, answered him:

"We will ward that off with swords," said Gernot. "Only those whose time has come will die there; let them lie dead. I cannot forget my honor over this. Our enemies shall be welcome here."

Then Hagen of Tronje said: "That does not seem good to me. Liudegast and Liudeger are arrogant men, and we cannot call up our forces in so few days." Then the bold warrior added: "Why don't you tell Siegfried?"

The messengers were given lodging in the town. However hostile they were, mighty Gunther ordered them well looked after, and that was well done, until he found out from his friends who would stand by him. In his worry the king was still deeply troubled. A gallant knight saw him brooding, a man who could not know what had happened to him, and he asked King Gunther to tell him what the matter was.

"It amazes me," said Siegfried, "how completely you have changed the cheerful ways you have long kept with us." Gunther, that handsome warrior, answered him: "I cannot tell everyone the trouble I must carry secretly in my heart. A man should lament his heart's distress only to steadfast friends." Siegfried's color went pale, then red. He said to the king: "I have never refused you anything. I will help you turn aside every sorrow you have. If you are looking for friends, I will be one of them, and I trust I can see it through with honor to the end of my days."

"God reward you, Lord Siegfried. I like what you say. And even if your courage never comes to my aid, I am glad to hear that you are so devoted to me, and if I live any time at all, you will be well repaid for it. I will let you hear why I stand here so sad. From my enemies' messengers I have learned that they mean to come looking for me here with armies. No warriors ever did that to us in these lands before."

"Make light of it," said Siegfried, "and calm your mind. Do what I ask you: let me win you honor and advantage, and ask your warriors to come to your aid as well. Even if your strong enemies could call on thirty thousand warriors to help them, I would take them on if I had no more than a thousand. Leave that to me." Then King Gunther said: "For that I will serve you as long as I live."

"Then have a thousand of your men brought to me, since I have none of my own here except twelve warriors, and I will defend your land. Siegfried's hand will serve you loyally forever. Hagen shall help us in this, and Ortwin too, Dankwart and Sindold, your dear warriors. And Volker, that bold man, shall ride with us and carry the banner; there is no one I would rather give it to. And let the messengers ride home to their lords' land. Let them know that they will be seeing us soon, so that our castles can be left in peace." Then the king sent for his kinsmen and his vassals.

Liudeger's messengers came to court, and they were very glad to be going home. Gunther the good king offered them rich gifts and gave them safe-conduct, and their spirits rose at that. "Now tell my strong enemies," said Gunther, "that they may as well stay home with their campaign. But if they want to come looking for me here in my lands, then unless my friends fail me, they will learn what hardship is." Rich gifts were brought out for the messengers; Gunther had more than enough to give, and Liudeger's men did not dare refuse them. When they took their leave, they went away in high spirits.

When the messengers reached Denmark and King Liudegast heard how they had come away from the Rhine, as it was told to him, their bold defiance truly grieved him. They said the Burgundians had a great many brave men, and that they had seen among them a warrior called Siegfried, a hero from the Netherlands. Liudegast was sorry when he understood that news properly. When the Danes heard this they hurried all the more to gather their friends, until Lord Liudegast had twenty thousand of his brave warriors for the campaign. King Liudeger of Saxony sent out his summons too, until between them they had forty thousand men and more, with whom they meant to ride into Burgundy.

Meanwhile, back at home, King Gunther had gathered his kinsmen, his brothers' vassals, and Hagen's warriors as well, the men they meant to lead to war. The heroes had need of them. Later, warriors would have to meet their deaths for it.

They threw themselves into getting ready, since they wanted to be gone. Volker the bold was to carry the banner as they rode out from Worms across the Rhine, and Hagen of Tronje was to command the troop. Sindold rode with them, and Hunold, men who knew well how to earn Gunther's gold, and Dankwart, Hagen's brother, and Ortwin too, men who could serve with honor on the campaign.

"Sir King, stay here at home," said Siegfried, "since your warriors are willing to follow me. Stay with the ladies and keep your spirits high. I trust I can guard your honor and your goods both. As for the men who wanted to come looking for you at Worms on the Rhine, I will see to it that they wish they had stayed home. We will ride so deep into their land that their arrogance turns to worry."

From the Rhine they rode through Hesse with their heroes toward Saxony, where the fighting would later be. With plunder and burning they laid the land waste, and both princes learned of it, to their grief. When they came to the border, the squires moved on ahead, and Siegfried the strong began asking: "Who is to guard the camp for us here?" Truly, no ride ever did the Saxons more harm. They said: "Let bold Dankwart guard the young ones on the road; he is a swift warrior. That way we will lose fewer to Liudeger's men. Let him and Ortwin have the rearguard."

"Then I will ride out myself," said Siegfried the warrior, "and keep the watch against the enemy until I find out exactly where their warriors are." Fair Sieglinde's son was quickly armed. When he set out he left the troops in the charge of Hagen and of bold Gernot, and then he rode alone into the land of the Saxons. Many a helmet-strap he cut through that day.

He saw the great army that lay camped on the plain, far more than the force he had with him: a good forty thousand, or more. Siegfried, in high spirits, looked on it gladly. Now a warrior on the other side had also gone up on watch against the enemy, fully armed. Lord Siegfried saw him, and the bold man saw Siegfried, and each began to watch the other with hatred. I will tell you who it was that kept the watch: a bright shield of gold lay before his hand. It was King Liudegast, guarding his own troops. The noble stranger came galloping at him splendidly.

Now Lord Liudegast too had marked him out as an enemy. They both drove the spurs into their horses' flanks and lowered their spears against the shields with all their strength, and the mighty king was suddenly in great trouble. After the thrust, the horses carried the two royal princes past each other as if a wind had blown them. With their bridles they wheeled round, like true knights, and the two grim men tried it with swords. Lord Siegfried struck so hard that the whole field rang. Out of the helmet flew fire-red sparks, as from great torches, struck by the hero's hand. Each had found his match in the other. Lord Liudegast struck many a grim blow too, and each man's full strength landed on the shields. Thirty of his men were standing watch nearby, but before they could reach him, Siegfried had won the victory with three deep wounds he dealt the king through his bright coat of mail, good as it was. The sword's edges brought blood out of the wounds, and King Liudegast was left in a sorry state.

He begged for his life and offered Siegfried his lands, and told him his name was Liudegast. Then his warriors came up; they had seen plainly what had happened between the two of them on the watch. Siegfried wanted to lead him away, but thirty of Liudegast's men attacked him. The hero's hand defended his royal hostage with huge blows, and after that the handsome warrior did worse harm still. Fighting for his life, he killed all thirty of them. One he left alive, and that man rode off fast and carried the news of what had happened here; the truth of it could be read on his reddened helmet. The Danes were bitterly grieved when they were told their lord was taken. It was told to his brother, and he began to rage with uncontrollable anger, for a wrong had been done him.

Liudegast the warrior was led away by Siegfried's might to Gunther's men, and Siegfried handed him over to Hagen. When they were told it was the king himself, their grief was not overwhelming. The Burgundians were ordered to tie on their banners. "Up!" said Siegfried. "More will be done here before the day is over, if I keep my life. Many a lovely woman in Saxony will grieve for it. Heroes from the Rhine, listen to me: I can lead you straight to Liudeger's army. There you will see helmets hewn by good heroes' hands. Before we turn for home, they will know what sorrow is."

Gernot and his men ran for their horses. The strong minstrel, Lord Volker, snatched up the banner and rode at the head of the troop, and the whole company was splendidly ready for battle. Still, they had no more than a thousand men, and the twelve warriors besides. The dust rose from the roads as they rode across the land, and many a splendid shield could be seen shining among them.

The Saxons had come too, with their companies and with well-sharpened swords, as I have since heard. Those swords cut hard in the heroes' hands, for they meant to defend their castles and their land against the strangers. The lords' commander led the troops forward. Siegfried had come up too, with the men he had brought with him from the Netherlands. In the storm of that day's fighting many a hand turned bloody. Sindold and Hunold and Gernot killed many a hero in the fight before the enemy learned how bold they really were. Many a noble woman would weep for it later.

Volker and Hagen and Ortwin dimmed the shine of many a helmet with flowing blood, storm-bold men that they were, and Dankwart did great wonders. The Danes gave a good account of themselves; the crash of shields rang out from the charges and from the sharp swords that were swung there in such numbers, and the battle-hardened Saxons did damage enough. But when the Burgundians pressed into the fight, they hacked open many a wide wound, and the blood could be seen running down over the saddles. That is how those bold, good knights went after honor.

The sharp weapons were heard ringing loud in the heroes' hands as the men from the Netherlands pressed after their lord into the thick of the enemy ranks. They came in bravely alongside Siegfried. Not one of the men from the Rhine could be seen following him. You could see the bloody stream running out through the bright helmets, drawn by Siegfried's hand, until he found Liudeger in front of his comrades. Three times now he had cut his way clean through the army and back. Now Hagen had come up, and he helped Siegfried do all that his heart desired in that battle. Many a good knight had to die before them that day.

When strong Liudeger found Siegfried, and saw how high he carried good Balmung in his hand and how many he was killing with it, the lord grew angry and grim enough. Then there was a great surge and a great clanging of swords as their men pressed in against each other, and the two warriors tested themselves all the harder. The ranks began to give way, and bitter hatred rose there. The ruler of the Saxons had already been told that his brother was captured, and it grieved him hard. He knew well enough that Sieglinde's son had done it; people were blaming Gernot, but he learned the truth of it later.

Liudeger's blows were so strong that Siegfried's horse staggered under the saddle. When the horse recovered itself, bold Siegfried took on a terrible manner in that fight. Hagen helped him well, and Gernot, Dankwart and Volker, and many lay dead because of it. Sindold and Hunold and Ortwin the warrior knew how to cut many a man down to his death in that battle. The noble princes stayed side by side in the storm of it. Many a spear was seen flying over the helmets and through the bright shields, thrown by heroes' hands, and many a splendid shield was seen stained the color of blood. In that hard fight many a man got down from his horse. Siegfried the bold and Liudeger ran at each other on foot; shafts were seen flying, and many a sharp spear. Then the shield-fittings flew off under Siegfried's hand. The hero from the Netherlands meant to win the victory over the bold Saxons, of whom so many wonders were seen that day. Oh, how many bright mail-rings bold Dankwart broke!

Then Lord Liudeger made out a crown painted on the shield in Siegfried's hand. He knew well now that this was the mighty man, and the hero began shouting to his friends: "Give up the fight, all my men! I have seen Siegmund's son here. I know him: it is strong Siegfried. The evil devil has sent him here to the Saxons."

He had the banners lowered in the middle of the fighting. He asked for peace, and later it was granted him, but he had to go to Gunther's land as a hostage. Bold Siegfried's hand had forced that on him. By common agreement they broke off the fight. They laid down out of their hands all the battered helmets and broad shields that could be found, and every one of them bore the color of blood, from the Burgundians' hands. They took captive whoever they chose, since they had the power to. Gernot and Hagen, those bold warriors, had the wounded laid on biers, and they led five hundred fighting men away captive to the Rhine.

The beaten warriors rode home to Denmark. Nor had the Saxons fought so well that anyone would give them praise, and that grieved those heroes. And the dead were sorely mourned by their friends. The Burgundians had the weapons loaded onto pack-horses again for the Rhine. Siegfried the warrior had fought splendidly with his heroes; he had done well, and every one of Gunther's men had to admit it.

Lord Gernot sent messengers to Worms. He let his kinsmen at home know how he and his men had fared, and that these brave warriors had won great honor. The pages ran, and the story spread. Those who had been grieving before now rejoiced for joy at the good news that had come to them, and the noble ladies were heard asking question after question about how the mighty king's men had fared. One of the messengers was told to go to Kriemhild. That was done very secretly, for she did not dare do it openly, since among those men was her own dear beloved.

When she saw the messenger coming to her chamber, beautiful Kriemhild spoke to him kindly: "Now tell me good news, and I will give you my gold. If you tell it without lying, I will always be your friend. How did my brother Gernot come out of the fighting, and the rest of my kinsmen? Are many of our men dead? And who did best there? You must tell me." The messenger answered at once: "We had no cowards anywhere. In earnest fighting no one rode so well, most noble princess, since I must tell it to you, as the noble guest from the Netherlands. Bold Siegfried's hand worked great wonders there.

"Whatever all the warriors did in the fight, Dankwart and Hagen and the rest of the king's men, however they fought for honor, it is so much wind beside Siegfried alone, King Siegmund's son. They cut down many a hero in the storm of battle, but no one could ever tell you the wonders Siegfried worked whenever he rode into the fight. Through their kinsmen he brought the women terrible grief. Many a woman's beloved had to stay behind there. His blows were heard landing so loud on the helmets that they brought the blood streaming from the wounds. In every virtue he is a bold and good knight.

"Whatever Ortwin of Metz did there, whoever he could reach with his sword was left wounded, or more often dead. But it was your brother who did the very greatest harm that could ever be done in battle. One must tell the truth about those chosen men: the proud Burgundians have carried themselves so well that they can keep their honor clear of every shame. Many a saddle was seen emptied by their hands, and the field rang loud with their bright swords. The warriors from the Rhine rode so hard that their enemies would have done better to avoid them.

"The bold men of Tronje did great harm too, when the armies met with their full strength. Bold Hagen's hand left many a man dead; there would be plenty to tell of it here in Burgundy. Sindold and Hunold, Gernot's men, and bold Rumold have done so much that Liudeger may regret forever that he ever declared war on your kinsmen at the Rhine. But the very finest fighting that was done anywhere there, first or last, that anyone saw, Siegfried's hand did gladly. He is bringing noble hostages into Gunther's land. That handsome man overpowered them by his courage, and King Liudegast must bear the loss of it, and so must his brother, Liudeger of Saxony. Now hear my news, noble princess: Siegfried's hand has captured them both. Never have so many hostages been brought into this land as are now coming to the Rhine through his doing. No news could ever be dearer to you. They are bringing five hundred or more of the unhurt, and of the mortally wounded, my lady, you should know, some eighty red biers into our land, most of them hacked down by strong Siegfried's hand. The men who in their arrogance sent their defiance to the Rhine must now be Gunther's prisoners, and they are being brought into this land with rejoicing."

Her bright color bloomed when she heard that news properly. Her lovely face turned rose-red, now that the handsome warrior, young Siegfried, had come out of that great peril in joy. She rejoiced for her kinsmen too, and rightly so. Then the lovely girl said: "You have told me well. For it you shall have rich clothes as your reward, and ten marks of gold, which I will have brought to you." That is why men are so glad to carry such news to rich ladies.

He was given his reward, the gold and the clothes. Then many a beautiful girl went to the windows and watched the roads, and there they saw them riding, many high-spirited men, into Burgundy. The unhurt came, and the wounded came too, and they could hear the greetings of their friends without any shame. The host rode out joyfully to meet his guests; his great sorrow had ended in joy. He received his own men well, and the strangers just the same, for nothing else would have been fitting for a mighty king than to thank those who had come back to him kindly, because they had won the victory with honor in the storm of battle.

Gunther asked for news of his kinsmen, who had been killed on the campaign. He had lost no one but sixty men, and they had to be mourned, as heroes always are. The unhurt brought many a hacked shield and many a battered helmet into Gunther's land. The troops dismounted in front of the king's hall, and a joyful clamor of welcome was heard. Then the warriors were given lodging in the town. The king asked that his guests be well looked after; he had the wounded tended and made comfortable. His goodness showed itself plainly in how he treated his enemies.

He said to Liudegast: "Now you are welcome here. I have suffered great damage through your fault; that will be repaid me now, if I have any luck. God reward my friends; they have given me joy."

"You may well thank them," said Liudeger. "No king ever won such noble hostages before. For fair treatment we will give great wealth, so that you deal mercifully with your enemies."

"I will let you both go free," said Gunther, "on condition that my enemies stay here with me. For that I want sureties, that they will not leave my lands without my leave." Liudeger gave his hand on it.

They were taken to rest and made comfortable. The wounded were bedded down kindly, and the unhurt were served mead and good wine, and the household could not have been merrier. Their hacked shields were carried off for safekeeping. Of bloody saddles there were plenty, and these were ordered hidden away so that the women would not weep. Many a good knight came home worn out from the war. The king looked after his guests wonderfully well; the land was full of strangers and of his own people. He asked that the badly wounded be cared for kindly. As for the enemies' arrogance, it had been laid very low. Those who knew the healing arts were offered rich pay, silver unweighed and bright gold besides, to heal the heroes after the ordeal of the battle, and on top of that the king offered his guests great gifts.

Those who had a mind to go home were asked to stay longer, as one does with friends. Then the king took counsel on how he should reward his men, for they had done his will with great honor. Lord Gernot said: "Let them ride home. But let them know that in six weeks they are to come back for a festival. By then many a man who now lies badly wounded will be healed."

Siegfried of the Netherlands asked for leave too, but when King Gunther learned what he wanted, he begged him lovingly to stay a while longer. If it had not been for the king's beautiful sister, Siegfried would never have agreed. Besides, Siegfried was too rich to take any pay, though he had well deserved it. The king was fond of him, and so were the king's kinsmen; they had seen what his strength had done in that battle. For the sake of the beautiful girl, he thought he would stay, in case he might get to see her. Later that happened: he came to know the girl just as he had wished, and later he rode home glad to Siegmund's land.

The host had knightly sports held at all hours, and many a young warrior joined in gladly. Meanwhile he had seats built on the river sand outside Worms for the guests who were to come to Burgundy. Right at the time when they were due to arrive, beautiful Kriemhild heard the news that the king meant to hold a festival for the sake of his dear friends. Then the beautiful women set to work in earnest on the dresses and headbands they were to wear. Ute, the great queen, heard the news of the proud warriors who were coming, and many rich gowns were taken out of the chests. For love of her children she had clothes made ready, and with them many a lady and many a girl were adorned, and many young warriors of Burgundy as well. And for many of the strangers, too, she had splendid garments made.

Chapter 5: How Siegfried First Saw Kriemhild

Every day now you could see them riding to the Rhine, the men who wanted to be at the festival. Many of those who came into the land for the king's sake were given horses and splendid clothes. Seats were made ready for them all, for the highest and the best, as we are told: thirty-two princes at that festival. And the beautiful women dressed for it as if in a contest with one another.

Young Giselher was kept very busy. He and Gernot, and both their companies of men, received the guests, strangers and old acquaintances alike, with great kindness, and truly they greeted the warriors as honor required. The visitors brought a great many gold-red saddles into the land, and fine shields and splendid clothes, to the festival on the Rhine. Many a wounded man was seen in good spirits afterwards. Even the men lying in their beds in the pain of their wounds had to forget how hard death was. People stopped mourning for the sick and injured and rejoiced instead at the news of the festival days coming, and at how well they would live at the feast. All the people who could be found there had joy beyond measure and more delight than they could hold, and great rejoicing rose over the whole of Gunther's land.

On a Whitsun morning, a great many bold men were seen going out in glorious clothes, five thousand of them or more, to the festival, and on every side the entertainments began, each outdoing the next. The host had his wits about him. He knew very well how deeply the hero from the Netherlands loved his sister, the girl everyone called more beautiful than any maiden alive, though Siegfried had never seen her.

Then the warrior Ortwin said to the king: "If you want to be at this festival with full honor, then you should let people see the lovely girls who live here in Burgundy in such great honor. What is a man's delight, what gladdens his heart, if not beautiful girls and splendid women? Let your sister go out before your guests." That advice was given to the joy of many a hero.

"I will gladly do that," said the king, and everyone who learned of it was very glad. He sent word to Lady Ute and her lovely daughter that they should come to court with their maidens. Then good clothes were fetched out of the chests, all the noble garments they could find folded away there, and bracelets and braided trimmings, of which they had plenty, and many a lovely girl dressed herself with care. Many a young warrior that day set his heart on being a fine sight for the ladies, and would not have traded the chance for a mighty king's land. They were eager to look at the women they had never seen before.

Then the mighty king ordered a good hundred of his men, his kinsmen and hers, to go with his sister and attend her; they carried swords in their hands. That was the court household of Burgundy. Ute, that great lady, was seen coming with her. She had chosen beautiful women for her company, a hundred or more, in rich clothes, and behind her daughter came many a lovely girl. They were seen coming all together out of the women's chambers, and then there was a great crush of heroes shoving forward, every one of them hoping, if it could possibly be done, to catch a happy sight of the noble girl.

Now the lovely one came out, like the red of dawn breaking from dark clouds. And the man who had carried her in his heart for so long was free of many a sorrow in that moment: he saw the lovely girl standing there before him in all her glory. Precious stones shone all over her dress, and her rose-red color glowed. Whatever anyone might have wished for, no one could say he had ever seen anything more beautiful in this world. As the bright moon stands before the stars, when its light falls so clear from the clouds, so she stood now before many a fine lady, and the spirits of the handsome heroes rose high at the sight.

The richly dressed chamberlains walked ahead of her, but the high-spirited warriors would not stop pressing forward to wherever they could see the lovely girl. And Lord Siegfried felt both joy and sorrow. He thought to himself: "How could it ever happen that I should love you? That is a fool's dream. But if I must stay a stranger to you, I would sooner be dead." Those thoughts turned him pale and red by turns. Siegmund's son stood there so graceful, it was as if a master artist had drawn him on parchment; people said that no hero so handsome had ever been seen. The ladies' escort ordered everyone to make way on all sides, and many a warrior did so. Those proud hearts gladdened many a man, and many a splendid woman was seen there in perfect courtesy.

Then Lord Gernot of Burgundy spoke: "Gunther, dear brother, the man who offered you his service so kindly — you should do the same for him, here before all these warriors. I will never be ashamed of that advice. Have Siegfried come to my sister, so that the girl may greet him. We will always profit by it. She who has never greeted a warrior shall greet this one, and that way we win that fine warrior for ourselves."

So the host's kinsmen went to where the hero was. They said to the warrior from the Netherlands: "The king has given you leave to go to court. His sister is to greet you. This is done to honor you." The lord was glad of it in his heart, and he carried joy there with no sorrow in it, that he was going to see fair Ute's child. With lovely grace she greeted Siegfried then.

When she saw that high-spirited man standing in front of her, his color flared. The beautiful girl said: "Welcome, Lord Siegfried, noble and good knight." And at her greeting his spirits rose high. He bowed to her with care, and she took him by the hand. How lovingly he walked at the lady's side! They looked at each other with tender glances, the lord and the lady, very secretly. Whether a white hand was pressed there in affection, out of heartfelt love, I cannot tell you. But I cannot believe it was left undone. She had quickly let him know that she wished him well. In the summertime, and in the days of May, he would never again carry in his heart so much high joy as he won there, when the woman he wanted for his love walked at his hand.

Many a warrior thought: "If only that had happened to me, to walk beside her the way I see him do, or to lie beside her — I would not object to that!" No warrior ever earned a princess so well. The guests, whatever kings' lands they had come from, had eyes for only those two. She was permitted to kiss the handsome man, and nothing so sweet had ever happened to him in all the world.

The king of Denmark said at once: "Because of this high greeting, many a man lies wounded by Siegfried's hand, and I feel that keenly myself. God never let him come into my kingdom again."

Then on every side they called for people to make way for fair Kriemhild, and many a bold warrior was seen walking with her in courtly order to church. There the handsome man was parted from her. She went into the cathedral with many women following her. The princess was so beautifully adorned that many a high hope was lost there; she had been made to be a feast for many a warrior's eyes. Siegfried could hardly wait for the singing of the Mass to end. He could thank his good fortune forever that the girl he carried in his heart was so well disposed toward him. And he loved the beautiful girl, as he had every reason to.

When she came out of the cathedral, the bold warrior was asked to go to her again, as he had done before. Now for the first time the lovely girl began to thank him for having fought so gloriously before so many heroes. "God reward you, Lord Siegfried," said the beautiful girl, "for earning the warriors' devotion with such true loyalty, as I hear them all say." Then he began to look lovingly at Lady Kriemhild.

"I will serve them always," said the warrior, "and I will never lay my head to rest until I have done their will, as long as I live. And all of it, my lady Kriemhild, is done to win your favor."

For twelve days, every one of them, the praiseworthy girl was seen at the warrior's side whenever she had to go to court before her kinsmen. That service was done for the warrior out of great affection. Every day there was joy and delight and a great noise in front of Gunther's hall, outside it and inside too, from many a bold man. Ortwin and Hagen performed great wonders. Whatever anyone wanted to play, those gallant heroes were ready for it, in full measure, and so the guests came to know the warriors well, and the whole of Gunther's land was adorned by it. Even the men who had lain wounded were seen coming out; they wanted their share of the sport with the household, fencing with shields and throwing javelin after javelin. Plenty of men helped them; there were great numbers of them.

For the whole festival the host had them cared for with the best of food. He had made up his mind to avoid every kind of disgrace that ever befell a king, and he was seen going among his guests in a friendly way. He said: "Good warriors, before you ride away, take my gifts. That is my intention; I will always be grateful if you do. Do not scorn what I have. I want to share it with you, and I do so gladly."

The men of Denmark answered at once: "Before we ride home to our own land, we want a lasting peace. We warriors need it. We have many a dear kinsman lying dead at your warriors' hands." Liudegast was healed of his wounds, and the lord of the Saxons had recovered after the battle, though they were leaving some of their dead in the land. Then King Gunther went to find Siegfried.

He said to the warrior: "Advise me what to do. Our enemies want to ride out early tomorrow, and they are asking me and my men for a lasting peace. Now advise me, warrior Siegfried, what you think is the right thing. I will tell you what the lords are offering: as much gold as five hundred horses could carry, and they would give it gladly if I let them go free."

Strong Siegfried said: "That would be badly done. You should let them ride away free, on the condition that those noble warriors never again come riding into your land as enemies. Have both lords give you their hands on it, here, as surety."

"I will follow that advice." With that they parted. His enemies were told that no one wanted the gold they had offered. At home their dear kinsmen were longing for the battle-weary men. Many shields heaped with treasure were carried in, and Gunther shared it out unweighed among his friends, five hundred marks apiece and for some a good deal more. Bold Gernot had advised him to do that. Then they all took their leave, since they wanted to go. The guests were seen going before Kriemhild and before Lady Ute, the queen, where she sat. Never were warriors sent on their way more handsomely.

The lodgings stood empty when they had ridden off. But the king stayed on at home in lordly style, with his kinsmen and many a noble man, and every day they were seen going to Lady Kriemhild. Now Siegfried, that good hero, wanted to take his leave too; he had no hope of winning what his heart was set on. The king heard that he meant to go, and young Giselher talked him out of the journey completely.

"Where would you ride to now, noble Siegfried? Stay with the warriors, do as I ask you, stay with King Gunther and his men. There are many beautiful women here, and we will gladly let you see them."

Strong Siegfried said: "Leave the horses standing. I meant to ride away, but I give it up. Take the shields back too. Truly, I wanted to go home to my own land, but Lord Giselher has turned me from it with great loyalty."

So the bold man stayed, for the love of his friends, and there was nowhere in all the lands he could have been so content; and the reason was that now he saw fair Kriemhild every day. For her boundless beauty the lord stayed on. They passed the time with many pastimes, except that her love tormented him and often gave him great distress. And for that love, later, the bold man lay pitifully dead.

Chapter 6: How Gunther Sailed to Isenland for Brunhild

Fresh news came up the Rhine. People said that over there lived many a beautiful girl, and good King Gunther thought he would win one of them for himself, and the idea sent the warrior's spirits soaring.

There was a queen who lived across the sea, and no one knew of her equal anywhere. She was beautiful beyond measure and her strength was enormous. For her love she threw the javelin against swift warriors; she hurled the stone a great distance and then leaped far after it. Any man who wanted her love had to win three games against that highborn lady, every one of them without fail, and if he lost at even one, his head was forfeit. The young lady had done this many, many times. A handsome knight by the Rhine heard of it, and he set his mind on that beautiful woman, and because of that, heroes would later lose their lives.

The lord of the Rhine said: "I will go down to the sea, to Brunhild, whatever comes of it for me. For her love I will stake my life, and I will lose it unless she becomes my wife."

"I advise against it," said Siegfried. "The queen keeps such terrible customs that any man who courts her love pays a high price for it. You would truly do well to give up the journey."

"Then here is my advice," said Hagen. "Ask Siegfried to carry the heavy load with you. That is my counsel now, since he knows so well how things stand with Brunhild."

The king said: "Will you help me, noble Siegfried, to win that lovely woman? Do what I ask of you, and if she becomes my beloved wife, I will stake my honor and my life for your sake."

Siegfried, Siegmund's son, answered: "Give me your sister, beautiful Kriemhild, that noble princess, and I will do it. I want no other reward for my pains."

"I pledge it, Siegfried, in your hand," said Gunther. "If beautiful Brunhild comes here into this land, I will give you my sister for your wife, and you can live happily with that beauty for the rest of your days."

So the noble warriors swore oaths on it. And from that their hardships grew far greater before they brought the lady to the Rhine; because of it, those bold men later found themselves in deep trouble.

Siegfried had to take the cloak away with him, the one the bold hero had won, at great risk, from a dwarf named Alberich. The bold and mighty warriors made ready for the journey. Whenever strong Siegfried wore that cloak of invisibility he had strength enough: the strength of a full twelve men on top of his own. With great cunning he won that splendid woman. The magic cloak was made in such a way that anyone inside it could do whatever he liked, and still no one could see him. That is how he won Brunhild, and sorrow came to him for it.

"Now tell me, Siegfried, before my journey begins: if we are to arrive at the sea with honor, should we take warriors into Brunhild's land? Thirty thousand fighting men could be summoned quickly enough."

"However many men we took," Siegfried answered, "the queen keeps such terrible customs that every one of them would die of her arrogance. I will show you a better way, bold and worthy warrior. We will go down the Rhine like knights traveling on their own, and I will name you the men it must be: just four warriors, we will go to the sea, and that is how we will win the lady, whatever happens to us afterward. I am one of the four; you must be the second; let Hagen be the third, and we may well come through alive; and the fourth Dankwart, that very bold man. A thousand others would never dare to stand against us in a fight."

"I would like to know one thing," the king said, "before we leave here, and I would be very glad of it: what clothes should we wear in front of Brunhild, what would suit us there? You must tell Gunther that."

"The very best clothing anyone ever found is worn at all times in Brunhild's land. So we must wear rich clothes before the lady, or we will be shamed when people hear the story told."

The good warrior said: "Then I will go myself to my dear mother and see if I can persuade her to have her beautiful maids help make us clothes we can wear with honor before that glorious girl."

Hagen of Tronje said, in his lordly way: "Why would you ask your mother for a service like that? Let your sister hear what you have in mind. Her help will serve you well on this journey to a foreign court."

So he sent word to his sister that he wished to see her, and the warrior Siegfried too. Before they came, the beauty had dressed herself to perfection. The coming of those bold men gave her no grief at all. Her household too was dressed as befitted it. The two princes came, and when she heard it she rose from her seat and went with perfect courtesy to receive the noble guest and her brother.

"My brother is welcome, and his companion with him. I would like to know," the girl said, "what you lords want, since you come to court. Let me hear how things stand with you noble warriors."

King Gunther said: "My lady, I will tell you. Along with our high spirits we have great cares to carry. We mean to ride courting, far into foreign lands, and for the journey we ought to have fine clothes."

"Now sit down, dear brother," said the king's daughter, "and let me hear exactly who these ladies are that you want to woo in other kings' lands." The lady took both those chosen men by the hand and went with them to where she had been sitting before, on rich cushions, I know it well, worked with fine figures raised in gold. There the men could pass the time pleasantly among the ladies. Friendly glances and kindly looks: a great many of those passed between the two of them. He carried her in his heart; she was as dear to him as his own life. Later beautiful Kriemhild became strong Siegfried's wife.

The mighty king said: "My dearest sister, without your help it cannot be done. We want to go adventuring in Brunhild's land, and there we will need splendid clothes to wear in front of the ladies."

The young lady said: "My dear brother, whatever help of mine can be of any use, I will show you that I am ready to give it. If anyone else refused you, that would grieve Kriemhild. Noble knight, you should not ask me anxiously; you should command me, like a lord. Whatever you want of me, I am ready, and I will do it gladly," said the lovely girl.

"We want to wear good clothes, dear sister, and your noble hand must help to make them. Let your maidens see it through so that it fits us properly, for nothing will talk us out of the journey."

The young lady said: "Now listen to what I say. I have silk of my own; now arrange to have gems brought to us on shields, and we will work the clothes." Gunther and Siegfried were ready to do that.

"Who are the companions," the princess said, "who are to be dressed to go to that court with you?" He said: "I make one of four. Two of my men, Dankwart and Hagen, go to court with me. And mark carefully what I tell you, my lady: each of us four is to wear, over four days, three changes of clothes apiece, and clothes so good that we leave Brunhild's land without shame."

The lords took proper leave and departed. Then Kriemhild the princess called thirty of her young ladies out of her chambers, maidens with great skill at that kind of work. Arabian silk as white as snow, and fine silk from Zazamanc as green as clover: into these they set the gems, and that made good clothes. Kriemhild, that glorious girl, cut them out herself. Well-made linings of the skins of strange fish, a rare sight for strangers' eyes, as many as could be got hold of, they covered over with silk, as the clothes were to be worn. Now hear great wonders told of that shining clothing. From the land of Morocco, and from Libya too, the very best silks that any king's house ever owned: they had plenty of those, and Kriemhild made it plain how fond she was of the two men. Since the heroes had set their hearts on so grand a journey, ermine furs seemed nothing special to them; over the fur lay silk as black as coal, the kind that would still look fine on swift heroes at a feast today. Out of Arabian gold shone many a gem. The ladies' labor was no small thing: within seven weeks they had the clothes ready. And by then the good warriors' weapons were ready too.

When they were all prepared, a strong little ship had been carefully built for them on the Rhine, to carry them all the way down to the sea. The noble young ladies ached from their work. The warriors were told that the fine clothes they were to take with them were now ready; just as they had wished, it was done. And now they would not stay by the Rhine a moment longer.

A messenger was sent to the companions, asking if they would like to look over their new clothes, in case anything was too short or too long for the heroes. Everything fit exactly, and they thanked the ladies for it. Everyone who came to look had to admit they had never seen anything better in all the world. So the men could wear them proudly at that court; no one could tell of finer clothes on a warrior. Great thanks were given, and not sparingly. Then the gallant heroes asked leave to go, and the lords did it with knightly courtesy. And bright eyes grew dim and wet with weeping.

She said: "Dear brother, you could still stay here and court other ladies, where your life would not hang on the outcome; I would call that well done. You could find a woman just as highborn closer to home."

I think their hearts told them what would come of it. They all wept alike, no matter what anyone said. The gold on their breasts grew dull with the tears that poured so thickly down from their eyes.

She said: "Lord Siegfried, let my dear brother be entrusted to you, on your loyalty and your good faith, so that nothing harms him in Brunhild's land." The bold man promised it into Lady Kriemhild's hand.

The mighty warrior said: "As long as my life lasts, my lady, you may be free of every worry. I will bring him safely back to you here on the Rhine; know that for certain." The beautiful girl bowed to him.

Their gold-colored shields were carried down to the sand for them, and all their gear was brought to them. Their horses were led up; they were ready to ride away. And the beautiful women wept and wept. The lovely girls stood at the windows. A strong wind stirred the ship and its sail. The proud companions took their places on the Rhine, and King Gunther said: "Now who is to be master of the ship?"

"I will," said Siegfried. "I can steer you well on the water from here, good heroes, believe me; the right waterways are well known to me." So they set out in high spirits from the Burgundians' land.

Siegfried quickly took up a pole, and the powerful man pushed them off from the bank. Bold Gunther took an oar himself, and so the swift, praiseworthy knights put out from the shore. They carried rich food with them and very good wine, the best to be found anywhere along the Rhine. Their horses stood comfortably; they were well looked after. The ship ran very smoothly; very little went wrong for them. Their strong sail-ropes were drawn taut, and with a good wind they sailed twenty of the old long miles before night fell, down toward the sea. Their hard labor would later bring those high-spirited men pain.

On the twelfth morning, as we hear tell, the winds had carried them far away, to Isenstein in Brunhild's land. Not one of them knew it except Siegfried. When King Gunther saw so many castles and the wide marches too, how quickly he spoke up: "Tell me, friend Siegfried, do you know whose castles these are, and whose this splendid land is?"

Siegfried answered: "I know it well. It is Brunhild's land and Brunhild's people, and the fortress of Isenstein, as you heard me say. There you may still see many a beautiful woman today. And I will give you heroes some advice: be of one mind and tell the same story; I think that is best. For when we go before Brunhild today, we will have to stand before the queen with great care. When we see that lovely woman among her household, you famous heroes must tell one tale and one only: that Gunther is my lord and I am his vassal. Then everything he hopes for will come to pass."

They were ready to promise whatever he asked of them; not one of them let arrogance stop him. They agreed to everything he wanted, and it served them well when King Gunther saw beautiful Brunhild.

"And I am not doing this so much out of love for you as for your sister, that beautiful girl. She is as dear to me as my soul and my own life. I will gladly earn it, that she becomes my wife."

Chapter 7: How Gunther Won Brunhild
