By now their ship had come so close to the castle that the king could see many beautiful girls standing high up at the windows. It annoyed Gunther that he did not know who any of them were. He asked his companion Siegfried: "Do you know anything about these girls looking down at us on the water? Whoever their lord may be, they carry themselves very proudly."

Lord Siegfried said: "Now look quietly over the young ladies and tell me which one you would take, if you had the choosing of it."

"I will," said Gunther, a bold and daring knight. "I see one of them standing in that window over there, in a dress white as snow. She is so beautiful that my eyes would choose her for her loveliness alone. If I had the power, she would have to be my wife."

"Your eyes have chosen well for you. That is noble Brunhild, the beautiful girl your heart has been straining after, and your mind and your spirit with it." Everything about her looked good to Gunther.

Then the queen ordered her fine ladies away from the windows. They were not to stand there for strangers to stare at, and they obeyed. What the women did next has since been told to us as well. They dressed up for the strangers, as pretty women have always done, and then came back to the narrow windows where they could see the heroes. It was done for the sake of looking.

There were only four of them who had come to the land. Through the windows the fine ladies saw bold Siegfried lead a horse out onto the sand, and King Gunther felt himself honored by it. Siegfried held that handsome horse by the bridle, a good and beautiful animal, very big and very strong, until King Gunther was sitting in the saddle. That is how Siegfried served him, and later Gunther forgot it completely.

Then Siegfried led his own horse off the ship as well. He had very seldom done such a service before, standing at any hero's stirrup; and the fine, noble ladies saw it all through the windows. The two handsome heroes were a perfect match: their horses and their clothes were snow-white and exactly alike, and their fine shields shone in the splendid men's hands. Their saddles were set with jewels and their breast-straps were narrow. They rode in lordly style up to Brunhild's hall, with bells of bright red gold hanging from the straps. So they came into the land, as their courage demanded, with newly sharpened spears and well-made swords that hung down to these fine men's spurs; the bold warriors' swords were sharp and broad. And Brunhild, that splendid girl, saw all of it.

Dankwart and Hagen came with them. We hear it told how those warriors wore costly clothes of raven black, and their shields were handsome, big, good and broad. The jewels they wore were seen to be from the land of India, and glittered gloriously on their clothes. They left their little ship by the water with no one to guard it, and so the bold, good heroes rode up to the castle. Inside it they saw eighty-six towers, three wide palaces, and one well-built hall of fine marble, green as grass; and there Brunhild herself was, with her household.

The castle was unlocked and the gates stood wide open. Brunhild's men ran out to meet them and welcomed the guests into their lady's land, and gave orders for their horses to be stabled and their shields taken from their hands. Then a chamberlain said: "You must give us your swords, and your bright coats of mail as well."

"That you will not get," said Hagen of Tronje. "We mean to carry them ourselves."

Then Siegfried began to tell him how things really stood. "The custom in this castle, I will tell you, is that no guest may carry weapons here. Let them take them away; that is the right thing to do." Hagen, Gunther's man, went along with it very unwillingly. The guests were given wine to drink and made comfortable, and many quick warriors in princely clothes were seen walking about the court on every side. And the bold strangers were stared at, a great deal.

Now Lady Brunhild was told the news that unknown warriors had come sailing in on the water, in magnificent clothes, and the good and beautiful girl began to ask about them. "Let me hear," said the queen, "who these unknown warriors standing so grandly in my castle may be, and for whose love these heroes have traveled here."

One of her household said: "My lady, I can say that I have never seen any one of them before, but one standing among them looks like Siegfried. You should welcome him well; that is my loyal advice. The second of the companions is so impressive that he would easily be a mighty king over wide princely lands, if he had the power and could hold them. He stands among the others in a truly lordly way. The third of the companions is grim, and yet handsome too, great queen. From the fierce looks he keeps giving, I think his mind runs to anger. The youngest of them is so fine; I see that rich warrior standing there with a maiden's modesty, so well-mannered and so winning. And yet we would all have cause to fear if anyone here did him any harm. However gently he keeps his manners, and however handsome he is, he could make many a fine woman weep if he once got angry. He is built for it: in every quality he is a bold and daring warrior."

Then the queen said: "Now bring me my clothes. And if strong Siegfried has come into this land for the sake of my love, it will cost him his life. I am not so afraid of him that I would become his wife."

Beautiful Brunhild was soon well dressed. A great many lovely girls went out with her, a good hundred or more, all finely adorned; the fine women wanted to see the guests. And with them went warriors from Isenland, Brunhild's men, carrying swords in their hands, five hundred or more; the guests did not like that at all. The bold and gallant heroes rose from their seats.

When the queen saw Siegfried, now you may gladly hear what the girl said. "Welcome, Siegfried, here in this land. What does your journey mean? I would like to know."

"You do me very great grace, my Lady Brunhild, generous prince's daughter, in greeting me first, ahead of this noble warrior standing here in front of me. He is my lord. That honor I would rather do without. He was born on the Rhine; what more need I tell you? It is for your love that we have traveled here. He means to woo you, whatever comes of it for him. Think it over now, while there is time: my lord will not let you off. His name is Gunther and he is a great king. If he won your love, he would ask for nothing more. Yes, it was that handsome warrior who ordered me to come here. If I could have refused him, I would gladly have stayed away."

She said: "If he is your lord and you are his vassal, then let him dare to try the games I set him. If he comes out the master, I will be his wife; and if I win, it will cost every one of you his life."

Then Hagen of Tronje said: "My lady, let us see these mighty games of yours. Before Gunther, my lord, had to yield to you, it would have to go very hard indeed. He is quite confident he can win a girl as beautiful as you."

"He must throw the stone and jump after it, and throw the javelin against me. Do not be in too much of a hurry. You may well lose your honor here, and your lives as well. Think it over very carefully," said the lovely woman.

Bold Siegfried went over to the king and told him to say everything that was on his mind to the queen; he need have no fear of her. "I will protect you from her with my arts."

Then King Gunther said: "Great queen, set whatever tasks you command, and even if there were more of them, I would face them all for the sake of your beauty. I will lose my head unless you become my wife."

When the queen heard that, she asked for the games to be hurried on, as was only proper. She had good fighting gear brought for herself: a coat of mail of red gold and a good shield. The girl put on a silk surcoat that no weapon had ever cut through in any fight, of fine silk from Libya, beautifully made, with bright embroidered borders shining on it.

All this while the warriors were being jeered at and threatened. Dankwart and Hagen were in no cheerful mood; they were worried about how the king would come out of it. They thought: "This trip is not going to do us warriors any good."

Meanwhile Siegfried, that fine man, had gone back to the ship before anyone noticed, where he found the cloak of invisibility lying where he had hidden it. He slipped into it quickly, and then no one could see him. He hurried back and found a great crowd of warriors where the queen was setting out her lofty games. He went in among them unseen, by the cloak's arts, so that not one of all those present saw him.

The ring had been marked out where the games were to take place, in front of many bold warriors who were to watch them. More than seven hundred of them were seen carrying weapons, and whoever won the game, they were to say so.

Brunhild had come. They found her armed as if she meant to fight for a whole kingdom. Over the silk she wore many bars of gold, and her lovely coloring shone out gloriously beneath. Then her household came, carrying a shield of pure red gold with bands of hard steel, very big and broad, which the lovely girl meant to fight behind. The lady's shield-strap was a noble band with stones on it green as grass, of many kinds, that flashed against the gold. The man that lady gave her heart to would have to be very bold. The shield the girl was to carry was a good three spans thick under the boss, so we are told, and rich enough in steel and gold that her chamberlain and three others could hardly carry it.

When strong Hagen saw that shield brought in, the hero of Tronje spoke in a grim temper: "What now, King Gunther? How we are going to lose our lives! The one you want to love is the devil's wife."

Hear more about her clothes, for she had plenty of them. She wore a surcoat of silk from Azagouc, noble and costly, and against its color many a splendid stone shone on the queen. Then they brought the lady a javelin, very heavy and big, sharp, strong and monstrous, huge and broad, the one she always threw, and its edges cut most horribly. Hear wonders told of that javelin's weight: a good three and a half measures of iron had gone into the forging of it, and three of Brunhild's men could barely carry it. Noble Gunther began to be badly afraid.

He thought to himself: "What is this? How could the devil out of hell survive her? If I were back in Burgundy with my life, she could stay free of my love for a long, long time."

Then Hagen's brother, bold Dankwart, said: "I am sorry from the bottom of my heart that we ever came to this court. We have always been called warriors; how we are going to lose our lives, if women are to get the better of us in this land! It galls me badly that I ever came into this country. If my brother Hagen had his weapon in his hand, and I had mine, then all Brunhild's men might walk a little more softly with their arrogance. You can be sure of that; they would take good care. And even if I had sworn a thousand oaths to keep the peace, before I watched my dear lord die, that beautiful girl would have to lose her life."

"We could leave this land uncaptured," said his brother Hagen, "if we had the armor we need in this extremity, and our good swords too. Then the strong lady's arrogance would be nicely tamed."

The noble girl heard very well what the warrior said. With a smile on her lips she looked back over her shoulder. "Since he thinks himself so bold, bring them their armor and put their sharp weapons into the warriors' hands."

When they had their swords again, as the girl ordered, bold Dankwart went red with joy. "Now let them play whatever they like," said the quick man. "Gunther cannot be beaten, now that we have our weapons."

Brunhild's strength was shown on a grand scale. A heavy stone was carried to her in the ring, huge and monstrous, very big and round; twelve bold, quick heroes could barely carry it. She always threw that after she had thrown the javelin. The Burgundians' fears grew very great. "God help us," said Hagen, "what has the king got himself for a sweetheart? She ought to be the foul devil's bride in hell!"

She rolled the sleeves back on her white arms. She took up the shield on her arm and raised the javelin high, and so the fight began. Gunther and Siegfried feared Brunhild's hatred, and if Siegfried had not come to help him, she would have had the king's life. He went up to him unseen and touched his hand, and Gunther, very alarmed, felt his arts at work. "What touched me?" thought the bold man. He looked all round and found no one standing there. Then Siegfried said: "It is I, Siegfried, your dear friend. You need have no fear at all of the queen. Give me the shield from your hand and let me carry it, and mark carefully what you hear me say. You make the motions; I will do the work." When Gunther knew who it was, it was a joy to him. "Now keep my arts hidden; you must never tell anyone. That way the queen will win very little glory from you, whatever she has in mind. Look how carelessly the lady stands there in front of you."

Then the splendid girl threw with all her strength, at a shield new, big and broad, that Sieglinde's son carried on his arm. The fire sprang from the steel as if the wind were blowing it. The edge of that mighty javelin broke clean through the shield, so that you could see flame leap out of the rings of the mail. The two strong men both staggered under the throw, and but for the cloak of invisibility they would have lain there dead. Blood burst from bold Siegfried's mouth. But he sprang back fast, and the good hero took hold of the javelin she had driven through his shield, and strong Siegfried's hand sent it back at her. He thought: "I will not pierce that beautiful girl." So he turned the javelin's edge behind his back and threw it butt-end first against her armor, so hard from his powerful hand that it rang out loud. The fire flew from her mail-rings as if the wind were driving it. Siegmund's son had thrown with real force: with all her strength she could not keep her feet against that throw. Truly, King Gunther could never have done it.

Beautiful Brunhild was on her feet again fast. "Gunther, noble knight, my thanks for that throw!" She thought he had done it with his own strength. A far stronger man had crept up on her. Then she went quickly, in a furious temper, and the noble, good girl lifted the stone high, swung it with great force far from her hand, and sprang after her throw, and all her armor rang. The stone had landed a full twelve fathoms away, and with her leap the beautiful girl outdid the throw. Then Lord Siegfried went to where the stone lay. Gunther heaved at it, and it was the hero who threw it. Siegfried was bold, very strong and very tall. He threw the stone further and jumped further too, and by his fine arts he had strength enough to carry King Gunther with him in the jump.

The jump was over, the stone was lying there, and nobody could be seen but Gunther the warrior. Beautiful Brunhild went red with anger. Siegfried had put King Gunther's death a long way off. When she saw him standing safe and sound at the edge of the ring, she spoke to her household, rather too loudly: "Come here, quickly, all my kinsmen and my vassals. You are all to be King Gunther's subjects now."

Then the bold men laid their weapons down and bowed at the feet of mighty Gunther of Burgundy, many a bold man of them. They thought he had won the games with his own strength. He greeted them lovingly; truly, he was rich in every grace. Then the admirable girl took him by the hand and granted him power over everything there, and Hagen, that bold and daring warrior, rejoiced at it.

She asked the noble knight to go with her into the wide great hall, and so it was done. Then the warriors were served better than before. Dankwart and Hagen had to let that be, with no ill will. Quick Siegfried was wise enough. He went and put his cloak of invisibility away, and came back to where many ladies were sitting, and said to the king, very cleverly: "What are you waiting for, my lord? Why do you not begin the games the queen is setting you so many of? Let us see at once what they are like." The cunning man acted as if he knew nothing about it.

Then the queen said: "How does this come about, Lord Siegfried, that you have not seen the games that Gunther's hand has won here?" Hagen of Burgundy answered her. He said: "You had put us in low spirits, my lady. Siegfried, that good hero, was down by the ship when the lord of the Rhine won the games from you. That is why he knows nothing about it," said Gunther's man.

"Good news for me," said Siegfried the warrior, "that your pride has been laid so low here, and that a man lives who can be your master. Now, noble maiden, you must follow us home to the Rhine."

The beautiful woman said: "That cannot be. My kinsmen and my vassals must hear of it first. I cannot leave my land so lightly as that; my best friends must be sent for first."

Then she had messengers ride out in all directions. She summoned her friends, her kinsmen and her vassals, and asked them to come to Isenstein without delay, and ordered that all of them be given rich and splendid clothes. Daily, late and early, they came riding in troops to Brunhild's castle. "Oh, no," said Hagen. "What have we done? We have waited here for beautiful Brunhild's men to our own great harm. If they come into this land now in strength, we do not know what the queen intends. What if she is so angry that we are lost? Then that noble girl was born to be the ruin of us."

Strong Siegfried said: "I will take care of that. What you are worried about, I will not let happen. I will bring you help here into this land, chosen warriors you have never yet known. Do not ask after me; I am going away. Meanwhile may God keep your honor safe. I will be back soon, and I will bring a thousand men, the very best warriors I have ever known."

"Only do not be too long," said the king. "We have good reason to be glad of your help."

"I will be back in a very few days," he said. "Tell Brunhild that you sent me."

Chapter 8: How Siegfried Went to Fetch His Nibelung Warriors

Then Siegfried went out through the gate and down to the sand, wrapped in his cloak of invisibility, and there he found a small boat. Siegmund's son stepped into it in secret and drove it away from shore as fast as if the wind were blowing it. No one could see the boatman. The little boat flew along, driven by Siegfried's strength, which was very great. People thought some uncommonly strong wind was pushing it. No: it was Siegfried driving it, the son of beautiful Sieglinde.

In the space of a day and one night, by his tremendous strength, he came to a land more than a hundred days' journey away. Its people were called the Nibelungs, and there he kept the great treasure. The hero sailed alone to a broad island, and the handsome knight quickly tied up the boat. Then he walked up to a mountain with a castle standing on it and asked for lodging, as travelers worn out from the road do.

He came to the gate and found it locked against him. Truly, these people guarded their honor, as people still do. The stranger began to pound on the door, which was well guarded, and inside he found a huge brute of a man keeping watch over the castle, with his weapons lying beside him at all hours. "Who is that," he said, "hammering so hard on the gate?" Lord Siegfried, standing outside, disguised his voice. "I am a warrior," he said. "Now unlock the gate, or before the day is out I will have made some of you angry out here, some man who would rather lie soft and take his ease." What Lord Siegfried said made the gatekeeper furious.

By now the bold giant had put on his armor and set his helmet on his head. The powerful man snatched up his shield, swung the gate open, and ran at Siegfried with savage fury. How dare he wake so many bold men! Swift blows came from the giant's hand. The noble guest began to defend himself, and then the gatekeeper burst the clasps of Siegfried's shield apart with an iron bar. The hero was in real danger now, and Siegfried began to be somewhat afraid of dying, the gatekeeper struck with such strength. For that, his master Siegfried thought well of him afterward.

They fought so hard that the whole castle rang with it, and the din was heard in Nibelung's hall. He overpowered the gatekeeper and then bound him, and the news of it spread through all the Nibelung land. Far through the mountain Alberich heard the fierce fighting: a wild dwarf, and a very bold one. He armed himself at once and ran to where he found the noble guest tying up the giant. Alberich was fierce and more than strong enough. He wore a helmet and mail on his body and carried a heavy whip of gold in his hand, and he ran at full speed to where Siegfried stood. Seven heavy knobs hung from the front of the whip, and with them he beat the shield on the bold man's arm so cruelly that much of it was smashed to pieces. The handsome guest began to fear for his life.

He flung the shattered shield from his arm and thrust his sword, a long one, back into its sheath. He had no wish to strike his own chamberlain dead; he held himself back, as his good breeding required. He rushed at Alberich with his strong hands, seized the old gray-bearded man by the beard, and hauled on it so roughly that Alberich screamed out loud. The young hero's handling hurt him badly.

The bold dwarf cried out loud: "Let me live! If I could be any warrior's bondsman but one — I have sworn him oaths that I am his subject — I would serve you till I died." That was a cunning man talking. He bound Alberich as he had bound the giant before, and Siegfried's strength hurt him badly. The dwarf asked: "What is your name?"

"My name is Siegfried," he said. "I thought you knew me well."

"How glad I am to hear it!" said Alberich the dwarf. "Now I have seen for myself, from your fighting, that you have every right to be lord of this land. I will do whatever you command, if you let me live."

Then Lord Siegfried said: "Go quickly and bring me the best warriors we have, a thousand Nibelungs, so that they can see me here." Why he wanted that, no one ever heard him say. He untied the giant and Alberich, and Alberich ran straight to where the warriors lay. Anxiously he woke the Nibelung men. "Up, heroes!" he said. "You are to go to Siegfried."

They sprang from their beds, all of them ready and willing. A thousand quick knights dressed themselves well and went to where they found Siegfried standing, and there was a handsome greeting, part of it in deeds. Many candles were lit, and they poured him spiced wine. He thanked them all for coming so fast. "You are to cross the water with me," he said, and he found the bold and good heroes very willing.

A good three thousand warriors had come at once, and from them the best thousand were chosen. Their helmets were brought to them, and the rest of their gear, because he meant to take them into Brunhild's land. "Good knights," he said, "I will tell you this: you must wear very rich clothes at court there, because many lovely women will be looking at us. So dress yourselves in fine attire."

Early one morning they set out. What swift companions Siegfried had won! They took good horses and splendid clothing with them, and so they came in knightly style into Brunhild's land.

The lovely girls were standing on the battlements. "Does anyone know," the queen said, "who those are that I see sailing so far out on the sea? They carry rich sails, whiter than snow." The king of the Rhine said: "They are my men. I left them behind near here on the journey; I sent for them, and now they have come, my lady." Everyone looked hard at the magnificent strangers.

Then they saw Siegfried standing at the prow of one of the ships in splendid clothes, and many other men. "Sir King," the queen said, "you must tell me: am I to receive these guests, or am I to withhold my greeting?" He said: "You should go out to meet them in front of the great hall, so that they understand we are glad to see them." The queen did as the king advised her, and by her greeting she set Siegfried apart from the others.

Lodgings were found for the guests, and their gear was taken into safekeeping. So many visitors had now come into the land that they crowded each other everywhere in their companies. Now the bold Burgundians wanted to go home to their own country.

The queen said: "I would be grateful to anyone who knew how to share out my silver and gold, of which I have so much, among the king's guests and mine." Dankwart, King Giselher's vassal, answered her: "Most noble queen, let me keep the keys. I trust myself to share it out," said the bold warrior, "and whatever disgrace I earn for it, let it be mine alone." That he was open-handed, the warrior made very plain.

Once Hagen's brother had taken charge of the keys, the hero's hand gave out gift after rich gift. Whoever asked for a mark was given so much that all the poor could live in comfort. He gave by the hundred pounds without counting, and plenty of men who had never worn such splendid clothes in their lives walked in front of the hall in rich dress. The queen heard of it, and truly, it grieved her.

The noble lady said: "Sir King, I could do without your chamberlain's plan to leave me nothing of my own wardrobe. He is squandering my gold. If anyone would put a stop to it, I would be grateful to him forever. He gives such rich gifts that the warrior must think I have sent for my own death. I mean to go on using it for a good while yet, and I trust myself to spend what my father left me. No queen ever had such a generous chamberlain."

Then Hagen of Tronje said: "My lady, let me tell you: the king of the Rhine has so much gold and so many clothes to give away that we have no need to carry off any of Brunhild's wardrobe." "No," said the queen, "for my sake, let me fill twenty traveling chests with gold and silk, so that my own hand has something to give when we come across to Gunther's land."

The chests were loaded with precious stones, and her own chamberlains had to stand by while it was done; she would not trust Gunther's man with it. Gunther and Hagen began to laugh at that.

The queen said: "To whom shall I leave my lands? Your hand and mine must first appoint someone." The noble king said: "Call forward whoever you think fit for it, and we will leave him here as ruler." The lady saw one of her highest kinsmen standing near her; it was her mother's brother. She said to him: "Now let my castles and my lands be in your keeping, until King Gunther's hand rules here."

Then she chose two thousand men from her household to travel with her to Burgundy, along with those thousand warriors from the Nibelung land. They made ready for the journey, and you could see them riding down onto the sand. She took eighty-six women with her and a hundred maidens as well, all of them very beautiful. They delayed no longer; they were eager to be off. Oh, how the people they left at home began to weep!

With courtly grace she left her own land. She kissed her close kinsmen, those she found around her, and after a proper leave-taking they came to the sea. To her father's land the lady never came again. On the voyage all sorts of games were heard, and they had plenty of amusement, for a true sea wind came to help them on their journey. So they sailed away from the land in great high spirits.

But she would not let her lord make love to her on the voyage. That pleasure was saved until they were home, in the castle at Worms, at the wedding feast, where in time she and her heroes arrived in great joy.

Chapter 9: How Siegfried Was Sent to Worms with the News

When they had been on the water a full nine days, Hagen of Tronje spoke up: "Now listen to what I say. We are slow with our news for Worms on the Rhine. Your messengers should be in Burgundy by now."

King Gunther said: "You are right. And no one would be so well suited to that errand as you, my friend Hagen. Ride to my country. No one can tell them about our journey home to court better than you."

Hagen answered: "I am no good as a messenger. Let me look after the baggage and stay on the river. I will stay with the ladies and guard their clothes until we bring them into the land of Burgundy. Ask Siegfried to carry the message. He has the strength and the courage to do it well. And if he refuses you the journey, then you must ask him courteously, as a friend, for your sister's sake."

Gunther sent for the warrior, and he came as soon as he was found. The king said: "Since we are drawing near my own lands, I ought to send messengers to my dear sister and my mother, to say that we are coming to the Rhine. That is what I ask of you, Siegfried. Do what I want in this, and I will be in your debt forever," said the good warrior. But bold Siegfried refused him, until Gunther began to plead with him in earnest.

"You must ride," he said, "for my sake and for the sake of Kriemhild, that beautiful girl, so that the glorious princess, like me, will owe you something for it." When Siegfried heard that, the warrior was very ready to go.

"Then tell me whatever you want sent. None of it will go unspoken. I will gladly carry it for the sake of that beautiful girl. Why should I refuse anything to the one I hold in my heart? Whatever you command, consider it done."

"Then tell my mother, Queen Ute, that we are in high spirits on this voyage. Let my brothers know how we have fared. You must let our kinsmen hear this news as well. And keep nothing from my beautiful sister: tell the girl that Brunhild and I send her our service, and tell the household too, and all my men. How well I have finished the thing my heart was always set on! And tell Ortwin, my dear nephew, to have seats built at Worms on the Rhine, and let the rest of my kinsmen know that I mean to hold a great wedding feast with Brunhild. And tell my sister this: once she has heard that I have come home to the country with my guests, she is to take great pains to welcome my dear bride. For that I will be in Kriemhild's service forever."

Lord Siegfried quickly took his leave of Lady Brunhild, as was proper, and of all her household, and rode to the Rhine. There could not have been a better messenger in all the world. He rode to Worms with twenty-four warriors. He came without the king, and when word of that got out, the whole household was in distress; they feared their lord had been left dead over there.

The riders dismounted in high spirits. Young Giselher, the good king, came quickly to meet them, and his brother Gernot with him, and how fast Giselher spoke when he saw that King Gunther was not at Siegfried's side! "Welcome, Siegfried. You must tell me where you have left my brother the king. I think Brunhild's strength has taken him from us, and then her high love has brought us a terrible loss."

"Set that fear aside, for yourself and for his kinsmen. My dear comrade sends you his service. I left him safe and well. He sent me ahead to be his messenger, with news for your country. However you arrange it, see to it quickly that I may see the queen and your sister. I have to tell them the message Gunther and Brunhild have sent. Both of them are doing very well."

Then young Giselher said: "You must go to them, for you have brought my sister great joy. She is sick with worry about my brother. The girl will be glad to see you — I will vouch for that."

Lord Siegfried said: "Whatever I can do to serve her, I will do willingly and loyally. Now who will tell the ladies that I want to go to them?" And handsome Giselher became the messenger for that.

Swift Giselher went to his mother and his sister, and found them together, and said: "Siegfried, the hero from the Netherlands, has come to us. My brother Gunther has sent him here to the Rhine. He brings us news of how things stand with the king. You should give him leave to come to court. He will tell you the true story straight from Isenland." Up to then the noble ladies had known nothing but worry.

They sprang up for their clothes and dressed, and then they asked Siegfried to come to them at court. He did so gladly, because he was glad to see them. Noble Kriemhild spoke to him kindly: "Welcome, Lord Siegfried, knight worthy of all praise. Where is my brother Gunther, the noble, mighty king? I think we have lost him to Brunhild's strength. Oh, poor girl that I am, that I was ever born into this world!"

The bold knight said: "Now give me my messenger's reward, you beautiful ladies, for you are weeping for nothing. I left him safe and well; I promise you that. The two of them have sent me to you both with this news. He and his bride send you their loving service, with a kinsman's affection, noble princess. Now stop your weeping. They will be here soon." She had not heard news so welcome in a long time.

With her snow-white hem she wiped the tears from her lovely eyes, and she began to thank the messenger for the news he had brought her. All her great sadness and her weeping were gone. She asked the messenger to sit, and he was very ready to. Then the lovely girl said: "It would not trouble me at all to give you my gold as a messenger's fee. But you are too rich for that. Instead I will be your friend forever."

"If I alone owned thirty kingdoms," he said, "I would still gladly take a gift from your hand."

"Then it shall be done," said the gracious princess, and she sent her chamberlain to fetch the messenger's reward. Twenty-four bracelets set with fine stones: that was what she gave him for his fee. And such was the hero's spirit that he would not keep them. He gave them away at once to her closest attendants, the women he found in her chambers.

Her mother offered him her service with great kindness. "I have a message to give you," said the bold man, "about what Gunther asks of you when he comes to the Rhine. If you do it, my lady, he will be grateful to you forever. I heard him ask that you receive his noble guests well, and that you grant him this: that you ride out to meet him on the riverbank in front of Worms. The king reminds you of this in all true loyalty."

The lovely girl said: "I am very ready to do it. Whatever I can do to serve him I will not refuse. It shall be done with a kinsman's loyalty." And her color deepened with the joy she felt. No prince's messenger was ever received better. If she had dared to kiss him, the lady would have done it. How lovingly he parted from the women!

Then the Burgundians did as Siegfried advised. Sindold and Hunold and Rumold the warrior had to work hard, setting up the seats on the riverbank outside Worms, and the king's stewards were found hard at work too. Ortwin and Gere would not be left out: they sent to summon their kinsmen from every direction and told them about the wedding feast that was to be held, and the beautiful girls dressed themselves up for it. The great hall and its walls, all of Gunther's palace, were decorated for the guests, and the king's hall was richly fitted out for the many strangers. So this magnificent feast began with great joy.

Then along every road in the land rode the kinsmen of the three kings, who had been sent for to wait on the travelers who were on their way home. Many a rich garment was taken out of the chests. Then came the news that Brunhild's people had been seen riding in, and there was great commotion from the crush of people in the Burgundian land. Oh, what bold warriors were to be found there on both sides!

Beautiful Kriemhild said: "My girls, those of you who want to be with me for the welcome, pick out the very best clothes from the chests, so that the guests will praise us and honor us."

Then the warriors came too, and had the splendid saddles carried out, all of red gold, on which the ladies would ride to the Rhine at Worms. There could be no better riding gear anywhere. Oh, how the bright gold shone on the black horses! Many a precious stone gleamed at them from the bridles. The golden mounting-stools were set on bright silk and brought to the ladies, and they were in a happy mood. In the courtyard the ladies' horses stood ready for the noble young women, as I have told you. You could see the horses wearing narrow breast-straps of the best silk anyone could tell you of.

Eighty-six ladies were seen coming out, wearing the headdresses of married women. They came to Kriemhild, those beautiful women, in their bright dresses, and many a lovely girl came as well, all dressed up, fifty-four of them from the land of Burgundy, and they were the highest-born to be found anywhere. You could see them walking with their fair hair under bright ribbons. What the king had asked for was done with care.

They wore rich silks, the best to be found, and so many fine garments in front of the foreign warriors that their great beauty was shown exactly as it deserved. Anyone who could feel ill will toward any of them would have to be a poor sort of man. There were many clothes of sable and ermine to be seen, and many an arm and hand was finely adorned with bracelets over the silk sleeves they were to wear. No one could tell you about all this finery to the end. Many a hand swung a finely worked belt, rich and long, over a bright gown, over fine skirts of Arabian silk. The noble young women were in very high spirits. Many a beautiful girl had a brooch sewn at her breast with loving care. Any girl whose bright complexion did not shine against her clothes had reason to be sorry for it. No king's family today has so beautiful a household.

When the lovely women had put on the clothes they were to wear, the men who were to escort them arrived at once: a great force of proud warriors, and with their shields they carried many an ashen spear.

Chapter 10: How Gunther Married Brunhild at Worms

On the far side of the Rhine, the king and his guests could be seen riding down to the shore in company after company, and many a girl was led to meet them by the bridle, for everyone who was to receive them stood ready. When the people from Isenland and Siegfried's Nibelung men came across in the ships, they hurried to land, and their hands were anything but idle, there where the king's kinsmen were found waiting on the other bank.

Now hear this too, of Queen Ute, the mighty lady: how she brought her girls down from the castle and rode out with them herself. Many a knight and many a girl made new acquaintances that day. Duke Gere led Kriemhild's horse by the bridle, but only as far as the castle gate; from there bold Siegfried had to serve her the rest of the way. She was a beautiful girl, and the young lady repaid him for it richly later on. Bold Ortwin rode beside Lady Ute, and knight and girl rode along together, two by two. At such a grand reception, we can safely say, so many ladies had never before been seen together.

Many a splendid buhurt was ridden by praiseworthy heroes on the way down to the ships before Kriemhild the beautiful; it would not have done to leave that out. And there many a lovely lady was lifted down from her horse. The king had come across now, and many a worthy guest with him. Ah, what stout lance-shafts splintered before the ladies' eyes! You could hear shield crash against shield as they charged, and ah, how loudly the costly shield-bosses rang in the press!

The lovely women stood at the landing as Gunther and his guests came down from the ships, and he led Brunhild by the hand himself. Bright jewels and bright clothes shone against each other. Lady Kriemhild went forward with perfect courtesy to receive Lady Brunhild and her household. White hands were seen pushing back the garlands from their brows as the two of them kissed; that was done out of courtesy. Then the princess Kriemhild said, well-bred as she was: "You are welcome in these lands, to me and to my mother and to all the loyal kinsmen we have." Bows were made all round. The ladies embraced each other again and again. No one ever heard of so loving a welcome as the two ladies, Lady Ute and her daughter, gave the bride; they kissed her sweet mouth over and over.

When all Brunhild's women had come up onto the sand, many a lovely woman was taken warmly by the hand by handsome warriors, and the beautiful girls were seen standing before Lady Brunhild. It was a long while before all the greeting was done, and truly, many a rose-red mouth was kissed. The two noble princesses still stood side by side, and that was a sight many a fine warrior was glad to see. Those who had heard it claimed before that nothing so beautiful as these two ladies had ever been seen now looked with their own eyes, and had to admit it was no lie. Nor could anyone find a trace of artifice on either of them. Those who knew how to judge women and lovely looks praised Gunther's wife for her beauty; but the wise ones, who had looked more carefully, said that Kriemhild must be ranked above Lady Brunhild.

Girls and women now went to meet one another, and many a beautiful figure was seen there in full array. Silken tents and many a rich pavilion stood all about; the whole field before Worms was full of them. There was crowding and pushing around the king's kinsmen. Then Brunhild and Kriemhild were asked to go, and all the ladies with them, to where there was shade, and the warriors of Burgundy escorted them there.

By now the guests had all got onto their horses, and many a splendid joust was ridden, lance through shield. The field began to throw up dust as if the whole land had burst into flame, and there heroes made their names. Many a girl watched what the warriors were doing. It seems to me that Lord Siegfried and his men rode many a charge back and forth before the tents; he led a thousand handsome Nibelungs. Then Hagen of Tronje came, as the host had told him to, and the hero broke up the buhurt gently, so that the beautiful girls would not be left covered in dust; and the guests obeyed him with good grace.

Then Lord Gernot said: "Let the horses stand until it starts to cool. Then we will set about serving the beautiful women before the great hall, and when the king wants to ride, see that you are all ready."

When the buhurt had died away over the whole field, the knights went to the ladies under the many pavilions to pass the time, with high hopes of joy, and there they whiled away the hours until they were ready to ride on. Toward evening, as the sun went down and it began to grow cool, they delayed no longer: many a man and woman set off for the castle, and many a beautiful lady was caressed with the eyes. Plenty of clothes were ridden to tatters by good heroes, by those high-spirited men, after the custom of the land, until the king dismounted in front of the great hall. Then the ladies were helped down, as high-hearted heroes do. Now the two mighty queens parted. Lady Ute and her daughter went with their household into a very spacious room, and on every side you could hear the great din of rejoicing.

The seats were made ready, for the king wanted to go to table with his guests. Beside him stood beautiful Brunhild, and she wore a crown in the king's land: truly she was powerful enough for that. Many a fine seat and good table was set with food, so we are told, with everything they could need and hardly anything lacking, and many a lordly guest was seen beside the king. The host's chamberlains carried the water around in basins of red gold. If anyone ever told you that men were served better at any prince's feast, there would be little point in it; I would not believe it.

Before the lord of the Rhine took the water to wash his hands, Lord Siegfried did what was his right: he reminded him, on his loyalty, of what he had promised him before he ever saw Brunhild at home in Isenland. He said: "You must remember what your hand swore to me: that when Lady Brunhild came to this land, you would give me your sister. What has become of those oaths? I went through great hardship on your voyage."

The king said to his guest: "You are right to remind me. My hand shall not be forsworn, truly. I will arrange it as best I possibly can." Then Kriemhild was sent for, to come to court before the king. She came with her beautiful girls to the front of the hall, and young Giselher sprang down the stairs. "Now tell these girls to turn back. No one but my sister alone is to go before the king."

So Kriemhild was brought to where the king was. Noble knights from many princes' lands stood in the wide hall, and they were told to stand still. By now Lady Brunhild had walked as far as the table. Then King Gunther said: "Sister, dear heart, by your own goodness, release me from my oath. I swore you to a warrior, and if he becomes your husband, you will have done my will most loyally."

The noble girl said: "Dearest brother, you need not plead with me. Truly, I will always be as you wish; whatever you command me, that shall be done. I will gladly promise myself to the man you give me, my lord, for a husband."

Siegfried's color rose at that loving glance, and the warrior offered Lady Kriemhild his service. They were told to step together into a ring, and she was asked whether she would take this handsome man. In girlish modesty she was shy about it, a little; but such was Siegfried's luck, and his happiness, that she had no wish to refuse him there and then, and the noble king of the Netherlands promised to take her for his wife. When he had pledged himself to her and the girl to him, Siegfried's arms were quick to give the lovely child a tender embrace, and then beautiful Kriemhild was kissed before the heroes.

When that had happened the company divided, and Siegfried was seen sitting with Kriemhild on the bench across from the king, with many a man to serve him, and the Nibelungs were seen going over to Siegfried's side. The king had taken his seat with Brunhild the bride when she saw Kriemhild sitting beside Siegfried, and nothing had ever grieved her so much. She began to weep, and hot tears ran down her bright cheeks.

The lord of the land said: "My dearest lady, why do you let the light of those bright eyes grow dim? You have every reason to be happy: my land and my castles are yours, and many a handsome man."

"I have good reason to weep," said the beautiful girl. "My heart aches for your sister. I see her sitting there beside your bondsman, and I will weep forever if she is to be ruined like that."

King Gunther said: "You would do well to say nothing now. I will tell you the whole story another time, why I gave my sister to Siegfried. Truly, she can live happily with that warrior all her life."

She said: "I will always grieve for her beauty and her breeding. If I knew where to go, I would gladly run away, and I will never lie close by your side until you tell me why Kriemhild is Siegfried's wife."

The noble king said: "Then I will tell you. He has castles, just as I do, and wide lands. Be sure of this: he is a mighty king. That is why I gave him the beautiful, praiseworthy girl to love." But whatever the king told her, she stayed downcast.

Now many a good knight hurried from the tables to the horses, and their buhurt grew so hard that the whole castle rang with it. But the host was heartily tired of his guests. He thought he would lie more softly beside the beautiful lady. He had not given up the hope in his heart that much joy was to come to him through her, and he began to look at Lady Brunhild lovingly. The guests were asked to leave off their knightly games, for the king wanted to go to bed with his wife. At the stairs of the hall Kriemhild and Brunhild met; there was no hatred between the two of them yet. Then their households came, wasting no time, and their noble chamberlains brought them lights. The warriors, the two kings' men, parted into two companies, and many a fighter was seen going off with Siegfried.

The two lords came to where they were to lie, and each of them thought of conquering his lovely lady with love. That put them both in a gentle mood. Siegfried's pleasure that night was very great indeed. When Lord Siegfried lay beside Kriemhild and caressed the young lady so tenderly with his noble love, she became as dear to him as his own life; he would not have taken a thousand other women in exchange for her alone. I will tell you no more of how he loved his lady. Hear instead how Gunther lay beside Lady Brunhild. That handsome warrior had often lain more comfortably beside other women.

The people had withdrawn, the ladies and the men, and the chamber was quickly shut. He thought he was about to caress her lovely body; but truly, it was a long way off yet before she became his wife. She went to the bed in a shift of white linen, and the noble knight thought: "Now I have here everything I ever wanted in all my days." Her beauty had every right to please him. The noble king's hand hid the lights away, and then the bold warrior went to where the lady lay. He lay down close to her, and his joy was great as the hero wrapped his arms around the lovely woman. He could have begun many a loving caress, if the noble lady had let him. But she grew so angry that it troubled him: he had expected a lover and found an enemy's hatred instead.

She said: "Noble knight, you will let it be. What you are hoping for cannot happen. I mean to stay a virgin — you had better understand that — until I have found out the truth." Then Gunther turned on her in anger. He wrestled for her love and tore her clothes, and the lordly girl reached for a belt, a strong woven band she wore around her waist, and with it she did the king harm enough. She tied his feet and his hands together, carried him to a nail, and hung him on the wall. Since he had disturbed her sleep, she denied him her love. Truly, her strength very nearly cost him his life.

Then the one who had thought himself the master began to beg. "Untie my bonds, most noble queen. I will never try to overpower you again, beautiful lady, and I shall hardly ever lie so close to you again."

She did not care how he felt, for she lay very comfortably. He had to hang there all night long until the day, until the bright morning shone through the windows. If he had ever had any strength, there was little of it left in him now.

"Now tell me, Lord Gunther," said the beautiful girl, "would it trouble you at all if your chamberlains found you tied up by a woman's hand?"

The noble knight said: "That would turn out badly for you, and I would have little honor from it either," said the quick-witted man. "By your own goodness, let me come to you now. Since my love is so hateful to you, I will never lay a finger on your clothes."

Then she untied him quickly and let him up. He went back to the bed, to the lady, and lay down so far away that after that he hardly ever touched her fine shift; and she wanted no more of it either.

Then their household came in and brought them new clothes, and plenty of them were ready for the morning. However cheerful everyone else was, the lord of the land was sad enough, even though he wore a crown that day. Following the custom they kept, and that was right to keep, Gunther and Brunhild delayed no longer but went to the cathedral, where Mass was sung. Lord Siegfried came there too, and a great crowd pressed in. Everything they needed stood ready for them, as suited their royal honor, their crowns and their robes. Then they were consecrated, and when that was done all four of them were seen standing joyfully under their crowns.

Many young men took the sword there, six hundred or more, in honor of the kings, I want you to know that. Great rejoicing rose in the land of Burgundy, and you could hear the shafts ringing in the hands of the new-made knights. The beautiful girls sat at the windows and watched many a bright shield flashing before them. But the king had drawn apart from his men; whatever anyone else was doing, the king was seen walking about in sorrow.

His mood and Siegfried's were not alike, and the noble knight had a good idea what was wrong with him. He went to the king and asked: "How did you get on last night? You must tell me."

The host said to his guest: "I have won myself shame and harm. I have brought the very devil home into my house. When I thought I was going to love her, she tied me up tight, carried me to a nail, and hung me high on the wall. There I hung in misery all night until day, before she untied me. And how comfortably she lay there! I complain of this to you as a friend, trusting in your good will."

Strong Siegfried said: "Truly, I am sorry to hear it. I will prove that to you, if you will let me without resenting it: I will see to it that tonight she lies so close beside you that she never keeps her love from you again." After all his hardship Gunther liked that talk well. Lord Siegfried said: "You can take heart. I think we fared very differently last night. Your sister Kriemhild is dearer to me than my life. Lady Brunhild must become your wife tonight.

"I will come to your chamber tonight," he said, "so secretly, in my cloak of invisibility, that no one will see through my trick. Send the chamberlains out to their quarters, and I will put out the lights in the pages' hands; by that you will know I am inside, and that I am glad to serve you. Then I will tame your wife for you, so that you can love her tonight, or I will lose my life doing it."

"So long as you do not make love to my dear lady yourself," the king said, "I am content. Otherwise do whatever you like with her, even if you took her life. I could put up with that; she is a terrible woman."

"I swear it on my loyalty," said Siegfried, "that I will not make love to her. Your beautiful sister is dearer to me than any woman I ever saw." And King Gunther fully believed what Siegfried said.

The pastimes had brought both joy and distress. Now all the buhurts and the uproar were called off, because the ladies were to go up to the hall, and the chamberlains told the people to clear the way. The courtyard was emptied of horses and people. A bishop led each of the ladies as they went to table before the kings, and many a handsome man followed them to the seats. The king sat there in good cheer and high hopes; he was thinking of what Siegfried had promised him. That one day seemed to him as long as thirty, for all his thoughts were fixed on his lady's love. He could hardly wait for the tables to be cleared. Then beautiful Brunhild was allowed to go to her rest, and Lady Kriemhild too, each to her own chamber. Ah, what quick warriors were seen going before the queens!

Lord Siegfried sat most lovingly beside his wife, in joy and without a care. She was stroking his hands with her white hand when he vanished before her eyes; she could not tell when. When she was playing with him like that and suddenly could not see him any more, the queen said to his household: "I wonder very much where the king has gone. Who has taken his hands out of mine?"

She let the matter drop. He had gone to where he found the chamberlains standing with the lights, and he began to put them out in the pages' hands; and by that Gunther knew it was Siegfried. He knew well what he wanted, and he told the girls and the ladies to leave. When that was done the mighty king himself barred the door and quickly shot two strong bolts across it. He hid the lights quickly under the bedclothes. Then a game began that there was no way around, between strong Siegfried and the beautiful girl, and to King Gunther it was joy and sorrow both.

Siegfried lay down close to the young lady. She said: "Now stop that, Gunther, if I mean anything to you, or you will suffer the same hardship as before." Later the lady made bold Siegfried pay for that.

He kept his voice hidden and said nothing at all. Gunther could hear well enough, though he could not see them, that nothing intimate was happening between those two; they had very little comfort on that bed. Siegfried behaved as if he were Gunther the mighty king and took the praiseworthy girl in his arms. She threw him out of the bed onto a bench nearby, so that his head cracked loudly against a stool. The bold man sprang up again with all his strength. He meant to do better this time; but when he set about forcing her, it went hard with him. I think no woman will ever again put up such a defense.

When he would not give up, the girl sprang up. "You are not to tear my white shift. You are a rough brute, and you will be sorry for it; I will see to that," said the handsome girl. She locked her arms around the splendid warrior and meant to tie him up as she had the king, so that she could have her peace in the bed. The lady took a hard revenge for the tearing of her clothes. What good were his great strength and his great power now? She showed the warrior what mastery her body had: she carried him by main force — there was no helping it — and jammed him hard between the wall and a chest.

"Oh no," the warrior thought. "If I lose my life now at a girl's hands, then every wife from this day on will be free to hold her head high against her husband, who would never have dared it otherwise."

The king heard it all, and feared for the man. Siegfried was furious, and shame came over him. With tremendous strength he set himself against her, and now, in desperation, he tried it again with Lady Brunhild. It seemed a long time to the king before he subdued her. She crushed his hands until the blood spurted out from under the nails, so great was her strength, and the hero did not like that. But in the end he forced the lordly girl to give up the unruly will she had asserted before. The king heard all of it, though he said nothing. Siegfried pressed her down onto the bed so that she screamed out loud; his strength hurt her terribly. She reached for her side, where she found the belt, and would have tied him with it, but his hand stopped her so hard that her limbs cracked, and her whole body with them. That decided the fight; and so she became Gunther's wife.

She said: "Noble king, let me live. Whatever I have done to you will be made good. I will never again resist your noble love. I have found out well enough that you know how to be a woman's master."

Siegfried stood back and left the girl lying there, as if he meant to take off all his clothes. He drew a golden ring from her hand, and the noble queen never noticed. He took her belt as well, a good strong band. I do not know whether he did that out of pride. He gave the two of them to his wife; later it cost him dearly. Then Gunther and the beautiful girl lay down together. He loved her as was his right, and she had to give up her anger and her shame. From his embrace she grew a little pale; and ah, how that great strength of hers drained away with her love! Now she was no stronger than any other woman. He caressed her lovely body tenderly, and if she had tried her strength again, what good would it have done her? Gunther had done all that to her with his love. How lovingly she lay beside him then, in tender affection, until the bright day.

Lord Siegfried, meanwhile, had gone out again, to where a lovely woman gave him a warm welcome. He headed off the question she had meant to ask, and for a long time he kept from her what he had brought, until she went crowned in his own land. But whatever he owed to give her, how little of it he ever withheld!

In the morning the host was in far better spirits than he had been the day before, and that made for good cheer all over his land, among many a noble man he had invited to his house; they were served to their hearts' content. The wedding feast lasted until the fourteenth day, and in all that time the noise of every kind of joy that anyone could devise never stopped, and the king's expense was reckoned very high. The noble host's kinsmen, as the king ordered, gave in his honor clothes and red gold, horses and silver too, to many a man from foreign lands; and those who asked for gifts went away happy. Lord Siegfried of the Netherlands and his thousand men gave away every garment they had brought to the Rhine, and the horses and saddles too; they knew how to live like lords. Before all the rich gifts had been handed out, those who wanted to go home thought it was taking too long. Never were guests better looked after. So the wedding feast ended, as Gunther the warrior wished.

Chapter 11: How Siegfried Took His Wife Home

When the guests had all ridden away, Siegmund's son said to his household: "We should get ready too, and go home to my lands." His wife was glad when she understood that he meant it. She said to her husband: "When are we to leave? I would rather not rush off in such a hurry. First my brothers must divide the lands with me." Siegfried did not like it when he heard that from Kriemhild.

The princes came to him, and all three of them said: "Know this, Lord Siegfried: our loyal service is yours, ready at your call until the day we die." He bowed to the warriors when it was offered so kindly. "We will share with you as well," said young Giselher, "the lands and castles that belong to us, and whatever part of the wide kingdom is subject to us. You and Kriemhild shall have a good share of it."

When Siegmund's son heard and saw what the lords intended, he said to the princes: "God grant that your inheritance brings you blessing forever, and the people in it too. My dear wife can easily do without the share you wanted to give her. Where she is going to wear a crown, if I live to see it, she will be richer than anyone alive. Whatever else you command, I am at your service."

Then Lady Kriemhild said: "You may do without the inheritance, but with the Burgundian warriors it is not settled so easily. A king would gladly lead men like these to his land. Truly, my dear brothers' hands must share them with me."

Lord Gernot said: "Then take whoever you want. You will find plenty here who would gladly ride with you. Of our three thousand warriors we give you a thousand men to be your own household." Kriemhild sent for Hagen of Tronje and for Ortwin, to ask whether they and their kinsmen would be hers. At that Hagen flew into a rage. He said: "Gunther cannot give us away to anyone on earth. Let the rest of your household follow you. You know the custom of the men of Tronje well enough: we must stay here at court with the kings. We will go on serving the lords we have followed until now."

They let the matter drop and got ready to leave. Lady Kriemhild took a noble retinue for herself: thirty-two maidens and five hundred men. Count Eckewart went with Siegfried. They all took their leave, knights and squires, maidens and ladies, as was only right. They parted with kisses, and left King Gunther's land in high spirits. Their kinsmen escorted them a long way on the road, and all through the kings' land, wherever they chose to stop, lodging was ordered for them for the night.

Then messengers were sent racing ahead to Siegmund, so that he and Sieglinde should know that his son was coming, and with him Ute's child, beautiful Kriemhild, from Worms across the Rhine. No news could have pleased them more. "How lucky I am," said Siegmund, "to have lived to see beautiful Kriemhild walk crowned here. All my inheritance is the richer for it. My son, noble Siegfried, shall be king here himself."

Then noble Sieglinde gave out red samite in plenty, and silver, and heavy gold: that was the messengers' reward. She rejoiced at the news, and her household dressed with care, as was fitting for them. They were told who was coming into the land with him, and at once seats were ordered to be set up where he would walk crowned before his kinsmen. Then King Siegmund's men rode out to meet him.

If anyone was ever better received than those famous heroes in Siegmund's land, I do not know of it. Beautiful Sieglinde rode out to meet Kriemhild with many lovely ladies, and handsome knights followed her, a full day's journey, before the guests came into sight. The people of the country and the strangers both had a hard, uncomfortable road until they reached a broad castle called Xanten, where the two of them would later wear the crown.

With laughing faces Sieglinde and Siegmund kissed Kriemhild again and again for love, and Siegfried too; their sorrow was lifted from them. All their company was warmly welcomed. The guests were brought before Siegmund's hall, and the beautiful young ladies were lifted down from their palfreys. There were men by the hundred, and they set about serving the lovely ladies with a will.

However great their wedding feast on the Rhine was known to have been, here the heroes were given far better clothes than they had ever worn in all their days. Great wonders could be told of their wealth. When they sat there in their high honor with everything they could want, what gold-colored gowns their retinue wore, with pearls and precious stones worked into them! So carefully did Sieglinde, the noble queen, look after them.

Then Lord Siegmund said before his kinsmen: "I make it known to all of Siegfried's family: he shall wear my crown before these warriors." The people of the Netherlands were glad to hear it. He handed over to him his courts of justice, his crown, and his land, and from then on Siegfried was master of them all. Whenever he sat in judgment and had to give a ruling, it was carried out in such a way that men went in great fear of beautiful Kriemhild's husband.

In this high honor he lived, it is the plain truth, and ruled under the crown until the tenth year, when his beautiful wife bore a son. That was just what the king's kinsmen had wished for. They hurried to have him baptized and gave him a name: Gunther, after his uncle, and he had no need to be ashamed of that. If he took after his kinsmen, things would go well for him. They raised him with care, and with good reason.

In those same days Lady Sieglinde died. Then the noble daughter of Ute held all the power, as was right for so great a lady over the lands. Many people grieved that death had taken Sieglinde from them.

Now on the Rhine as well, so we hear tell, beautiful Brunhild had borne mighty Gunther a son, in Burgundy. For the hero's sake they named him Siegfried. How carefully they had him watched over! Noble Gunther gave him tutors who knew how to raise him into a fine man. Oh, how much misfortune later robbed him of his kinsmen!

Stories were told all the time of how gloriously the handsome warriors lived in Siegmund's land, and Gunther lived the same way with his distinguished kinsmen. The Nibelung land served Siegfried here, and Nibelung's warriors, and the wealth of both; none of his kinsmen was ever richer than he. The bold man held his head all the higher for it. The greatest treasure a hero ever won, apart from those who had held it before him, the bold man now had: he had won it with his own hand before a mountain, and for its sake he had struck many a fine knight dead. He had honor to his heart's content, and even if he had not, anyone would still have to admit that the noble warrior was one of the best men who ever sat a horse. Men feared his strength, and they were right to.

Chapter 12: How Gunther Invited Siegfried to a Festival

Now Gunther's wife was thinking, all the time: "How grandly Lady Kriemhild carries herself! And yet her husband Siegfried is our bondsman, and for a long while now he has done us very little service." She carried this in her heart and kept it well hidden. That they stayed such strangers to her, that so little service ever came to her from Siegfried's land, grieved her deeply, and she would have given a great deal to know the reason.

So she tried the king, to see whether it might be arranged for her to see Kriemhild again. She said in private what she had in mind, and the lord did not think much of the idea. "How could we bring them here to this land?" said the mighty king. "It would be impossible. They live too far from us, and I would not dare to ask it of them." Lady Brunhild answered him with a cunning turn: "However high and mighty a king's vassal may be, whatever his lord commands him, he still must not leave it undone." Gunther smiled when she said that. However often he saw Siegfried, he never counted it as a vassal's service.

"Dearest lord," she said, "for my sake, help me bring Siegfried and your sister to this land, so that we can see them here. Truly, nothing could ever make me happier. Your sister's good breeding and her gracious spirit: whenever I think of them, how it warms me, how we sat together when I first became your wife. She may love bold Siegfried with honor."

She begged him so long that at last the king said: "I tell you, there are no guests I would be gladder to see. You need not press me hard. I will send my messengers for the two of them, so that they come to us on the Rhine."

Then the queen said: "Then you must tell me when you mean to send for them, and in how many days our dear kinsmen are to come into the land. And the men you mean to send, let me know who they are."

"I will," said the prince. "I will let thirty of my men ride there." He had them come before him, and by them he sent his message to Siegfried's land. To their delight, Brunhild gave them very splendid clothes.

Then King Gunther said: "You warriors shall say from me, and hold back nothing that I send, to strong Siegfried and to my sister, that no one in the world could be fonder of them than I am. And ask them both to come to us on the Rhine; for that, I and my lady will be forever at their service. Before this midsummer he and his men shall see many here who wish him great honor. To King Siegmund tell my service, that I and my people will always be his friends. And tell my sister, too, that she must not fail to ride to her kinsmen. Never did a festival suit her better."

Brunhild and Ute and every lady to be found at court all sent their service to Siegfried's land, to the lovely women there and to many a bold man. With the counsel of the king's kinsmen the messengers set out. They traveled fitted out for the road; their horses and their clothing had all been brought to them, and then they left the land. They were eager to reach the place they were going, and the king had the messengers carefully guarded with an escort. In three weeks they came riding into the land, to Nibelung's castle in the border country of Norway, where they had been sent, and there they found the warriors. The messengers' horses were very weary from the long roads.

Siegfried and Kriemhild were both told that knights had arrived wearing clothes of the kind that was the fashion in Burgundy. She sprang from a couch where she lay resting and asked a maid to go to a window. The girl saw bold Gere standing in the courtyard, him and the companions who had been sent there with him. Against her heartache at being so far from home, what joyful news she found!

She said to the king: "Now look, there they stand, the men walking in the courtyard with strong Gere, the ones my brother Gunther has sent us down the Rhine." Then strong Siegfried said: "They shall be welcome to us."

The whole household ran to wherever the messengers could be seen, and each of them in turn spoke to the envoys very kindly, as well as he knew how. Lord Siegmund was very glad of their coming. Then Gere and his men were given lodgings, and the horses were ordered looked after. The messengers went to where Lord Siegfried sat beside Kriemhild; they had been given leave to come to court, so that is what they did. The host and his wife stood up at once. Gere of Burgundy was warmly received, with his companions, Gunther's men, and mighty Gere was asked to go and sit down.

"Allow us our message before we go and sit. Let us travel-weary guests stand for now. We are to tell you news of what Gunther and Brunhild have sent you; their affairs stand very high. And also what Lady Ute, your mother, sends. Young Giselher and Lord Gernot too and your best kinsmen have sent us here. They send you their service from Gunther's land."

"Now God reward them," said Siegfried. "I trust them fully, for loyalty and good will, as one should trust kinsmen; and so does their sister. You must tell us more: are our dear kinsmen at home in good spirits? Since we parted from them, has anyone done anything to my wife's kinsmen? You must let me know. I will always help them bear it in loyalty, until their enemies have reason to curse my service."

Then Margrave Gere, a very good warrior, said: "They are in every virtue so high of heart. They invite you to the Rhine for a festival. They would be very glad to see you, you may be sure of that. And they ask my lady to come there with you, once the winter has come to an end. Before this midsummer, they would like to see you." Then strong Siegfried said: "That could hardly happen."

Then Gere of Burgundy spoke again: "Your mother Ute, Gernot and Giselher have urged you: you must not refuse them. That you live so far from them, I hear lamented every day. Brunhild my lady and all her maidens rejoice at the news, and if it could be that they saw you again, it would lift their hearts high." That news sounded good to beautiful Kriemhild.

Gere was her kinsman. The host told him to sit, and he ordered drink poured for the guests; there was no more delay about that. Now Siegmund too had come to where he saw the messengers, and the lord spoke to the Burgundians in a friendly way: "Welcome, you warriors, you men of Gunther. Since my son Siegfried took Kriemhild as his wife, you ought to be seen more often here in this land, if you would show us any kinship."

They said they would gladly come whenever he wished. Their great weariness was lifted from them with good cheer. The messengers were asked to sit, food was brought to them, and Siegfried had his guests given plenty of it. They had to stay there a full nine days, and in the end the swift knights complained that they could not ride home to their own land. Meanwhile King Siegfried had sent for his kinsmen.

He asked them what they advised: should they go to the Rhine? "My kinsman Gunther has sent for me, he and his kinsmen, for a festival. I would gladly go to him, except that his land lies too far away. And they ask Kriemhild to travel with me. Now advise me, dear kinsmen: how am I to get there? If I had to go to war for them through thirty lands, Siegfried's hand would gladly serve them."

Then his warriors said: "If you have a mind to make the journey to the festival, we will advise you what to do. You should ride to the Rhine with a thousand warriors; then you can stand with honor there among the Burgundians."

Then Lord Siegmund of the Netherlands said: "If you want to go to the festival, why do you not tell me so? If you do not scorn it, I will ride there with you, and I will bring a hundred warriors to make your company larger."

"If you will ride with us, dearest father," said bold Siegfried, "I shall be very glad of it. Within twelve days I will leave my lands." All who asked for them were given horses and clothes. Since the noble king had a mind for the journey, the good swift messengers were told to ride home again. To his wife's kinsmen on the Rhine he sent word that he would gladly be at their festival.

Siegfried and Kriemhild, as we hear it told, gave the messengers so much that their horses could not carry it home. He was a rich man. Their strong pack-horses were driven off in good cheer. Siegfried and Siegmund clothed their people, and Count Eckewart at once ordered ladies' clothes sought out, the best to be found, or that could be bought anywhere in all Siegfried's land. The saddles and the shields began to be made ready. The knights and ladies who were to go with them were given whatever they wanted, so that they lacked nothing. And so he brought his kinsmen many a magnificent guest.

The messengers hurried home along the roads. Then Gere the warrior came to Burgundy and was warmly received. They got down from their warhorses and their mounts in front of Giselher's hall. Young and old came, as people do, asking for news. The good knight said: "When I tell it to the king, you will hear it then and there." And he went with his companions to where he found Gunther.

For sheer joy the king sprang up from his seat, and beautiful Brunhild thanked them for coming back so quickly. Gunther said to the messenger: "How is Siegfried, who has given me so much good?"

Bold Gere said: "He turned red with joy, he and your sister. No man ever sent his kinsmen such loyal greetings as Lord Siegfried and his father have sent you."

Then the noble king's wife said to the margrave: "Now tell me, is Kriemhild coming to us? Has that beautiful woman kept any of the good manners she used to show so well?"

"She is coming to you, be sure of it," said Gere the warrior.

Ute quickly asked the messengers to come before her, and anyone could tell from her questions how glad she was to hear it: was Kriemhild still well? He told her how he had found her, and that she would be coming very soon. Nor were the gifts kept hidden at court, the gold and the clothes Lord Siegfried had given them. These were brought out for the men of the three kings to see, and he was thanked for his great generosity.

"He can easily afford to give," said Hagen then. "He could not squander it if he lived forever. The Nibelung treasure is locked in his hand. Ah, if only it ever came into the land of the Burgundians!"

The whole household rejoiced that the guests were coming. Early and late the men of the three kings were hard at work, and many seats were set up for the company. Bold Hunold and Sindold the warrior had plenty to do; all that while they had to serve as stewards and cupbearers and set up many a bench. Lord Ortwin helped them with it, and Gunther thanked them for it. Rumold the master of the kitchen, how well he ruled his underlings in the days that followed! Broad kettles, pots and pans by the dozen; what a lot of them were found there! And they made the food ready for the people who were coming into the land.

Chapter 13: How Kriemhild and Her Husband Rode to the Festival

Let us leave all their bustle now and tell how Lady Kriemhild and her maidens traveled from the Nibelung land toward the Rhine. Never did horses carry so much rich clothing. A great many traveling chests were loaded onto the wagons. Then Siegfried the warrior rode off with his kinsmen, and the queen with him, toward the place where they hoped for joy. Later it turned to great sorrow for them all.

They left Siegfried's little boy, Kriemhild's son, at home. It had to be so. Great grief would come to him from their journey to court: the child never saw his father or his mother again. Lord Siegmund rode away with them too. Had he known for certain how the festival would turn out, he would never have seen any of it. No worse grief could ever have come to him through the people he loved.

Messengers were sent ahead to carry the news. Then many of Ute's kinsmen and Gunther's vassals rode out to meet them in a splendid company, and the host began to busy himself greatly for his guests. He went to Brunhild where he found her sitting. "How did my sister receive you when you came into my land? You must receive Siegfried's wife just the same way."

"I will do that gladly, and with good reason," she said. "I am fond of her."

Then the mighty king said: "They arrive early tomorrow morning. If you mean to receive them, set to work quickly, so that we are not left waiting for them here in the castle. In all my life, guests so dear to me have never come."
