At once she told her maids and her ladies to look out good clothes, the best to be found, for her household to wear in front of the guests. They did it gladly, I can easily tell you that. Gunther's vassals hurried to serve them too, and the host gathered all his warriors around him. Then the queen rode out in great state, and the dear guests were given a mighty welcome.

With what joy they received those guests! It seemed to them that Lady Kriemhild had never received Lady Brunhild in Burgundy half so well. Those who had never seen her before found their spirits lifted by the sight. Now Siegfried had arrived too, with his vassals. The heroes were seen wheeling back and forth across the whole field in huge companies; no one could escape the crush and the dust.

When the ruler of the land saw Siegfried and Siegmund, how warmly he spoke to them! "Be heartily welcome to me and to all my kinsmen. We shall be in high spirits because of this journey of yours to our court."

"God reward you," said Siegmund, that honor-seeking man. "Ever since my son Siegfried won you as a kinsman, my heart has told me that I ought to come and see you."

King Gunther said: "It is a joy to me that you did."

Siegfried was received, as was only right, with the greatest honor; no one there bore him any ill will. Giselher and Gernot helped in this with great courtesy. I believe guests were never offered such kindness before.

Now the two kings' wives drew near each other. Many a saddle was emptied as beautiful women were lifted down onto the grass by heroes' hands; those who liked to serve the ladies had plenty to do. The lovely women went up to each other, and many a knight rejoiced that the two of them greeted each other so beautifully. Warriors in crowds were seen standing beside their young ladies. The splendid company took one another by the hand. There was a great deal of courteous bowing, and loving kisses from beautiful women: a happy sight for Gunther's and Siegfried's men.

They waited no longer but rode into the town. The host asked that his guests be shown clearly how glad everyone in Burgundy was to see them, and many a splendid jousting charge was run before the young ladies. Hagen of Tronje and Ortwin made it very clear that they were in command there: whatever they ordered, no one dared leave undone, and the dear guests were given great service by both of them. At the castle gate you could hear shields ringing from thrusts and blows; the host and his guests held back out there a long time before they went in. Truly, the hours went by for them in great sport.

Then they rode before the great hall with the ladies. Many a fine silk cloth, good and well cut, was seen hanging over the beautiful women's saddles on every side. Then Gunther's men came up, and the guests were told to be led quickly to their lodgings. Now and then Brunhild was seen glancing at Lady Kriemhild, who was beautiful enough: her color shone gloriously against the gold.

All through the town of Worms the household could be heard shouting. Gunther told Dankwart, his marshal, to take charge of them, and he began to lodge the company very comfortably. They were fed outside and inside; truly, foreign guests were never better cared for. Whatever they wanted was ready for them: the king was so rich that no one was refused anything. They were served in friendship and without a trace of ill will. Then the host sat down at table with his guests. Siegfried was asked to sit where he had sat before, and many a handsome man went with him to his seat. A good twelve hundred warriors sat at table in his circle. Brunhild the queen thought that no bondsman could be richer than this. Yet she was still so well disposed to him that she gladly let him live.

One evening, as the king sat at table, many rich clothes were soaked with wine where the cupbearers had to walk between the tables; full service was given with great care. As has long been the custom at festivals, the ladies and the young girls were given fine lodgings. Wherever they had come from, the host wished them well, and all of them were given plenty, with kindness and honor.

When the night ended and the day appeared, many a precious stone set in good clothing flashed out of the traveling chests as the ladies' hands touched them, and many a splendid garment was picked out. Before it was fully light, many knights and squires came out before the hall, and the noise rose again, before the early Mass that was sung for the king. The young heroes rode so well that the king thanked them for it. Many a trumpet blared with tremendous force, and from the drums and flutes the noise grew so great that the whole wide town of Worms rang with it. The high-spirited heroes were on horseback everywhere.

Then a very high game began in that land, with many a good warrior; you could see plenty of them there, their young hearts full of high spirits, and many a fine good knight under his shield. In the windows sat the glorious ladies and many beautiful girls, all richly adorned; they watched the sport of many a bold man. The host himself began to ride with his kinsmen.

So they passed the time, and it did not seem long. Then the ringing of many bells was heard from the cathedral. The horses came, and the ladies rode off, and many a bold man followed the noble queens. They dismounted onto the grass in front of the cathedral. Brunhild was still well disposed to her guests. They went into the wide cathedral wearing their crowns. Later that affection was broken, and great hatred brought it about.

When they had heard Mass, they rode back again with many honors, and afterward they were seen going happily to table. Their joy never flagged at that festival until the eleventh day.

Chapter 14: How the Queens Quarreled

One day, before vespers, a great commotion broke out in the courtyard, where many warriors were riding at knightly games to pass the time, and crowds of women and men ran out to watch. The two mighty queens were sitting together, and their thoughts were on two warriors who were well worth praising. Then beautiful Kriemhild said: "I have a husband who ought by rights to hold all these kingdoms in his hands."

Lady Brunhild said: "How could that be? If no one were alive but him and you, then the kingdoms might well be subject to him. But as long as Gunther lives, it can never happen."

Kriemhild answered: "Just look at him standing there. Look how royally he walks ahead of the warriors, like the bright moon in front of the stars. I have every reason to be glad."

Lady Brunhild said: "However handsome your husband is, however noble and however fine, you must still put Gunther ahead of him, the warrior, your noble brother. Know this for certain: he must rank above all kings."

Then Lady Kriemhild said: "My husband is so worthy that I have not praised him without cause. His honor is great in a great many things. Believe me, Brunhild, he is easily Gunther's equal."

"You must not take it badly of me, Kriemhild, for I have not said this without cause. I heard them both declare it, when I first saw them, when the king got the better of me and won my love in such knightly fashion: there Siegfried himself said that he was the king's vassal. And so I have counted him my bondsman ever since I heard him say it."

Beautiful Kriemhild said: "Then I would have been badly served. How could my noble brothers have arranged things so that I should be a bondsman's wife? So I ask you, Brunhild, as a friend: for my sake, be kind and let this talk drop."

"I cannot let it drop," said the king's wife. "Why should I give up so many knights who, along with that warrior, owe us their service?"

Beautiful Kriemhild grew very angry. "You will have to give him up, and any idea that he will ever do you a day's service. He is worthier than my brother Gunther, noble man that he is. You must spare me any more of what I have just heard from you. And it always surprises me, since he is your bondsman and you have such power over the two of us, that he has been allowed to leave his tribute unpaid for so long. By rights I should be spared your arrogance."

"You set yourself too high," said the king's wife. "Now I will gladly see whether people hold you in as much honor as they hold me."

The two ladies were both in a fury. Lady Kriemhild said: "Then this is what must happen. Since you have declared my husband your bondsman, the men of both kings shall see today whether I dare walk into the cathedral ahead of the king's wife. You will see today that I am noble and freeborn, and that my husband is worthier than yours. I will not be insulted with that. And you shall see tonight how your bondswoman walks to court ahead of the warriors of Burgundy. I mean to be held worthier myself than anyone ever knew a queen to be who ever wore a crown." So a great deal of hatred rose up between the two ladies.

Then Brunhild said: "If you will not be a bondswoman, then you and your ladies must keep apart from my household when we go to the cathedral." And Kriemhild answered: "Truly, that will be done."

"Dress yourselves, my maids," said Siegfried's wife. "I must come out of this without shame. Let it be seen whether you have rich clothes. Brunhild may yet be glad to take back what she has said." They did not need much urging; they went looking for rich clothes, and many a lady and maiden was handsomely turned out. Then the noble king's wife went out with her household, and beautiful Kriemhild's women were handsomely turned out too. She had brought forty-three maidens with her to the Rhine, and they wore bright silks woven in Arabia. So the lovely maidens came to the cathedral, and all Siegfried's men were waiting for them in front of the house.

The people wondered what had happened, that the queens were seen so separated, no longer walking together as they used to. Later many a warrior suffered bitterly for it. Here, in front of the cathedral, stood Gunther's wife, and many a knight was passing the time pleasantly with the lovely ladies he was watching. Then Lady Kriemhild came with a large and splendid company. Whatever clothes the children of noble knights had ever worn, beside her household they were so much wind. She was so rich in goods that the wives of thirty kings together could not have produced what Kriemhild did. Wish as you might, you could not say that such rich clothes had ever been seen before as her lovely maidens wore at that hour. Kriemhild would have let it be, except to hurt Brunhild.

They met in front of the wide cathedral. And the mistress of the house, out of sheer hatred, told Kriemhild rudely to stand still: "A bondswoman shall never walk in ahead of a king's wife."

Beautiful Kriemhild answered, and her heart was full of anger: "You should have kept your mouth shut; that would have been better for you. You have shamed your own lovely body yourself. How could a vassal's concubine ever become a king's wife?"

"Whom are you calling a concubine here?" said the king's wife.

"I am calling you one," Kriemhild said. "It was Siegfried, my dear husband, who was the first to have your lovely body. It was not my brother who took your virginity. Where were your wits? It was a wicked trick. Why did you let him love you, if he is your bondsman? I hear you complaining," said Kriemhild, "without any cause at all."

"Truly," said Brunhild, "I will tell Gunther this."

"What is that to me? Your arrogance has betrayed you. With your own words you have claimed me for your service. Know this, on my true loyalty: it will grieve me as long as I live, and I will never again be a loyal friend to your secrets."

Brunhild wept. Kriemhild waited no longer: she walked into the cathedral with her household ahead of the king's wife. That was the start of a great hatred, and bright eyes were dimmed and wet because of it.

However much was sung and done there in God's service, the time seemed far too long to Brunhild, for her body and her spirit were heavy. Many a bold, good hero would pay for that later. Brunhild went out with her ladies and stood in front of the cathedral. She thought: "Kriemhild must tell me more about what that sharp-tongued woman is accusing me of so loudly. If he has boasted of it, it will cost Siegfried his life."

Now noble Kriemhild came out with many a bold man, and Lady Brunhild said: "Stand still a moment. You said I was a concubine; you must let me see your proof. Your words have hurt me, I want you to know that."

Lady Kriemhild said: "You had better let me pass. I prove it with the gold I wear on my hand: my lover brought it to me when he first lay with me." Brunhild never lived through a worse day. She said: "This fine gold was stolen from me, and it has been wickedly hidden from me a very long time. I will get to the bottom of who took it from me." Both the ladies were now in a great passion.

Kriemhild said again: "I will not be made out a thief. You should have thought of that, if your honor was dear to you. I prove with the belt I wear here around my waist that I am not lying: truly, my Siegfried was your man." The belt she wore was silk from Nineveh, set with precious stones, and truly it was fine enough. When Lady Brunhild saw it, she began to weep. Now Gunther and all the men of Burgundy would have to hear of this.

The queen said: "Send here for the prince of the Rhine. I want him to hear how his sister has shamed me. She says openly here that I am Siegfried's woman."

The king came with his warriors, and he saw his dear wife weeping. How gently he spoke: "Tell me, dear lady, who has done anything to you?" She said to the king: "I stand here in misery. Your sister would gladly strip me of all my honor. I make my complaint to you: she says her husband Siegfried has had me as his concubine."

King Gunther said: "Then she has done wrong."

"She is wearing my belt here, which I lost, and my red gold. I bitterly regret that I was ever born unless you clear me, King, of this great shame. If you do, I will serve you for it forever."

King Gunther said: "He shall come forward. If he has boasted of it, he must let us hear it, or else he must deny it, the hero from the Netherlands." And Kriemhild's beloved was sent for at once.

When Lord Siegfried saw the two distressed women, he had no idea of the story, and how quickly he spoke: "Why are these ladies weeping? I would like to know that, and what reason the king has for sending for me."

King Gunther said: "It grieves me badly. My lady Brunhild has told me a story here: that you boasted you were the first to have her lovely body. Your wife Kriemhild says so."

Strong Siegfried said: "If she has said that, she will be sorry for it before I am done. And I will clear myself before all your men with solemn oaths that I never said it."

The king of the Rhine said: "Then let it be seen. The oath you offer, let it be sworn here and now, and I will hold you clear of every false charge." The proud Burgundians were told to stand in a ring. Bold Siegfried held out his hand for the oath. Then the mighty king said: "Your great innocence is so well known to me that I release you. What my sister accuses you of, you never did."

Siegfried said: "If my wife gets any good out of having made Brunhild unhappy, then truly I am sorry for it beyond all measure." And the good, handsome knights looked at one another.

"Women should be brought up," said Siegfried the warrior, "to leave idle talk alone. Forbid it to your wife, and I will do the same to mine. Truly, I am ashamed of her gross bad manners."

Many fair women were parted by those words. And Brunhild grieved so bitterly that it moved Gunther's men to pity. Then Hagen of Tronje came to his lady. He found her weeping and asked what was wrong. She told him the story, and on the spot he promised her that Kriemhild's husband would pay for it, or he himself would never be happy again.

Ortwin and Gernot joined the talk, as the heroes were planning Siegfried's death. Giselher came too, noble Ute's son, and when he heard what they were saying, he spoke like a loyal man: "You good warriors, why are you doing this? Siegfried never earned such hatred that he should lose his life for it. Truly, women get angry over very little."

"Are we to raise cuckolds?" Hagen said. "Good warriors like us would have little honor from that. Because he has boasted of my dear lady, I will die for it myself unless it costs him his life."

Then the king himself said: "He has done us nothing but good and brought us nothing but honor. Let him live. What would it gain me to turn against the warrior now? He was always loyal to us, and he was loyal gladly."

Ortwin of Metz, the warrior, said: "His great strength will not help him. If my lord allows it, I will do him harm." So the heroes had declared themselves his enemies without any cause. No one took it further, except that Hagen kept telling Gunther, every chance he got, that if Siegfried were no longer alive, a great many kings' lands would be subject to him. The hero began to brood on it.

They let it rest. The games went on, and oh, what stout shafts were splintered in front of the cathedral, before Siegfried's wife, all the way to the hall! But all Gunther's men were in a black mood. The king said: "Let this murderous anger go. He was born to be our good fortune and our honor. Besides, the man is so wonderfully bold and so terribly strong that if he ever found out, no one would dare face him."

"No," said Hagen. "You can keep quiet about it. I trust myself to arrange it so secretly that he will be sorry for Brunhild's tears. Truly, Hagen is his enemy from this day on."

King Gunther said: "How can that be done?" And Hagen answered: "I will tell you. We have messengers ride into this land to us, men no one here knows, and declare war on us openly. Then you announce in front of the guests that you and your men are going to war. Once that is done, he will promise to serve you in it, and that is how he will lose his life: I will get the secret out of the bold warrior's wife."

The king followed Hagen, his vassal, and it was a bad thing he did. The chosen knights began to plot a great treachery before anyone could find it out. Because two women quarreled, many a hero lost his life.

Chapter 15: How Siegfried Was Betrayed

On the fourth morning thirty-two men were seen riding to court, and mighty Gunther was told that war had been declared on him. Out of that lie grew the greatest sorrow Lady Kriemhild ever knew. The men were given leave to come before the king, and they claimed to be Liudeger's men, the king whom Siegfried's hand had beaten before and brought as a hostage into Gunther's land. The king greeted the messengers and told them to sit down. One of them spoke up: "My lord, let us stand until we have delivered the message we were sent to bring you. Believe me, you have many a mother's son for an enemy. Liudegast and Liudeger, whom you once hurt so badly, declare war on you. They mean to ride into this land with an army." The king began to rage when he heard the news.

The false messengers were sent off to their lodgings. How could Lord Siegfried have guarded himself, he or anyone else, against what they were plotting? In the end that plot brought great sorrow on the plotters themselves. The king went about whispering with his kinsmen, and Hagen of Tronje never let him rest. Plenty of the king's men would still have put a stop to it, but Hagen would not be turned from his plan for a single day.

One day Siegfried found them whispering, and the hero from the Netherlands began to ask: "Why are the king and his men going about so sadly? If anyone has done him any wrong, I will always help him avenge it."

Lord Gunther said: "I have good reason to be sad. Liudegast and Liudeger have declared war on me. They mean to ride openly into my land."

"Then Siegfried's hand will stop that," said the bold warrior, "eagerly, and in a way that does full justice to your honor. I will deal with those warriors as I dealt with them before: I will lay their castles and their lands in ruins before I turn back, and my head is your pledge of it. You and your warriors are to stay at home, and let me ride against them with the men I have. I will let you see how gladly I serve you. Believe me, your enemies are going to suffer at my hands."

"How glad I am to hear it," said the king, as if he were honestly delighted at the help; and the treacherous man bowed low to him, with falseness in his heart. Lord Siegfried said: "You need not worry at all."

So they prepared the campaign with the squires, all of it staged for Siegfried and his men to see. He had his men from the Netherlands get ready too, and Siegfried's warriors looked out their fighting gear. Then strong Siegfried said: "My father Siegmund, you are to stay here. If God gives us luck, we will be back on the Rhine in a short while. You are to stay here with the king and enjoy yourself."

They fastened on their banners as if they meant to march, and plenty of Gunther's men there had no idea what it was really about. A great company could be seen gathered around Siegfried. They tied their helmets and their coats of mail onto the horses, and many a strong knight made ready to leave the land. Then Hagen of Tronje went to find Kriemhild and asked her leave to go, since they were about to leave the country.

"How glad I am," said Kriemhild, "that I ever won a husband who dares to stand up so well for my dear kinsmen, as my lord Siegfried is doing for my family. That puts me in high spirits," said the queen. "Dear friend Hagen, remember this: that I serve you gladly and never once bore you any hatred. Let my dear husband have the benefit of that. He must not pay for it if I did anything to Brunhild. I have been sorry for it since," said the noble lady. "And he has beaten me black and blue for it, because I ever said the things that troubled her heart. That bold and good hero has avenged it thoroughly."

He said: "You and she will be reconciled soon enough, in a few days' time. Kriemhild, my dear lady, tell me how I can serve you in the matter of Siegfried, your husband. I will gladly do it, my lady, for there is no one I would rather do it for."

"I would be free of all worry," said the noble lady, "that anyone might take his life in battle, if only he would not follow his own arrogance. Then that bold and good warrior would be safe."

"My lady," Hagen said, "if you have any fear that he can be wounded, you must let me know what kind of cunning I need to prevent it. I will always ride and walk at his side to guard him."

She said: "You are my kinsman, and I am yours. I entrust my dear love to your loyalty, so that you will guard my beloved husband well." And she told him things that had better been left unsaid.

She said: "My husband is bold, and strong enough too. When he killed the dragon at the mountain, the gallant warrior bathed in its blood, and since then no weapon has ever cut him in battle. And yet I am afraid whenever he stands in the fighting and so many spears fly from heroes' hands, afraid that I will lose my dear husband there. Oh, the worry I so often suffer over Siegfried! I am telling you this, dearest friend, trusting in your good faith, so that you will keep your loyalty to me: I am going to let you hear where my dear husband can be cut down. I do it in trust. When the hot blood flowed from the dragon's wounds and the bold good knight bathed in it, a broad linden leaf fell between his shoulder blades. There he can be wounded, and that is why I worry so much."

Hagen of Tronje said: "Sew a small mark on his clothing, so I know by it where I must guard him when we stand in battle." She thought she was saving the hero's life; it was done to bring about his death. She said: "With fine silk I will sew a secret cross on his clothing. There, hero, your hand must guard my husband when it comes to the thick of it, when he stands in the fighting before his enemies."

"I will do that, my dear lady," said Hagen. And the lady thought it would be to his good, but with that Kriemhild's husband was betrayed. Hagen took his leave, and went away cheerfully.

The king's whole household was in good spirits. I do not believe any warrior will ever again commit such a monstrous betrayal as Hagen did then, when Queen Kriemhild put her trust in his loyalty.

The next morning Lord Siegfried rode out cheerfully with a thousand of his men. He thought he was going to avenge the wrong done to his kinsmen. Hagen rode so close beside him that he could study his clothes. As soon as he had seen the mark, he secretly sent off two of his men, who brought back a different message: that Gunther's land was to remain at peace, and that Liudeger had sent them to the king. How unwillingly Siegfried turned back now, before he had avenged any of his kinsmen's wrong! Gunther's men could hardly turn him round. Then he rode to the king, and his host began to thank him.

"God reward you for your good will, friend Siegfried, for doing so willingly what I ask of you. I will always serve you for it, as I rightly should. I trust you more than any of my kinsmen. Now that we are rid of this campaign, I mean to ride out hunting bear and boar in the Vosges forest, as I have done so often." That was Hagen's counsel, that treacherous man. "Tell all my guests that we ride out early tomorrow. Those who want to hunt with me should get ready; and those who would rather stay here and pass the time with the ladies will please me just as well."

Lord Siegfried said, in his lordly way: "Whenever you ride out hunting, I will gladly come along. You must lend me a tracker and a few hounds, and I will ride into the forest."

"Will you take only one?" the king answered at once. "If you like, I will lend you four who know the woods well, and the trails the game follows, so that they will not let you lose your way back to camp."

Then the gallant warrior rode off to his wife, and in no time Hagen had told the king: "We shall have that excellent warrior yet." No man should ever practice treachery like that.

Chapter 16: How Siegfried Was Murdered

Gunther and Hagen, those bold warriors, treacherously proclaimed a hunt in the forest. With their sharp spears they meant to hunt boars and bears and bison. What could be braver? Siegfried rode with them in lordly style, and food of every kind was carried along. At a clear spring he would later lose his life, and it was Brunhild, King Gunther's wife, who had counseled it.

The bold warrior went to find Kriemhild. His fine hunting clothes and his companions' were already loaded on the pack-horses; they were going to cross the Rhine. Kriemhild could never have been sadder. He kissed his beloved on the mouth. "God grant that I see you again in health, lady, and that your eyes see me. Pass the time with your kinsmen who love you; I cannot stay at home."

Then she thought of what she had told Hagen—she dared not say a word of it—and the noble queen began to lament that she had ever been born. Lord Siegfried's wife wept without measure. She said to the warrior: "Let your hunting be. Last night I dreamed that two wild boars chased you across the heath, and the flowers turned red. I have good reason to weep like this, believe me. I am terribly afraid of some plot, that we may have wronged someone who could turn hatred against us. Stay here, dear lord. I advise it in true loyalty."

"My beloved," he said, "I will be back in a few days. I know of no one here who bears me any hatred. All your kinsmen are my friends alike, and I have deserved nothing else from the warriors here."

"No, Lord Siegfried, truly I fear your fall. Last night I had a terrible dream: two mountains fell down on top of you, and I never saw you again. If you leave me, it hurts me to the heart."

He took the good woman in his arms and caressed her lovely body with tender kisses, and in a little while he took his leave and went. Sadly, she never saw him in health again.

Then they rode away into a deep forest for the sport of it. Many a bold knight followed Gunther and his men, but Gernot and Giselher had stayed at home. Many loaded horses had crossed the Rhine ahead of them, carrying for the hunting party bread and wine, meat and fish, and plenty of other provisions, such as a king so rich has every right to have. The proud, bold hunters had camp made at the edge of the green forest, where the game would break cover, on a wide stretch of flat land by the river where they meant to hunt. Siegfried had arrived there too, and the king was told of it.

The hunting party now manned the watch-posts on every side. Then the bold man, Siegfried the strong, said: "Who is going to guide us into the forest after the game, you bold and daring heroes?"

"Shall we split up," said Hagen, "before we begin the hunt? That way my lords and I can tell who the best hunters on this forest trip are. We will divide the men and the hounds between us, and then let each man go wherever he likes. Whoever hunts best shall have our thanks." After that the hunters did not stay together long.

Lord Siegfried said: "I have no need of hounds, except for one tracker so well trained that it can follow an animal's trail through the pines. We will find game enough," said Kriemhild's husband.

Then an old huntsman took a good tracking hound, and in a short while he had brought the lord to where they found animals in plenty. Whatever rose from its lair the companions hunted down, as good hunters still do. Whatever the hound started, bold Siegfried, the hero from the Netherlands, killed with his own hand. His horse ran so fast that nothing got away from him, and he won the praise of them all at the hunt.

He was more than capable in everything. The first animal he killed with his hand was a strong young boar, and soon after that he found a monstrous lion. When the hound started it, he shot it with his bow; he had fitted a strong arrow to the string, and after the shot the lion ran only three bounds. His hunting companions thanked Siegfried. After that he quickly killed a bison and an elk, four strong aurochs and a savage schelch, a beast of the old forests. His horse carried him so fast that nothing escaped him; hart or hind could hardly get away from him.

The tracking hound found a great boar. When it tried to run, the master of that hunt was there at once and stood in its path. The boar charged the hero in a rage, and Kriemhild's husband killed it with his sword. Another hunter would not have managed that so easily. When he had felled it, they leashed the tracking hound, and the Burgundians soon knew all about his rich hunting.

Then his hunters said: "If it can be done without offense, Lord Siegfried, leave a few of the animals alive for us. You are emptying the mountain and the forest for us today." At that the bold, daring warrior began to smile.

Now on every side they heard clamor and din. The noise of men and hounds was so great that the mountain and the pine woods echoed it back. The hunters had loosed twenty-four packs, and many an animal had to lose its life. Each of them thought he would be given the prize of the hunt, but that could not happen, once strong Siegfried was seen standing by the fire. The hunt was over, and yet not entirely. Those who were going to the fire brought with them hides of many animals and game in plenty. What a load of it the king's household carried to the kitchen!

Then the king had word sent to the chosen hunters that he wanted to eat. A horn was blown once, very loud, and by that they knew the noble prince was to be found at the camp. One of Siegfried's huntsmen said: "My lord, I hear from a horn's blast that we are to come to the camp now. I will answer it." And with much blowing of horns the companions were called in.

Lord Siegfried said: "Now let us leave the pine woods too." His horse carried him at an easy pace, and they hurried along with him. With their noise they started a fearsome animal: it was a wild bear. The warrior said over his shoulder: "I am going to give us companions some good entertainment. Let the hound loose; I see a bear. It is coming with us to the camp. Unless it runs very fast, it will never get away."

The hound was loosed and the bear bounded off. Kriemhild's husband wanted to ride it down, but it got into a thicket where that could not be done, and the strong animal thought itself safe from the hunter. Then the proud, good knight sprang from his horse and ran after it on foot. The animal was off its guard; it could not escape him, and he caught it at once. Without giving it a single wound the hero quickly bound it, so that it could neither scratch nor bite anyone. He tied it to his saddle and the swift man mounted. In his high spirits the warrior, bold and good, brought it to the fire as a bit of fun.

How splendidly he rode to the camp! His spear was huge, strong and broad. A handsome sword hung down to his spurs, and the lord carried a beautiful horn of red gold. I never heard tell of better hunting clothes. He was seen wearing a coat of black silk and a hat of sable, rich enough, and what costly bands he had on his quiver! A panther's skin was drawn over it, for the sweet smell. He also carried a bow that anyone else who meant to draw it would have had to draw with a winch; he did it himself. His whole outfit was of otter skin from head to foot, and scattered over the bright fur were strips of gold, shining on both sides of the bold master of the hunt. He carried Balmung, a fine broad weapon, so sharp that it never failed wherever it was struck on helmets; its edges were good. The splendid hunter was in high spirits. Since I am to tell you the whole story: his noble quiver was full of good arrows with golden sockets, their blades a good hand's breadth across. Whatever he cut with those had to die very soon.

So the noble knight rode off in his hunter's gear. Gunther's men saw him coming; they ran to meet him and took his horse, and there beside the saddle he led a big, strong bear. When he had dismounted he loosed the bonds from its feet and its muzzle. At once the whole pack set up a howl, every hound that saw the bear. The animal wanted to get to the forest, and the people were in trouble. Frightened by the noise, the bear blundered into the kitchen. How many kitchen boys it scattered from the fire! Many a kettle was knocked over and many a burning brand scattered, and what good food was found lying in the ashes!

The lords and their men sprang up from their seats. The bear grew angry, and the king ordered the whole pack loosed from the leashes where it lay. If it had all ended well, they would have had a merry day. With bows and spears, and no more delay, the quick men ran after the bear. There were so many hounds about it that no one could shoot, and the whole mountain rang with the people's shouting. The bear began to run from the hounds, and no one could keep up with it except Kriemhild's husband. He ran it down with his sword and struck it dead, and then the bear was carried back to the fire. Those who saw it said he was a powerful man.

The proud hunting party was called to the tables, and a good many of them sat down on a beautiful meadow. What rich food was carried to those noble hunters! But the cupbearers who should have brought the wine were slow in coming. Otherwise heroes could never have been better served. If there had not been such false hearts among them, the warriors would have been safe from every disgrace.

Lord Siegfried said: "It amazes me, when they send us so much from the kitchen, that the cupbearers do not bring us the wine. If hunters are not looked after better than this, I will not be a hunting companion. I have earned better treatment than this."

The king spoke from his table, falsely: "We will gladly make good whatever is lacking. It is Hagen's fault; he wants to let us die of thirst."

Hagen of Tronje said: "My dear lord, I thought today's hunt was to be in the Spessart forest, and I sent the wine there. If we go thirsty today, I will take good care it never happens again."

Lord Siegfried said: "Bad luck to you for it. Seven pack-loads of mead and clear wine should have been brought here for me, and if that could not be done, we should have been seated nearer the Rhine."

Hagen of Tronje said: "You noble, bold knights, I know a cold spring near here. Do not be angry; let us go there." That advice would bring many a warrior great sorrow. Thirst was tormenting Siegfried the warrior, and he ordered the table cleared away all the sooner; he wanted to go past the hills to the spring. The advice had been given by the warriors with evil in it. The animals Siegfried's hand had killed were ordered loaded on wagons and sent home, and everyone who saw them gave him great honor. Hagen broke his loyalty to Siegfried, and broke it badly.

When they were about to set off for the broad linden tree, Hagen of Tronje said: "I have often been told that no one can keep up with Kriemhild's husband when he wants to run. If only he would show us!"

Bold Siegfried of the Netherlands said: "You can easily test that, if you will race me to the spring. When it is done, the prize goes to whoever is seen to win."

"Then let us try it," said Hagen the warrior.

Swift Siegfried said: "Then I will lie down in the grass at your feet." When Gunther heard that, how glad it made him! The bold warrior went on: "I will tell you more. I will carry all my gear with me, my spear and my shield and all my hunting clothes." He quickly strapped on his quiver and his sword.

Then the other two pulled their clothes off, and the two of them were seen standing in their white shirts. Like two wild panthers they ran through the clover, but it was bold Siegfried who was seen at the spring first. He carried off the prize in everything, ahead of many a man. He quickly unbuckled his sword and laid the quiver aside, and he leaned his strong spear against a branch of the linden. The splendid guest stood beside the running spring. Siegfried's courtesy was very great. He laid his shield down where the water flowed, and however badly he thirsted, the hero did not drink until the king had drunk. He got poor thanks for it.

The spring was cool, clear and good. Gunther bent down to the water, and when he had drunk he straightened up again. Bold Siegfried would gladly have done the same, and now he paid for his good manners. Hagen carried his bow and his sword away out of his reach, then sprang back to where he had left the spear, and looked for the mark on the bold man's clothing. As Lord Siegfried bent over the spring and drank, Hagen shot him through the cross, so that the blood from his heart spurted out of the wound and splashed hard on Hagen's clothes. No hero will ever commit so great a crime again. He left the spear sticking in him near the heart. Never in the world did Hagen run so desperately from any man as he fled then.

When Lord Siegfried felt the great wound, the lord sprang up from the spring in a fury, with a long spear-shaft standing out from his heart. The prince thought to find his bow or his sword, and if he had, Hagen would have been paid what his service deserved. When the sorely wounded man found no sword, he had nothing left but the rim of his shield. He snatched it up from the spring and ran at Hagen, and King Gunther's man could not get away from him. Wounded to death as he was, he struck so powerfully that a shower of precious stones flew from the shield and the shield itself burst to pieces. The splendid guest would gladly have had his revenge. Hagen was knocked to the ground by his hand, and the flat by the river rang loudly with the force of the blow. If he had had the sword in his hand, it would have been Hagen's death, so terribly did the wounded man rage. He had good reason to.

His color had drained away; he could not stand. All the strength of his body was leaving him, for he carried death's mark in his pale face. Later many beautiful women wept for him. Then Kriemhild's husband fell among the flowers. The blood was seen running hard from his wound. In his great extremity he began to curse those who had plotted the treacherous death against him.

The mortally wounded man said: "You wretched cowards. What good are my services, now that you have killed me? I was always loyal to you, and this is what it has cost me. You have done a wicked thing to your own kinsmen. Everyone born of your blood from this day on is disgraced by it. You have avenged your anger far too hard on my body. In shame you shall be set apart from all good warriors."

The knights all ran to where he lay slain. For many of them it was a joyless day, and those who had any loyalty in them mourned him. The bold and gallant knight had well deserved it. The king of Burgundy lamented his death. The dying man said: "There is no need for the man who did the harm to weep over it. He earns great blame for it. It would have been better left undone."

Grim Hagen said: "I do not know what he is lamenting. All our fear and all our sorrow are over now. We will find very few who dare to stand against us. Good for me, that I have put an end to his rule."

"You may easily boast," said Siegfried. "If I had recognized your murderous nature, I would have kept my life safe from you. Nothing grieves me so much as Lady Kriemhild, my wife. Now God have mercy that I ever had a son, who will be reproached in time to come because his own kinsmen murdered a man. If I could," said Siegfried, "I would have every right to lament that."

Then the mortally wounded man said pitifully: "Noble king, if you ever mean to keep loyalty to anyone in this world, let my dear beloved be commended to your grace. Let it count for her that she is your sister. By the virtue of all princes, stand by her loyally. My father and my men will have a long wait for me. Never was a woman so wronged in a beloved husband."

The flowers all around were wet with blood. Then he struggled with death, but not for long, for death's weapon always cuts too deep. Then the bold and gallant warrior could speak no more.

When the lords saw that the hero was dead, they laid him on a shield of red gold and took counsel how to arrange it so that no one would know Hagen had done it. Many of them said: "This is an evil thing that has happened to us. You must all hide it and all tell the same story: that Kriemhild's husband rode out hunting alone, and robbers killed him as he went through the pine woods."

Hagen of Tronje said: "I will bring him home myself. I do not care in the least if she finds out, the woman who has so troubled Brunhild's heart. It matters very little to me how much she weeps."

Chapter 17: How Kriemhild Mourned Her Husband, and How He Was Buried

They waited for nightfall and then crossed the Rhine. No heroes ever hunted worse. The one beast they killed was wept for by noble girls, and truly, many good fighting men would pay for it later. Now you can hear about great arrogance and terrible revenge. Hagen had Siegfried of the Nibelung land carried, dead as he was, to the door of the chamber where Kriemhild lodged. He had him laid there secretly, against the door, so that she would find him when she went out to matins before daybreak—the early service that Lady Kriemhild hardly ever slept through.

The bells rang from the cathedral as they always did. Lady Kriemhild the beautiful woke her many maids and asked for a light to be brought, and her clothes. Then a chamberlain came along and found Siegfried. He saw him red with blood, his clothes soaked through. That this was his lord he had no idea. He carried the light on into the chamber, and that was how Lady Kriemhild learned the terrible news. As she was about to set out with her ladies for the cathedral, the chamberlain said: "Stop where you are. A knight is lying outside this room, struck dead." At that Kriemhild began to wail beyond all measure.

Before she had even made sure that it was her husband, she began to think of Hagen's question—how he could protect him. Then her sorrow truly began. With his death she gave up every joy she had. She sank to the floor without a word, and there the beautiful woman lay, all her happiness gone. Kriemhild's anguish grew past measuring. When she came out of her faint, she screamed until the whole chamber rang with it.

Her household said: "What if it is some stranger?" The blood burst from her mouth, from the pain in her heart, and she said: "It is Siegfried, my own dear husband. Brunhild planned this, and Hagen did it."

The lady had herself led to where the hero lay. She lifted his handsome head with her white hand, and red with blood as it was, she knew him at once. There lay the hero of the Nibelung land, a piteous sight. Then the gracious lady cried out in her grief: "Oh, my sorrow! Your shield is not hacked by any sword. You lie here murdered. If I knew who did this, I would plot his death for the rest of my life."

All her household wailed and cried out with their dear lady, for they were sick at heart for the noble lord they had lost. Hagen had avenged Brunhild's anger, and avenged it hard.

Then the grieving woman said: "Chamberlain, go now, and quickly wake Siegfried's men. And tell Siegmund of my anguish, and ask whether he will help me mourn bold Siegfried."

A messenger ran fast to where Siegfried's heroes from the Nibelung land lay sleeping, and with his terrible news he took their joy away. They would not believe it until they heard the weeping. The messenger soon came to where the king lay too. Lord Siegmund was not asleep; I think his heart had told him what had happened to him. He would never see his dear son alive again.

"Wake up, Lord Siegmund. My lady Kriemhild sent me for you. A wrong has been done to her that cuts her to the heart more than any sorrow before, and you must help her mourn it, for it touches you deeply too."

Siegmund sat up. He said: "What is this sorrow of beautiful Kriemhild's that you are telling me about?"

The messenger said, weeping: "I cannot keep it from you. Bold Siegfried of the Netherlands has been killed."

Lord Siegmund said: "Stop this joking, for my sake, and stop telling anyone such evil news, that he has been killed. I could never finish mourning him as long as I lived."

"If you will not believe what you hear me say, then you can go and hear for yourself how Kriemhild and all her household are mourning Siegfried's death." Then Siegmund was badly frightened, and truly he had reason.

He and a hundred of his men sprang from their beds and snatched up their long sharp weapons, and they ran toward the wailing, sick with grief. Then came a thousand warriors, bold Siegfried's men. When they heard the women lamenting so pitifully, some of them remembered they ought to be wearing clothes. Truly, their grief had robbed them of their wits. A great heaviness had buried itself in their hearts.

Then bold Siegmund came to where Kriemhild was. He said: "Alas for the journey to this land! Who has robbed me of my child, and you of your husband, so murderously, here among such good friends?"

"Oh, if I knew him," said the noble woman, "neither my heart nor my body would ever wish him well again. I would plan such harm for him that his kinsmen would be weeping because of me."

Lord Siegmund wrapped his arms around the prince. Then the anguish of his kinsmen grew so great that the palace and the hall, and the whole town of Worms, rang with their crying and weeping. No one could comfort Siegfried's wife. They stripped the clothes from his handsome body, washed his wounds, and laid him on the bier. His people ached with the greatness of their grief.

Then his warriors from the Nibelung land said: "Our hands will avenge him, and gladly. The man who did this is here in this castle." And all Siegfried's men rushed for their weapons. The chosen fighters came with their shields, eleven hundred warriors; Lord Siegmund had that many in his company. He wanted to avenge his son's death, and truly he had reason. They did not know whom they should fight, unless it was Gunther and his men, with whom Lord Siegfried had ridden to the hunt.

Kriemhild saw them armed, and it grieved her terribly. However great her anguish and however deep her distress, she was so afraid the Nibelungs would die at the hands of her brothers' men that she stopped it. She warned them kindly, as loving kinsmen warn their kin. The grief-stricken woman said: "My lord Siegmund, what are you starting? You do not understand how things stand here. Truly, King Gunther has so many bold men that you will all be lost if you take those warriors on."

With their shields raised they were eager to fight. The noble queen begged and commanded the brave warriors to leave it alone, and when they would not give it up, truly it grieved her. She said: "Lord Siegmund, you must let it rest until a better time comes. Then I will avenge my husband with you, always. Once I have proof of who took him from me, I will make him pay. There are too many arrogant men here on the Rhine, so I will not advise you to fight. They have a good thirty men for every one of yours. Now may God give them what they have earned from us. Stay here and bear my sorrow with me, and when day begins to break, you brave heroes, help me lay my dear husband in his coffin." The fighters said: "That shall be done."

No one could tell you the whole wonder of it, how knights and ladies were heard lamenting, until the wailing was noticed in the town itself. The noble townspeople came hurrying. They mourned with the guests, for it grieved them deeply that no one had told them of any guilt of Siegfried's, any reason why the noble warrior should have lost his life. The wives of the good townsmen wept with the ladies of the court. Smiths were ordered to hurry and make a coffin of silver and gold, big and strong, and to band it tight with good steel. Every heart was heavy.

The night was gone; they said day was dawning. Then the noble lady had Lord Siegfried, her dear husband, carried to the cathedral. Whatever friends he had there were seen weeping as they went. When they brought him into the cathedral, many bells rang, and on every side you could hear the chanting of many priests. Then King Gunther came with his men, and grim Hagen too, toward the sound of wailing.

He said: "Dearest sister, alas for your sorrow, and that we cannot be spared this great loss. We will mourn Siegfried's death forever."

"You do that without cause," said the grieving woman. "If it grieved you, it would never have happened. You forgot me, I can say that now, when I was parted from my dear husband. Would to God," said Kriemhild, "that it had been done to me instead."

They denied it firmly. Then Kriemhild said: "Whoever claims he is innocent, let him prove it. He must walk up to the bier in front of everyone. That way the truth will be known quickly enough."

It is a great wonder, and it still happens often today: whenever a murderer, stained with the killing, is seen beside the dead man, the wounds begin to bleed. And that is what happened here, and so the guilt was seen to be Hagen's. The wounds flowed freely, as they had before, and those who had been weeping already now wept far more.

Then King Gunther said: "I want you to know that robbers killed him. Hagen did not do it."

"I know those robbers well," she said. "Now may God let his kinsmen's hands avenge it. Gunther and Hagen, you did this." Siegfried's fighters were ready to go to war. Then Kriemhild said once more: "Bear this distress with me."

Now these two came to where they found him dead: Gernot, her brother, and young Giselher. They mourned him loyally, along with the others, and wept from their hearts for Kriemhild's husband. Mass was to be sung, and so from every side men, women, and children went to the cathedral. Even those who could easily have spared him wept for Siegfried then.

Gernot and Giselher said: "Sister, comfort yourself after this death, since it must be so. As long as we live we will try to make it up to you." But no one in the world could give her any comfort.

His coffin was ready around midday. They lifted him from the bier where he lay, but still the lady would not let him be buried, and that cost everyone a great deal of trouble. They wrapped the dead man in rich silk, and I think there was no one there who did not weep. Ute, that noble woman, and all her household mourned his handsome body with all their hearts. When word went around that there was singing in the cathedral and that he had been laid in his coffin, a great crowd gathered. What offerings they brought for his soul! He had good friends enough, even among his enemies.

Poor Kriemhild said to the chamberlains: "Those of you who wished him well and are loyal to me must take some trouble for my sake. For Siegfried's soul, share out his gold." No child was so small, if it had any sense at all, that it did not go up to make an offering before he was buried. More than a hundred masses were sung that day, and there was a great crush of Siegfried's friends.

When the singing was over, the people went away. Then Lady Kriemhild said: "Do not let me keep watch over this chosen warrior alone tonight. All my joy lies in his body. I will let him lie three days and three nights, until I have had my fill of looking at my dear husband. And what if God wills that death should take me too? Then poor Kriemhild's misery would be well ended."

The people of the town went back to their lodgings. She asked the priests and the monks to stay, and all his household who had served the hero. They had hard nights and weary days. Many a man went without food or drink, and those who wanted some were told that they would be given plenty; Lord Siegmund saw to that. Those were hard days for the Nibelungs.

For those three days, as we have heard, the men who could sing had to bear a great deal of labor. And what offerings people brought them! Those who had been very poor became rich enough. Whatever poor people were found who had nothing to give were sent to the offering with gold from Siegfried's own treasury. Since he could not live, many thousand marks were given for his soul. They gave away lands with their rents across the whole country, wherever cloisters and good people were found, and gave the poor silver and clothing in plenty. By what she did she showed plainly how much she loved him.

On the third morning, at the hour of Mass, the wide churchyard beside the cathedral was full of country people weeping. They served him after his death as one should serve beloved kin. In those four days, so it has been said, thirty thousand marks or even more were given to the poor for his soul. His great beauty and his life lay low.

When God had been served and the singing was finished, a great many people struggled with overwhelming grief. He was carried out of the cathedral to the grave, and those who could hardly bear to lose him were seen weeping and lamenting. The people went with him, crying out loud; no one was glad there, neither woman nor man. Before they buried him they sang and read the service. Oh, what good priests were at his burial!

Before Siegfried's wife had even reached the grave, her loyal heart wrestled so hard with its anguish that again and again they had to douse her with water from the well. Her misery was beyond all measure. It was a great wonder that she survived it at all. Many a lady helped her lament. Then the queen said: "You men of Siegfried, by your loyalty, show me this one mercy. After all my sorrow let me have this small joy, that I may see his handsome head once more." She begged so long, with the strength of her anguish, that they had to break open the splendid coffin.

They brought the lady to where he lay. She lifted his handsome head with her white hand and kissed the good and noble knight, dead as he was. Her bright eyes wept blood for grief. It was a parting of terrible anguish. They carried her away from there, for she could not walk, and then they found the glorious woman lying senseless. Her lovely body might have died of grief.

When the noble lord had been buried at last, all those who had come with him from the Nibelung land were seen to suffer grief beyond measure, and Siegmund was very seldom seen glad. There were some among them who for three days neither ate nor drank for the greatness of their sorrow. But they could not let their bodies waste away entirely. They recovered from their grief, as plenty of people still do.

Chapter 18: How Kriemhild Stayed Behind and Siegmund Rode Home

Kriemhild's father-in-law went to find her. He said to the queen: "We must go home to our own land. I think we are unwelcome guests here on the Rhine. Kriemhild, dear lady, come with me now to my lands. Treachery has robbed us of your noble husband here in this country, but you shall not pay for that. For my son's sake I will stand by you; have no doubt of it. You shall have, my lady, all the power that bold Siegfried gave you before. The land and the crown too shall be yours to command, and all Siegfried's men will serve you gladly."

Then the squires were told that they were to ride, and there was a great rush for the horses; they hated being among their strong enemies. The ladies and the girls were told to pack their clothes.

When King Siegmund was ready to ride, Kriemhild's kinsmen began begging her to stay there with her mother. The lady said: "My lord, that could never be. How could I ever look at the man who has brought such grief on me, poor woman that I am?"

Then young Giselher said: "My dear sister, for your loyalty's sake you should stay here with your mother. The people who have hurt you and darkened your spirit, you need nothing from them. Live on my goods alone."

She said to the warrior: "No, it cannot be. I would die of grief every time I had to look at Hagen."

"I will see to that, my dear sister. You shall live with your brother Giselher, and truly I will make up to you for your husband's death." Then the grief-stricken woman said: "Kriemhild has need of that."

Since young Giselher offered it so kindly, Ute and Gernot and her loyal kinsmen began to plead with her too. They begged her to stay, for she had few relatives among Siegfried's men. "They are all strangers to you," said Gernot. "No man alive is so strong that he will not lie dead one day. Think of that, dear sister, and comfort your heart. Stay with your family; truly, it will be good for you."

She promised Giselher that she would stay. The horses had been led out for Siegmund's men, since they meant to ride to the Nibelung land, and all the warriors' gear was loaded on the pack-horses. Then Lord Siegmund went and stood beside Kriemhild. He said to the lady: "Siegfried's men are waiting by the horses. Now we must ride, for I have no wish to stay among the Burgundians."

Lady Kriemhild said: "My kinsmen advise me, all the loyal ones I have, that I should stay here with them. I have no family in the Nibelung land." It grieved Siegmund deeply when he found out what Kriemhild had decided.

King Siegmund said: "Let no one tell you that. Before all my kinsmen you shall wear the crown with all the power you had before. You shall not suffer for it that we have lost the hero. And come back with us for your little child's sake; my lady, you must not leave him an orphan. When your son is grown, he will comfort your heart. Until then many a bold and good hero shall serve you."

She said: "Lord Siegmund, no, I cannot ride. Whatever becomes of me, I must stay here with my kinsmen, who help me mourn." That answer did not please the good warriors at all. They all said the same thing: "Then we may well say that only now has real sorrow come to us. If you stay here among our enemies, no heroes ever rode home from a court more sorrowfully."

Kriemhild answered: "You shall ride without fear, in God's keeping. You will be given a good escort; I will have you guarded well all the way to Siegmund's land. And my dear little child I commend to your care, you warriors."

When they understood that she would not come, all Siegmund's men wept together. How bitterly Siegmund parted from Lady Kriemhild! Now he knew what misery was. "Curse this festival!" said the noble king. "Never again will a king and his kinsmen suffer, for the sake of a pastime, what has happened to us here. We shall never be seen among the Burgundians again."

Then Siegmund's men said openly: "There may yet be a journey back to this land, once we find out for certain who killed our lord. They have enemies enough among his kinsmen, and strong ones."

He kissed Kriemhild. How sorrowfully he spoke, when he saw for certain that she would stay: "Now let us ride home to our land, with no joy in us. Only now do I know all my sorrows."

They rode from Worms down to the Rhine without an escort. They could be very sure that if anyone attacked them with hostile intent, the bold Nibelungs would know how to defend themselves. They asked no one's leave to go. But then Gernot and Giselher were seen coming to Siegmund in friendship; his loss grieved them, and the bold, spirited heroes made sure he knew it. Prince Gernot said courteously: "God in heaven knows well that I bear no guilt for Siegfried's death, and I never heard it said who his enemy was here. I have good reason to mourn him."

Then young Giselher gave them a good escort. He brought the king and his warriors, full of sorrow, out of the land and home to the Netherlands. How few of their kinsmen were found in good spirits there!

How they fared after that, I cannot tell you. At Worms, Kriemhild could be heard lamenting at every hour, and no one comforted her heart or her spirit except Giselher; he was loyal and good. Beautiful Brunhild sat in her arrogance. However much Kriemhild wept, it meant nothing to her, and she was never again ready to show her any good faith. Later Lady Kriemhild caused her the bitterest sorrow of heart in return.

Chapter 19: How the Nibelung Treasure Was Brought to Worms

When noble Kriemhild was left a widow like this, Count Eckewart stayed on in the land with his men and served her every day; and often he helped his lady mourn his lord. At Worms, beside the cathedral, they built a house for her, wide and very large, rich and grand, and there she lived from then on with her household, without joy. She loved to go to church, and she went faithfully. To the place where her beloved lay buried she went all the time, with a heavy heart; how seldom she missed a day. She asked the good God to care for his soul, and she wept for the warrior many times over, with great loyalty.

Ute and her household comforted her at every hour, but her heart was so terribly wounded that nothing could help her, whatever comfort they offered. She had the greatest longing for her dear husband that any woman ever had for the man she loved; you could see her great goodness in it. She mourned until her end, as long as she lived. Later, bold Siegfried's wife took her revenge, and took it with courage.

So she sat with her grief after her husband's death, it is all true, for a good three and a half years, and in all that time she never spoke a word to Gunther and never once set eyes on her enemy Hagen.

Then the hero of Tronje said: "If you could manage to make a friend of your sister again, then the Nibelung gold would come to these lands. You could win a great deal of it, if the queen were well-disposed toward us."

"Let us try it," said Gunther. "My brothers are with her. We will ask them to bring it about that she is our friend, if we can win her over to the point that she is glad to see it."

"I do not believe," said Hagen, "that it will ever happen."

Then he had Ortwin go to court, and the margrave Gere too. When that was done, they brought Gernot as well, and young Giselher. Then they tried it with Lady Kriemhild in a friendly way. Bold Gernot of Burgundy said: "Lady, you have mourned Siegfried's death too long. The king will give you proof that he did not kill him. People hear you lamenting so terribly, all the time."

She said: "No one accuses him of it. Hagen's hand struck him down, at the place where he could be wounded. When he found that out from me, how could I have believed that he hated him? I would have taken good care," said the queen, "never to betray his life. Then I could stop my weeping now, poor woman that I am. I will never be a friend to the ones who did it." Then Giselher, that very handsome man, began to plead with her.

"I will greet the king." When she had granted him that, Gunther was seen standing before her with his closest kinsmen. But Hagen did not dare come into her presence; he knew his guilt well enough. He had done her wrong. Since she was willing to give up her hatred of Gunther, and he was to kiss her, it would have suited him far better if the wrong had never been done to her on his advice; then he could have gone to Kriemhild with nothing on his conscience. Never was peace made among kinsmen with so many tears. Her loss still hurt her. She forgave them all, except the one man: no one would ever have killed Siegfried if Hagen had not done it.

Not long after that they arranged for Lady Kriemhild to have the great treasure brought from the Nibelung land to the Rhine. It was her morning gift, the gift a husband gives his bride the morning after the wedding, and it was hers by right. Giselher and Gernot went to fetch it. Kriemhild ordered eight thousand men to bring it from where it lay hidden, where the warrior Alberich guarded it with his closest kinsmen.

When they saw the men from the Rhine coming for the treasure, bold Alberich said to his kinsmen: "We dare not keep back any of the treasure from her, since the noble queen claims it as her morning gift. And yet it would never happen at all," said Alberich, "if we had not lost, along with Siegfried, the good cloak of invisibility — and lost it so badly. Fair Kriemhild's beloved wore it all the time. Now it has turned out badly for Siegfried, worse luck, that the hero took that cloak from us and that all this land had to serve him." Then the keeper went to where the keys were kept.

Kriemhild's men stood before the mountain, and some of her kinsmen with them. They had the treasure carried down to the sea and into the ships, and it was carried on the waves upriver to the Rhine. Now you can hear wonders told of that treasure. As much as twelve wagons could carry at the most, they hauled it away from the mountain in four days and nights, and each wagon had to make three trips a day. It was nothing but gems and gold, and if the wages of the whole world had been paid out of it, it would not have been a single mark the poorer. Truly, Hagen had not wanted it for nothing. The best of it all lay buried in it: a little rod of gold, and whoever learned its secret could be master over every single man in all the world. Many of Alberich's kinsmen came away with Gernot.

When they had stored the treasure in Gunther's land and the queen first took charge of it, chambers and towers were filled with it. No one ever heard such wonders told of wealth. And had there been a thousand times as much, if only Lord Siegfried were alive and well, Kriemhild would gladly have stood beside him with empty hands. No hero ever won a more loyal wife.

Now that she had the treasure, she brought many warriors no one knew into the land. Yes, the lady's hand gave so freely that such generosity was never seen again. She practiced many good virtues; people granted the queen that. She began to give now to the poor and the rich alike, so freely that Hagen said if she lived much longer she would win so many men into her service that it would end in sorrow for them.

Then King Gunther said: "Her life and her goods are her own. How am I to stop whatever she does with them? I had a hard enough time winning her goodwill back. Let us not care where she shares out her silver and her gold."

Hagen said to the king: "A sensible man would not leave any of that treasure to any woman. With her gifts she will bring on the day that the bold Burgundians may bitterly regret."

King Gunther said: "I swore her an oath that I would never do her any wrong again, and I will keep it from now on. She is my sister."

Then Hagen said: "Let me be the guilty one."

Some of their oaths were not kept. They took the mighty treasure from the widow, and Hagen got hold of all the keys. That made her brother Gernot angry when he learned the truth of it. Lord Giselher said: "Hagen has done my sister a great wrong. I ought to put a stop to it. If he were not my kinsman, it would cost him his life." Siegfried's wife wept all over again.

Lord Gernot said: "Before this gold troubles us forever, we should have the whole of it sunk in the Rhine; then it would belong to no one." Very pitifully she went and stood before her brother Giselher. "Dear brother," she said, "you must think of me. You must be my guardian, for my life and my goods both." He said to the lady: "It shall be done when we come back again. We are planning to ride."

The king and his kinsmen then left the land, the very best men among them that could be found anywhere — all but Hagen alone. He stayed behind out of the hatred he bore Kriemhild, and he stayed behind on purpose. Before the mighty king came home again, Hagen had taken the whole treasure. He poured it all into the Rhine at Loche. He thought he would make use of it himself; that was never to be. The princes came back, and many men with them. Kriemhild began to lament her great loss, with her maidens and her ladies; it grieved them bitterly. Giselher would gladly have stood by her with all his loyalty. They all said together: "He has done wrong." Hagen kept out of the princes' anger until he had won back their favor, and they let him live. But Kriemhild could never have been more his enemy than she was now.

Before Hagen of Tronje hid the treasure like this, they had bound themselves with oaths so strong that it should stay hidden as long as any one of them was alive. Afterwards they could not give it to themselves or to anyone else. Her heart was weighed down with fresh sorrow, for her husband's death and because they had taken her wealth from her so completely, and her lament never stopped as long as she lived, down to her last day. After Siegfried's death, it is the plain truth, she lived in great pain for thirteen years, and she could not forget the warrior's death. She was loyal to him; most people grant her that.

Part Two: Kriemhild's Revenge

Chapter 20: How King Etzel Sent to Worms for Kriemhild

It was in the days after Lady Helche died, when King Etzel was looking for another wife, that his kinsmen urged him toward a proud widow in the land of Burgundy. Her name was Lady Kriemhild. Now that beautiful Helche was gone, they said: "If you ever want to win a noble wife, the highest and the best a king ever had, then take this same lady. Strong Siegfried was her husband."

The mighty king said: "How could that ever happen? I am a pagan, and I have never been baptized, and the lady is a Christian. She would never consent. It would take a miracle for it ever to come about."

But the quick-witted men said: "What if she does it anyway, for the sake of your great name and your great wealth? It is worth putting the question to so noble a woman. You could love her beautiful self very gladly."

The noble king asked: "Which of you knows the people and the land by the Rhine?" Then good Rüdiger of Pöchlarn spoke up: "I have known those noble kings since I was a child: Gunther and Gernot, fine and noble knights, and the third is called Giselher. Each of them does everything that honor and good breeding can ask of a man, and their forefathers always did the same."

Etzel said again: "Friend, you must tell me whether she is worthy to wear a crown in my land. And if she is as beautiful as I have been told, my closest kinsmen will never have cause to regret it."

"In beauty she is the equal of my own dear lady, mighty Helche. Truly, there could be no king's wife in all this world more beautiful. The man she chooses as her husband can take real comfort in it."

The king said: "Then go and win her, Rüdiger, as you love me. And if I ever lie beside Kriemhild, I will reward you for it as well as I know how, for you will have done my will to the very fullest. From my own treasury I will have you given horses and clothes and everything you want, so that you and your companions can live in good cheer. I will have plenty of it made ready for your embassy."

To that Rüdiger, the mighty margrave — the lord who guarded Etzel's western border — answered: "To ask for your goods would do me no credit. I will gladly be your messenger to the Rhine at my own expense, with what I already have from your hand."

The mighty king said: "Now, when will you set out after the lovely woman? May God keep you on the journey in all honor, and my lady too. And may good luck help me, so that she looks kindly on us."

Rüdiger said: "Before we leave this land, we must first make ready weapons and clothing, so that we can stand before princes with honor. I will take five hundred fine men to the Rhine, so that wherever they see me and mine in Burgundy, every one of them will say of you that no king ever sent so many men so far in such fine style as you have sent to the Rhine. And, mighty king, if you will not give it up on this account: she was bound in noble love to Siegfried, Siegmund's son. You have seen him here yourself. In all truth, one had to grant him real honor."

The noble king said: "If she was that warrior's wife, then the noble prince was precious enough that I should not disdain the queen. Her great beauty pleases me well."

The margrave said: "Then I will tell you this: we set out from here in twenty-four days. I will send word to Gotelind, my dear wife, that I myself mean to be the messenger to Kriemhild."

Rüdiger sent his message to Pöchlarn, and the margrave's wife grew both sad and proud. He told her that he was to win a wife for the king, and she thought lovingly of beautiful Helche. When the margravine heard the message, part of her grieved, and weeping became her, as she wondered whether she would ever have a lady like the one before. Whenever she thought of Helche, it pained her to the heart.

Rüdiger rode out of Hungary seven days later. King Etzel was glad and in high spirits at that. There in the city of Vienna they made their clothes ready for him; then he could put off his journey no longer. At Pöchlarn Gotelind was waiting for him, and the young margravine, Rüdiger's daughter, was glad to see her father and his men. Beautiful girls were waiting for them with love. Before noble Rüdiger rode to Pöchlarn out of the city of Vienna, all their clothes had been loaded in full on the pack-horses. They traveled so well guarded that hardly anything was taken from them.

When they came into the town of Pöchlarn, the host very kindly had his traveling companions lodged and made them comfortable, and mighty Gotelind was very glad to see the host come home. So was his dear daughter, the young margravine; nothing could have pleased her more than his coming. And how glad she was to see the heroes from the land of the Huns! With a laughing heart the noble young lady said: "A great welcome to you, my father and his men." Then many a good knight gave the young margravine warm and careful thanks. Gotelind knew Lord Rüdiger's mind very well.

That night, as she lay close beside Rüdiger, how kindly the margravine asked him where the king of the Huns had sent him. He said: "My lady Gotelind, I will gladly tell you. I am to win another wife for my lord, now that beautiful Helche is dead. I am going to ride to the Rhine for Kriemhild. She is to be a mighty queen here among the Huns."

"God grant it," said Gotelind, "and may it come about, since we hear people speak of so much honor in her. Perhaps she can take our lady's place for us in our old age. We would gladly let her wear the crown among the Huns."

The margrave said: "My dearest, to the men who are to ride with me from here to the Rhine you must offer your goods with a loving hand. When heroes travel richly equipped, they are in high spirits."

She said: "There is not one of them who will gladly take from me whom I will not give whatever suits him, before you and your men leave this place." The margrave said: "That is a kindness to me."

Ah, what rich samite was carried out of her chambers! The noble warriors were given a generous share of it, and clothed in it with care from the neck to the spurs. Rüdiger had picked out the men who pleased him for this.

On the seventh morning the host rode out of Pöchlarn with his warriors. They took weapons and clothes in plenty through the land of Bavaria, and on the road they were seldom set upon by robbers. Within twelve days they reached the Rhine. There the news could not be kept hidden. The king and his men were told that foreign guests had come, and the host began to ask whether anyone knew them; if so, he should say. People saw their pack-horses carrying such heavy loads that it was plain they were very rich. Lodgings were found for them at once in the wide city.

When the strangers had settled in, the same lords were closely watched. People wondered where these warriors could have ridden to the Rhine from. The host sent for Hagen, in case he might know them.

The hero of Tronje said: "I have not seen them. But once we have a look at them, I can tell you for certain where they have ridden from into this land. They must be very foreign indeed if I do not know them soon."

The guests had now been given lodgings. The envoy had come in very rich clothes, and so had his companions in arms, and they rode to court wearing fine garments, cut with great skill. Quick Hagen said: "As far as I can judge — for I have not seen the lord for a long time — they ride exactly as if it were Rüdiger from the land of the Huns, that bold and noble warrior."

"How am I to believe," said the king at once, "that the lord of Pöchlarn has come into this land?" As King Gunther finished speaking, bold Hagen caught sight of good Rüdiger. He and his kinsmen all ran out, and five hundred knights were seen getting down from their horses. The men from the land of the Huns were well received; no messengers ever wore such splendid clothes.

Hagen of Tronje called out in a loud voice: "Now welcome, in God's name, to these warriors, the lord of Pöchlarn and all his men!" The quick Huns were received with full honor. The king's closest kinsmen went to where they stood, and Ortwin of Metz said to Rüdiger: "In all our days we have never seen guests here so gladly. I say that in all truth."

The warriors thanked them all around for their greeting. With their followers they went into the hall, where they found the king among many splendid men. The lord rose from his seat; that was done out of great courtesy. How courteously he went to meet the envoys! Gunther and Giselher received the guest and his men with great warmth, as was only proper. He took good Rüdiger by the hand and led him to the seat where he himself sat. The guests were served drink, and gladly: the very best mead, and the finest wine to be found anywhere in the land along the Rhine. Giselher and Gere had both come in, and Dankwart and Volker had heard about the guests too. They were in good spirits, and they greeted the fine, noble knights in the king's presence.

Hagen of Tronje said to his lord: "These warriors ought to repay forever what the margrave once did for our sake. Beautiful Gotelind's husband deserves a reward for it."
