King Gunther said: "I cannot hold back the question. You must tell me how they both are, Etzel and Helche in the land of the Huns." The margrave said: "I will gladly tell you." He rose from his seat with all his men and said to the king: "If it may be so, and you give me leave, prince, then I will hold nothing back. I will gladly tell you the news I bring."

The king said: "Whatever news has been sent to us through you, I give you leave to tell it without taking counsel with my kinsmen. Let me and my men hear it, for I begrudge you no honor you may win here."

The worthy envoy said: "My great lord sends to you on the Rhine his loyal service, and to all the kinsmen you may have. This embassy is made in great loyalty. The noble king asks you to share his sorrow. His people are without joy. My lady is dead: mighty Helche, my lord's wife. By her death many a young lady has been orphaned, daughters of noble princes whom she had raised. The land is in a pitiful state because of it. There is no one now, alas, to care for them faithfully, and so I think the king's grief will be slow to settle."

"God reward him," said Gunther, "for sending his service so willingly to me and to my kinsmen. I have gladly heard his greeting here, and both my kinsmen and my men will gladly repay it."

Gernot, the warrior of Burgundy, said: "The world will always mourn beautiful Helche's death, for the many virtues she knew how to practice." Hagen agreed with what he said, and so did many another warrior.

Then Rüdiger, the noble, lordly envoy, spoke again: "Since you give me leave, king, I shall tell you more of what my dear lord has sent to say to you, for since Helche died his life has gone very hard. My lord has been told that Kriemhild is without a husband, that Lord Siegfried is dead. If that is how things stand, and if you will allow it, she is to wear a crown before Etzel's warriors. That is what my lord told me to say."

The mighty king said, and his manner was very courtly: "She shall hear my wish, if she is willing. I will let you know her answer within three days. Why should I refuse Etzel before I have learned her mind?"

Meanwhile the guests were made comfortable, and they were served so well that Rüdiger admitted he had friends among Gunther's men. Hagen served him gladly; Rüdiger had once done the same for him.

So Gunther let the matter rest until the third day. Then the king sent for counsel — he acted very wisely — to see whether his kinsmen thought it a good thing that Kriemhild should take King Etzel as her husband. All of them advised it together, all but Hagen. He said to Gunther the warrior: "If you have any sense, you will take good care that you never do it, even if she were willing to go along with you."

"Why," said Gunther, "should I not go along with it? Whatever happiness comes to the queen, I ought to be glad to grant her; she is my sister. We ought to be arranging it ourselves, if it can bring her honor."

Hagen said: "Drop this talk. If you knew Etzel as I know him, and if she is to love him, as I hear you say, then you will have reason to worry for the first time, and with good cause."

"Why?" said Gunther. "I will take very good care never to come so near him that I have to suffer any hatred from him, even if she were his wife." But Hagen said: "I will never advise it."

They sent for Gernot and for Giselher, to see whether the two lords thought it a good thing that Kriemhild should love the mighty, noble king. Hagen still argued against it, and no one else.

Giselher of Burgundy, the warrior, said: "Friend Hagen, you can still show some loyalty. Make up to her the wrongs you have done her. Whatever good comes to her, you ought to leave it unopposed. Truly, you have done my sister so many wrongs," bold Giselher went on, "that she would have every reason to be angry with you. No man ever robbed a woman of more joy."

Hagen answered: "I will tell you plainly what I know for certain: if she takes Etzel, and lives to see the day, she will still do us great harm, however she manages it. Truly, many a fine man will be in her service."

Bold Gernot answered Hagen: "It can stay that way until they are both dead — that we never ride into Etzel's land. Let us be loyal to her; that serves our honor."

Hagen said again: "No one can tell me otherwise. If noble Kriemhild is to wear Helche's crown, she will do us great harm yet, however she brings it about. You should let it be; that suits you warriors far better."

Giselher, beautiful Ute's son, said angrily: "We need not all act like traitors. Whatever honor comes to her, we ought to be glad of it. Whatever you say, Hagen, I will serve her, out of my loyalty."

When Hagen heard that, it put him in a dark mood. Gernot and Giselher, the proud, good knights, and mighty Gunther decided in the end that if Kriemhild consented, they would let it happen without any ill will.

Prince Gere said: "I will tell the lady that she should look kindly on King Etzel. So many warriors are his subjects and stand in great fear of him. He can well make up to her whatever wrong has been done her."

The quick warrior went to where he found Kriemhild. She received him kindly, and how quickly he spoke: "You may well greet me gladly and give me the messenger's reward. Good luck is about to part you from all your troubles, and very soon. For the sake of your love, lady, one of the very best men who ever won a king's land with full honor, or who ever ought to wear a crown, has sent here to us. Noble knights have come wooing; your brother told me to tell you so."

The grief-stricken woman said: "God forbid that you and all my kinsmen should mock a poor wretch like me. What could I be to any man who had ever known heartfelt love from a good wife?"

She argued hard against it. But then her brother Gernot came, and young Giselher, and they lovingly begged her to comfort her heart: if she took the king, it would truly be good for her. But no one could bring the woman round to loving any man at all. Then the warriors begged her: "If you do nothing else, at least let it be seen that you are willing to receive the envoy."

"That one thing I will not refuse," said the noble woman. "I would gladly see Rüdiger, for his many virtues. If it were not he who had been sent here, whoever else the envoy was, he would never have met me at all. Tomorrow," she said, "tell him to come here to my chamber. I will let him hear my wishes in full; I will tell him myself." And her great lamenting broke out all over again.

Noble Rüdiger wanted nothing more than to see the high-born queen. He knew himself to be so persuasive that, if it could ever be done at all, she would have to let the warrior talk her round.

Early the next morning, when Mass had been sung, the noble envoys came. There was a great crowd of those who wanted to go to court with Rüdiger, and many a splendid man was seen in full dress. High-born Kriemhild, very heavy at heart, waited for Rüdiger, the good and noble envoy. He found her in the clothes she wore every day, while her household around her wore rich clothes in plenty. She went to the door to meet him and received the noble man very kindly. He came in to her as one of twelve. He was shown great respect; no higher envoys had ever come to her.

The lord and his men were asked to sit. The two margraves, Eckewart and Gere, fine and noble warriors, were seen standing before her. Out of regard for the lady of the house, they saw no one there in good spirits. They saw many beautiful women seated before her, but Kriemhild herself knew nothing but grief. The dress over her breast was wet with hot tears, and the noble margrave saw it on Kriemhild plainly.

The lordly envoy said: "Most noble king's daughter, will you allow me and the companions who came here with me to stand before you and tell you the news we have ridden here to bring?"

"You have my leave," said the queen. "Whatever you wish to say, I am of a mind to hear it gladly. You are a good envoy." But the others could hear her unwilling heart plainly enough.

Lord Rüdiger of Pöchlarn said: "Etzel, a noble king, has sent you, lady, great love in all loyalty, here into this land. He has sent many good warriors here to woo for your love. He offers you with all his heart love without sorrow, and he is ready to give you the same lasting friendship he once gave Lady Helche, who lay close to his heart. Truly, since he lost her virtues he has had many a joyless day."

The queen said: "Margrave Rüdiger, if there were anyone who knew my bitter pain, he would not ask me to love any man. Truly, I lost one of the best husbands a woman ever had."

"What can make up for grief," said the very bold man, "but loving companionship? Whoever can bring that about, and then chooses the one who is rightly suited to him — nothing does so much good against heartfelt sorrow. And if you are willing to love my noble lord, you shall hold power over twelve mighty crowns. On top of that my lord will give you the lands of a full thirty princes, every one of which his brave hand has conquered. You shall also be lady over many worthy men who were subjects of my lady Helche, and over many ladies of high princely blood who were under her rule," said the bold, brave warrior. "And besides, my lord says to tell you that if you consent to wear the crown beside the king, he will give you the very highest power Helche ever held. You shall hold it with full authority over Etzel's men."

The queen said: "How could I ever again want to be a hero's wife? Death has done me such bitter wrong through one man that I must live without joy to the end of my days."

The Huns said: "Mighty queen, your life with Etzel will be so glorious that it will delight you forever, if it comes to pass, for the mighty king has many a splendid warrior. If Helche's young ladies and your maidens were to be one household together, warriors would take heart at the sight of them. Let yourself be persuaded, lady; it will truly be good for you."

She said, with all her courtesy: "Let the matter rest now until early tomorrow, when you are to come here again. Then I will give you my answer to what you have in mind." The bold, good warriors had to accept that.

When they had all gone back to their lodgings, the noble lady sent for Giselher and for her mother as well. She told them both that weeping was what became her, and nothing else.

Her brother Giselher said: "Sister, I have been told, and I am ready to believe it, that King Etzel would wipe away all your sorrows, if you take him as your husband. Whatever anyone else advises, I think it well done. He can well make up your loss to you," Giselher went on. "From the Rhône to the Rhine, from the Elbe down to the sea, there is no king so powerful. You may rejoice right away, if he makes you his wife."

She said: "My dearest brother, why do you advise me this? Mourning and weeping would always suit me better. How could I go to court before warriors? If I was ever beautiful, that is gone from me now."

Lady Ute said to her dear daughter: "Whatever your brothers advise, dear child, do it. Follow your kinsmen and good things may come to you. I have watched you in great grief for far too long."

Then Kriemhild prayed to God many times to grant her the means to have gold, silver and clothes to give, as she had once at her husband's side, when he was still alive and well. But she never again lived through so happy an hour.

She thought to herself: "If I give myself to a pagan — and I am a Christian woman — I will carry shame in the world forever. If he gave me every kingdom there is, I could never do it." And there she let it rest. All night until daybreak the lady lay in her bed deep in thought, and her bright eyes never dried, until in the morning she went to matins again.

Just at the hour of Mass the kings came, and again they took their sister in hand. Truly, they urged her to love the king of the land of the Huns, and not one of them found the lady anything but sad.

Then they sent for Etzel's men, who by now would gladly have taken their leave and gone, won or lost, however it turned out. Rüdiger came to court, and the heroes agreed among themselves that they should learn the noble prince's true mind, and learn it soon; that seemed the best course to all of them, for the road home to their own land was long. Rüdiger was brought to where he found Kriemhild.

The warrior began to beg the noble queen, very lovingly, to let him hear what message she wished to send to Etzel's land. I think he found nothing in her but refusal: she would never love any man again. The margrave said: "That would be a wrong. Why would you let such beauty waste away? You can still, with honor, become a good man's wife."

All their begging did no good, until Rüdiger spoke to the noble queen in private: he would make good to her whatever had ever happened to her. At that her great misery began, a little, to ease.

He said to the queen: "Let your weeping be. If you had no one among the Huns but me alone, and my loyal kinsmen, and my men, anyone who had ever done you any wrong would pay dearly for it."

That lightened the lady's heart a good deal. She said: "Then swear me an oath that whatever anyone does to me, you will be the first to right my wrongs." The margrave said: "Lady, I am ready to do that." Rüdiger swore to her then, with all his men, to serve her always in loyalty, and that the noble warriors from Etzel's land would never refuse her anything that could bring her honor. Rüdiger's hand gave her that pledge.

Then the loyal woman thought: "Now that I have won so many friends, I will let people say whatever they like about me, a woman full of grief. What if my dear husband's death might still be avenged?"

She thought: "Since Etzel has so many warriors, if I command them, I can do whatever I want. And he is so rich, too, that I will have something to give. Hateful Hagen has stripped me of everything I had."

She said to Rüdiger: "If I had heard that he was not a pagan, I would gladly go wherever he wished, and take him as my husband." The margrave said: "Let that thought go, lady. He has so many warriors of the Christian faith that you will never be unhappy at the king's side. And what if you managed to bring him to be baptized? For that alone you might gladly become King Etzel's wife."

Her brothers said again: "Now promise it, sister. You must let your unhappiness go." They begged her for so long that at last, sad as she was, she promised before the heroes that she would be Etzel's wife. She said: "I will do as you say — poor queen that I am — and go to the Huns as soon as it can be managed, once I have the kinsmen who will take me to his land." And beautiful Kriemhild gave her hand on it before the heroes.

The margrave said: "If you have two men, I have more than that. It will be done well: we will bring you across the Rhine with full honor. You are not to stay here in Burgundy any longer, lady. I have five hundred men, and my kinsmen besides. They shall serve you here, and at home too, lady, whatever you command, and I will do the same myself; whenever you remind me of this promise, I will never be ashamed of it. Now have your riding gear made ready — Rüdiger's advice will never bring you sorrow — and tell the maidens you mean to take with you. Truly, many a chosen hero will come to meet us on the road."

She still had the harness they had ridden with before, in Siegfried's time, so that she could take many a maiden along with honor whenever she wanted to leave. Ah, what good saddles they found for the beautiful women! If they had ever worn rich clothes before, many more were made ready now for their journey, for they had been told so much about the king. They unlocked the chests that had stood well locked until then. For a full four and a half days they were hard at work, bringing out of the presses all that lay inside. Then Kriemhild began to open up her whole treasury: she wanted to make all of Rüdiger's men rich.

She still had gold from the Nibelung land, and she meant to share it out among the Huns with her own hand — so much that a hundred horses could not have carried it all. Hagen heard the news of what Kriemhild was doing. He said: "Since Kriemhild will never be my friend, Siegfried's gold must stay here too. Why should I leave my enemies such great wealth? I know very well what Kriemhild would do with this treasure. If she took it away from here, I am convinced it would be handed out to buy hatred against me. Besides, they have no horses that could carry it. Hagen means to keep it; tell Kriemhild that."

When she heard this news, it was a bitter wrong to her. All three kings were told of it too, and they would gladly have changed it, but when that did not happen, noble Rüdiger spoke very cheerfully: "Mighty queen, why do you grieve over the gold? King Etzel loves you so much that once his eyes have seen you, he will give you more than you could ever spend. That I will swear to you, lady."

The queen said: "Most noble Rüdiger, no king's daughter ever had greater wealth than what Hagen has stripped from me." Then her brother Gernot went to her chamber. With the king's authority he thrust the key into the door, and Kriemhild's gold was handed out: thirty thousand marks' worth, or more. He told the guests to take it, and Gunther was glad of that.

Gotelind's husband, the lord of Pöchlarn, said: "If my lady Kriemhild had all of it, every bit that was ever carried out of the Nibelung land, little of it would ever touch my hand or the queen's. Have them keep it, for I want none of it. Truly, I brought so much of my own from home that we will do very well on the road, and our provision for the journey is in splendid order."

Before this, in all that time, her maidens had filled twelve chests with the very best gold to be found anywhere. That they took with them, and many of the women's ornaments that they were to have on the journey. The power of grim Hagen seemed to her too great. She still had a thousand marks of her gold for offerings, and she gave it out for the soul of her dear husband. Rüdiger thought that done in great loyalty.

The grieving lady said: "Where are my friends, those who are willing to go into exile for my sake? Those who will ride with me into the land of the Huns, let them take my treasure and buy horses and clothes."

Margrave Eckewart said to the queen: "From the first day I became one of your household I have served you loyally," said the warrior, "and I will go on doing the same for you to the end of my days. I will take five hundred of my own men with me too, and give them to your service in true loyalty. Nothing will ever part us, unless death does it." Kriemhild bowed to him for those words; truly, she had need of them.

Then the horses were led out, for they wanted to set off. There was great weeping among her kinsmen. Mighty Ute and many a beautiful girl showed how much Lady Kriemhild's going grieved them. She took a hundred high-born maidens with her, dressed as well as suited them. The tears fell from their bright eyes. But later they also lived to see much joy with Etzel.

Then Lord Giselher came, and Gernot too, with their followers, as their good breeding demanded. They wanted to escort their dear sister on her way, and they brought a full thousand fine warriors of their own. Quick Gere came, and Ortwin, and Rumold, master of the kitchen, had to be there too. They arranged night quarters all the way to the Danube. Gunther himself rode no farther than a little way out of the city.

Before they left the Rhine they had sent their messengers ahead, riding fast, into the land of the Huns, to tell the king that Rüdiger had won the noble, high-born queen to be his wife.

Chapter 21: How Kriemhild Left Worms for the Land of the Huns

Let the messengers ride. Now we will tell you how the queen traveled through the lands, and where Giselher and Gernot parted from her. They had served her as their loyalty required. They rode with her as far as Pföring on the Danube, and there they began to ask the queen's leave, for they meant to ride back to the Rhine. Good kinsmen could not part like that without tears.

Swift Giselher said to his sister: "Whenever you have need of me, my lady, if anything troubles you, send me word, and I will ride into Etzel's land to serve you."

She kissed those who were her kinsmen on the mouth. Then you could see the margrave's men, Rüdiger's men, taking a loving leave of them. The queen had many lovely girls with her, a hundred and four of them, wearing costly dresses of rich patterned silk, and many broad shields were carried close beside the ladies on the road. Then many a splendid warrior turned back from her and rode home.

They moved on quickly, down through Bavaria. Then the news was told that a great many unknown strangers had hurried on ahead, to the place where a monastery still stands today, where the Inn flows into the Danube. In the city of Passau there lived a bishop. The lodgings emptied, and the prince's court as well, as everyone hurried up into Bavaria to meet them, to the place where Bishop Pilgrim found beautiful Kriemhild. The warriors of that country were not at all sorry to see so many lovely girls riding in her train; their eyes courted the daughters of the noble knights. Afterwards good lodgings were given to the guests.

The bishop rode to Passau with his niece. When the townspeople were told that Kriemhild was coming, their prince's sister's child, the merchants gave her a fine welcome. The bishop had hoped they would stay, but Lord Eckewart said: "That cannot be. We must travel on down into Rüdiger's land. Many warriors are waiting for us there, for they all know very well that we are coming."

Beautiful Gotelind knew the news by now. She made herself ready with great care, and her noble daughter too. Rüdiger had sent her word that he thought it right for her to comfort the queen's spirits by riding out to meet her with his men, up to the Enns. When that word was given, the roads were busy on every side: people set out to meet the guests on horseback and on foot. By now the queen had come to Eferding. There were plenty of men in Bavaria who, as was their habit, might have robbed travelers on the road, and they could easily have done the guests harm. The noble margrave had seen to it that this did not happen: he led a thousand knights and more. Gotelind, Rüdiger's wife, had come as well, and with her came many a noble warrior in splendid style.

When they had crossed the Traun, onto the field by the Enns, they saw huts and tents set up, where the guests were to spend the night. Rüdiger paid for all of it. Beautiful Gotelind left her lodgings behind her and went along the roads, and many a fine palfrey went with her, its bridle jingling. The welcome was a fine one, and it pleased Rüdiger greatly. The warriors who came along the roads from both sides rode so that you had to praise them, and there were many of them. They practiced their knightly games, which many a girl watched, and the knights' service did not displease the queen either. When Rüdiger's men reached the guests, you could see splinters of lances flying up into the air from the warriors' hands, as knights do, and they rode before the ladies as if a prize hung on it.

Then they let that be, and many men greeted one another very warmly. Then they escorted beautiful Gotelind to where she could see Kriemhild. The men who knew how to serve ladies had little rest. The lord of Pöchlarn rode to his wife. The noble margravine was not sorry that he had come home from the Rhine safe and sound; a good part of her worry gave way to great joy. When she had welcomed him, he told her to dismount on the grass with all the ladies who were with her. Many a noble man was kept busy then, serving the ladies with the greatest care.

Then Lady Kriemhild saw the margravine standing there with her household. She would let her horse go no nearer, but pulled the palfrey up by the bridle and asked to be lifted down from the saddle at once. You could see the bishop leading his sister's child, he and Eckewart together, to Gotelind. Everyone drew back at once. Then the exiled queen kissed Gotelind on the mouth. Rüdiger's wife spoke with great tenderness: "How happy I am, dear lady, that I have seen your lovely self in these lands with my own eyes. Nothing dearer could have happened to me in all this time."

"God reward you, noble Gotelind," said Kriemhild. "If I keep my health, and Botelung's son keeps his, it may yet bring you joy that you have seen me." Neither of them knew what was bound to happen later.

Many girls went up to one another with perfect manners, and the warriors were eager to serve them. After the greetings they sat down on the clover, and they made many acquaintances that had been strangers to them before. The ladies were served wine; it was already midday. The noble company did not linger there any longer but rode on to where they found the many broad tents, and there plenty of service waited for the noble guests.

That night they rested until early morning, and the people of Pöchlarn made ready to lodge so many foreign guests. Rüdiger had arranged it so well that they lacked for very little. The windows in the walls stood open; the castle at Pöchlarn was thrown wide. In rode the guests, a welcome sight, and the noble host had good comfort prepared for them. Rüdiger's daughter went with her household to greet the queen with great affection, and her mother the margrave's wife was there too. Many a young lady was greeted with love. They took one another by the hand and went into a wide hall, very finely built, with the Danube flowing beneath it. They sat facing the breeze and passed the time very happily.

What more they did there I cannot tell you. But Kriemhild's warriors were heard complaining that the journey was going so slowly, for it grieved them. And what good warriors rode with her from Pöchlarn! Rüdiger offered them loving service, and the queen gave Gotelind's daughter twelve red arm-rings, and clothes as good as any she had brought into this land. The Nibelung gold had been taken from her, but even with the little she still had, she won the heart of everyone who saw her. The host's household received great gifts. In return Lady Gotelind showed the guests from the Rhine such honor, and so kindly, that very few of the strangers could be found who were not wearing her jewels or her splendid clothes.

When they had eaten and were ready to leave, the lady of the house offered Etzel's wife her loyal service, and the young lady, her daughter, was hugged and kissed a great deal. The girl said to the queen: "Whenever you think it right, I know my dear father will gladly send me to you in the land of the Huns." Kriemhild saw very well how loyal the girl was to her.

The horses were saddled and brought before Pöchlarn, and the noble queen took her leave of Rüdiger's wife and his daughter. Many a lovely girl parted from them with a greeting too. They very seldom saw one another after those days. From Melk people came out carrying in their hands many rich golden cups filled with wine, to welcome the guests on the road. A lord lived there named Astolt, and he showed them the road into Austria, down the Danube toward Mautern. There the queen was served very well later on.

The bishop parted lovingly from his niece. How earnestly he urged her to conduct herself well, and to win honor for herself as Helche had done! And what great honor she won later among the Huns! They brought the guests on to the Traisen, and Rüdiger's men cared for them attentively until the Huns came riding across the country. Then great honor was shown to the queen. Near the Traisen the king of the Huns had a mighty castle, famous far and wide, called Traismauer. Lady Helche had lived there before, and had shown such great goodness that it will surely never happen again, unless it is through Kriemhild, who also knew how to give. After all her sorrow she could well enjoy the joy of it: that Etzel's men honored her too, and in time she won that honor in full measure among the heroes.

Etzel's power was so widely known that at all times you could find at his court the boldest warriors ever heard of, among Christians and among pagans alike, and they had all come there with him. At all times there were both at his court, which will surely never be seen again: Christian ways and also the pagan faith. Whatever kind of life each man led, the king's generosity saw to it that they were all given enough.

Chapter 22: How Kriemhild and Etzel Were Married in Vienna

She stayed at Traismauer until the fourth day. All that while the dust on the road never settled; it rose on every side as if the land were burning, as King Etzel's men came riding through Austria. By now the king too had been told exactly how splendidly Kriemhild was coming through the lands, and at the thought of it his sorrows melted out of his mind. The king hurried to where he would find the beautiful woman.

On the roads ahead of Etzel rode many bold warriors of many tongues, Christians and pagans, troop after wide troop, and when they found the lady they came up to her in splendor. Many a man rode there from Russia and from Greece. Poles and Wallachians were seen going at speed on their fine horses, riding hard, and whatever the customs of their lands, they left very little of them out. From the land of Kiev rode many a warrior, and the wild Pechenegs, who were busy shooting with their bows at the birds that flew overhead; they drew their arrows back hard, right to the bow's edge.

By the Danube, in the Austrian land, lies a town called Tulln. There she came to know many a foreign custom she had never seen before. Many who received her there would later come to grief through her.

In front of King Etzel rode a retinue, glad and very rich, courtly and merry: a good twenty-four princes, noble and lordly. They wanted nothing more than to see their lady. Duke Ramung from Wallachia came galloping up before her with seven hundred men; they were seen moving like birds in flight. Then came Prince Gibeche with splendid troops. Hornboge the swift, with a full thousand men, turned away from the king and toward his lady. There was loud shouting, after the custom of that land, and the kinsmen of the Huns rode wildly there too.

Then came bold Hawart of Denmark and swift Iring, a man with no treachery in him, and Irnfried of Thuringia, a handsome man. They received Kriemhild in a way that did her honor, with twelve hundred men they led in their troop. Then came Lord Blödel, Etzel's brother from the land of the Huns, with three thousand; in great splendor he came to where he found the queen. Then came King Etzel, and Lord Dietrich too with all his companions. There was many a noble knight there, worthy and good, and all of it lifted Lady Kriemhild's spirits high.

Then Lord Rüdiger said to the queen: "My lady, here I will receive the noble king. Whomever I tell you to kiss, you must kiss. Truly, you cannot greet all of Etzel's men alike."

Then they lifted the noble queen down from her palfrey. Mighty Etzel waited no longer; he got down from his horse with many a bold man, and he was seen going joyfully toward Kriemhild. Two mighty princes, as we are told, walked beside the lady and carried her train as King Etzel came up to meet her, and she received the noble prince graciously with a kiss. She pushed back her headdress, and her lovely color shone out of the gold. Many a man there said that Lady Helche could not have been more beautiful. Close by stood the king's brother Blödel. Rüdiger, the mighty margrave, told her to kiss him, and King Gibeche too; Dietrich stood there as well. Etzel's wife kissed twelve of the warriors, and many another knight she received with a greeting.

All the while Etzel stood beside Kriemhild, the young men did what people still do today: many a splendid charge with lances was ridden there, by Christian heroes and by pagans too, each after their own custom. How truly knightly it was, the way Dietrich's men sent their lance-shafts flying in splinters high over the shields from good knights' hands! The German guests pierced many a shield's rim. A great din of breaking shafts was heard. By now all the warriors of the land had come, and the king's guests too, many a noble man. Then the mighty king went off with Lady Kriemhild.

They saw a magnificent tent standing near them, and all around it the field was filled with pavilions where they were to rest after their hardships. Many a beautiful girl was led by the heroes to her place under it, where they sat with the queen on richly cushioned seats. The margrave had arranged it so well that everyone found it very fine, and Kriemhild's seat gladdened Etzel's heart. What Etzel said then, I cannot tell you. Her white hand lay in his right hand, and they sat together lovingly, for Rüdiger the warrior would not let the king be alone with Kriemhild in private.

Then the buhurt was called off everywhere, and the great noise ended with honor. Etzel's men went to the pavilions, and lodgings were given them far and wide on every side. The day was now at its end. They took their ease until the bright morning was seen shining again, and then many a man had come to the horses. Oh, what entertainments were begun in the king's honor! The king asked the Huns to arrange everything with due honor. Then they rode from Tulln to the city of Vienna, where they found many a lady adorned, and they received King Etzel's wife with every honor. Everything they needed was prepared for them in great abundance. Many a glad hero rejoiced at the celebration; they began to find lodgings, and the king's wedding feast began joyfully.

They could not all be lodged in the city, so Rüdiger asked those who were not guests to take lodgings out in the country. I think that at every hour you could find at Kriemhild's side Lord Dietrich and many another warrior. They had given up rest and taken on labor to keep the guests in good spirits, and Lord Rüdiger and his friends had fine entertainment. The wedding fell on a Whitsunday, when King Etzel lay beside Kriemhild in the city of Vienna. I think she had never, with her first husband, gained so many men for her service. With gifts she made herself known to men who had never seen her before, and many among them said to the guests: "We thought Lady Kriemhild had no wealth at all; now she has done wonders here with her gifts."

The wedding feast lasted seventeen days. I think no one tells of any king whose wedding was greater; if there was one, it has been kept from us. Everyone who was there wore brand-new clothes. In the Netherlands, I think, she had never before sat among so many warriors; and I believe this too: however rich in wealth Siegfried was, he never gained so many noble warriors as she saw standing before Etzel. Nor did anyone ever give at his own wedding so many rich mantles, long and wide, or such good clothes, of which there was plenty to be had, as they all gave now for Kriemhild's sake. Her kinsmen and the guests too were of one mind: they would spare no kind of goods. Whatever anyone asked of them they gave at once, so that many warriors stood there stripped of their clothes out of sheer generosity.

She thought of how she had sat by the Rhine beside her noble husband, and her eyes grew wet; she hid it hard, so that no one could see. After so much sorrow, so much honor had come to her.

Whatever anyone else did in generosity was nothing but wind beside Dietrich: whatever Etzel, Botelung's son, had given him was now spent away entirely. The hand of generous Rüdiger worked great wonders there too. Prince Blödel from Hungary had many traveling chests emptied of their silver and gold, and all of it was given away. The king's heroes were seen living in high delight. Werbel and Swemmel, the king's minstrels, each of them won at the wedding, I think, a good thousand marks or better still, where beautiful Kriemhild sat crowned beside Etzel.

On the eighteenth morning they rode out of Vienna. Many shields were cut to pieces in knightly sport by the spears the warriors carried in their hands. So King Etzel came all the way into the land of the Huns. At old Hainburg they stayed the night. No one could tell the number of the people, or with what strength they rode across the land. Oh, what beautiful women they found in his homeland! At mighty Wieselburg they went aboard ships. The water was covered with horses and men as if it were solid ground, wherever you saw it flowing. The travel-weary ladies had rest and comfort. Many a good ship was lashed together, so that neither the waves nor the current could harm them, and over them many a good tent was spread, as if they still had both land and field beneath them.

Then the news came from there to Etzel's castle, and inside it women and men rejoiced. Etzel's household, which the lady Helche had looked after before, lived many a happy day afterward with Kriemhild. Many a noble girl stood waiting there who had suffered great grief from Helche's death. Kriemhild still found seven kings' daughters there, and all Etzel's land was adorned by them. The young lady Herrat still looked after the household — Helche's sister's daughter, full of virtues, Dietrich's betrothed, the child of a noble king, Nentwin's daughter; much honor came to her later. Her heart rejoiced at the coming of the guests, and great wealth had been made ready for it too. Who could tell you how the king held court afterward? Never did people live better among the Huns with any queen.

When the king rode up from the shore with his wife, each lady was named to noble Kriemhild, and she greeted them all the better for it. Oh, how mightily she sat later in Helche's place! Much loyal service was shown to her. Then the queen dealt out gold and clothing, silver and jewels; whatever she had brought with her over the Rhine to the Huns had to be given away entirely. All the king's kinsmen and all his vassals later became subject to her service, so that Lady Helche had never held such power as the woman they now served — and would serve until Kriemhild's death.

The court and the land stood in such honor that at all times one found there whatever amusement each heart desired, through the king's love and the goodness of the queen.

Chapter 23: How Kriemhild Planned to Avenge Her Wrongs

They lived together in great honor, it is the plain truth, until the seventh year. In that time the queen gave birth to a son, and nothing could have made King Etzel happier. She would not rest until she had brought it about that Etzel's child was baptized according to Christian rite. He was named Ortlieb, and there was great rejoicing over all of Etzel's lands.

Whatever good qualities Lady Helche had possessed, Lady Kriemhild now worked hard for many a day to match them. Herrat, the exiled maiden, taught her the customs of the court; in secret, Herrat still grieved deeply for Helche. Strangers and household alike knew Kriemhild well. They said that no lady had ever ruled a king's land better or more generously, and they held that to be the truth. She carried that praise among the Huns until the thirteenth year.

By now she had learned that no one ever crossed her, as a king's warriors still do for a prince's wife, and that at every hour she saw twelve kings standing before her. And she thought of the many wrongs that had been done her at home. She thought too of the many honors in the Nibelung land that had once been hers to command, and that Hagen's hand had stripped from her entirely with Siegfried's death, and whether that could ever still be made to cost him sorrow at her hands. "It would, if I could only bring him into this land." She dreamed that her brother Giselher walked often hand in hand with her, and that they kissed each other again and again in gentle sleep. Later they both came to know hardship.

I believe it was the evil devil himself who counseled Kriemhild to break the friendship she had made with Giselher, the brother she had kissed in Burgundy as a sign of peace. Then her dress began to be stained once more with hot tears. It lay on her heart early and late: how, through no fault of her own, she had been brought to the point of having to love a pagan man. Hagen and Gunther had forced that misery on her.

She rarely let that wish go from her heart. She thought: "I am so powerful, and I have such great wealth, that I can still do my enemies a wrong. For Hagen of Tronje I would be ready and willing. My heart aches so often for my loyal kin. If I could be near the ones who did me wrong, then my husband's death would be well avenged. I can hardly wait for it," said Etzel's wife.

All the king's men, Kriemhild's warriors, held her dear, and that was only right. Eckewart had charge of her treasury, and it won him friends. No one could stand in the way of what Kriemhild wanted. All the time she was thinking: "I will ask the king." She would ask him, in the kindest way, to let her kinsmen be brought into the land of the Huns. No one detected the evil purpose in the queen.

One night, as she lay beside the king and he held her in his arms, as he always did when he caressed the noble lady — she was as dear to him as his own life — that glorious woman thought of her enemies. She said to the king: "My dearest lord, I would like to ask you something, if it may be done with your good will: that you let me see whether I have earned it, and whether you are truly fond of my kinsmen."

The mighty king answered, and his heart was loyal: "I will make that plain to you. Whatever good fortune comes to those warriors, in their lives or their goods, I must take joy in it, for a woman's love never brought me better kin."

The queen said: "You have been told, I am sure, that I have very high-born kinsmen. That is why it grieves me so that they so seldom care to see me here. I hear the people call me nothing but an exile."

King Etzel said: "My dearest lady, if it did not seem too far to them, I would invite across the Rhine whoever you would like to see here in my lands." The lady rejoiced when she learned his will.

She said: "If you will keep faith with me, my lord, then send messengers to Worms across the Rhine. I will let my kinsmen know what I have in mind, and then many a good and noble knight will come here to our land."

He said: "Whenever you command it, let it be done. You could not be gladder to see your kinsmen than I would be to see noble Ute's children. It troubles me deeply that they have been strangers to us so long. If it pleases you, my dearest lady, I will gladly send my fiddlers to Burgundy for your kinsmen." He had the good fiddlers sent for at once.

They hurried to where the king sat beside the queen. He told the two of them that they were to be his messengers to Burgundy, and he had splendid clothes made ready for them. Clothing was prepared for twenty-four warriors, and the king gave them their message too: how they were to invite Gunther and his men. Lady Kriemhild would speak with them separately.

The mighty king said: "I will tell you what to do. I send my kinsmen my love and every good wish, and ask that they be so kind as to ride here into my land. I have known very few guests I would be so glad of. And if Kriemhild's kinsmen wish to do anything at all for my sake, let them not fail to come to my festival this summer, for so much of my joy depends on my wife's kinsmen."

Then Swemmel, the proud fiddler, asked: "When is your festival to be held in these lands, so that we can tell your kinsmen over there?"

King Etzel said: "At next midsummer."

"We will do whatever you command," said Werbel. The queen asked that the messengers be brought to her chamber in secret, and there she spoke with them. Many a warrior later saw little joy from it.

She said to the two messengers: "Now earn yourselves great wealth, by doing what I want faithfully and well, and by saying what I send home to our land. I will make you rich, and I will give you splendid clothes. And whichever of my kinsmen you may see at Worms on the Rhine, you must never tell them that you ever saw me troubled in spirit. And carry my service to those bold and good heroes.

"Ask them to do what Rüdiger asked of them, and so set me free from all my distress. The Huns think I have no kinsmen at all. If I were a knight, I would go to them myself now and then. And tell Gernot too, my noble brother, that no one in the world could be fonder of him than I am. Ask him to bring our best kinsmen with him into this land, so that it brings us honor. And tell Giselher to remember well that I never suffered any wrong through any fault of his. My eyes would be so glad to see him here. I would love to have him here, for his great loyalty.

"Tell my mother too of the honor I enjoy. And if Hagen of Tronje means to stay behind over there, who is to guide them through the lands? He has known the roads here to the Huns since he was a child."

The messengers had no idea why they were being told not to let Hagen of Tronje stay behind on the Rhine. Later it brought them sorrow. Along with him, many a warrior was marked for a grim death. Letters and messages were now given to them. They set off rich in goods, and they could live in style. Etzel gave them leave to go, and so did his beautiful wife, and they were handsomely dressed in fine clothes.

Chapter 24: How the Envoys Came to the Rhine

When Etzel sent his envoys to the Rhine, the news flew from land to land. By messengers riding hard he asked, and commanded too, that people come to his festival. Many a man found his death there.

The envoys rode out of the land of the Huns toward Burgundy. They had been sent for three noble kings and for their men, who were to come to Etzel; and so they made haste.

They came riding to Pöchlarn, where people were glad to serve them; nothing was left undone. Rüdiger and Gotelind sent their service with them to the Rhine, and so did the dear child the two of them shared. Nor did they let the envoys leave without gifts, so that Etzel's men would travel the better for it. To Ute and her children Rüdiger sent word that there was no margrave anywhere who wished them better than he did. To Brunhild too they sent their service and good wishes, unfailing loyalty and a willing heart. When the envoys had heard these messages and were ready to ride, the margravine asked God in heaven to keep them safe.

Before the envoys had come all the way through Bavaria, quick Werbel sought out the good bishop. What word he sent to his kinsmen on the Rhine I do not know; only that he gave the envoys his red gold as a token of friendship, and let them ride on. Then Bishop Pilgrim said: "If I could see them here, my sister's sons, it would do my heart good, for I can very seldom get to them on the Rhine."

Which roads they took through the lands to the Rhine I cannot tell you. No one robbed them of their silver or their clothes: people feared their master's anger. Truly, that noble, high-born king was a powerful man.

Within twelve days Werbel and Swemmel came to the Rhine, to Worms and its country. The news was told to the kings and their men that strange messengers had arrived, and Gunther began asking about them. The lord of the Rhine said: "Who can tell us where these strangers riding into our land have come from?" No one knew, until Hagen of Tronje saw them. Then he said to Gunther: "There is fresh news for us, I promise you that. I have just seen Etzel's fiddlers here. Your sister has sent them to the Rhine, and for their master's sake we must make them very welcome."

They were already riding up in front of the great hall. No prince's minstrels ever traveled in finer style. The king's household received them at once; they were given good lodgings and their baggage was taken into keeping. Their traveling clothes were rich and so well made that they could have gone before the king in them with honor. But they did not want to wear them any longer at court, and the envoys asked whether anyone there might care to have them. Sure enough, people were found who were glad to take the clothes, and the clothes were sent to them. Then the guests put on far better garments, the kind it is fitting for a king's envoys to wear in state.

Then, with leave, Etzel's men went to where the king was sitting, and people were glad to see it. Hagen sprang up courteously to meet the envoys and greeted them warmly, and the squires thanked him for it. Wanting to hear what news they brought, he began asking how Etzel was faring, and his men. The fiddler said: "The land never stood better, nor the people so happy. You may be sure of that."

They went in to the host. The great hall was full. There the guests were received as is right and proper when such friendly greetings are sent into another king's land. Werbel found a great many warriors there at Gunther's side. The king greeted them courteously: "Welcome, both of you, you fiddlers of the Huns, and your companions with you. Has mighty Etzel sent you here to the land of the Burgundians?"

They bowed to the king. Then Werbel said: "My dear lord sends you his devoted service into this land, and so does Kriemhild, your sister. They have sent us to you warriors in good faith."

The mighty king said: "I am glad of this news. How is Etzel? And Kriemhild, my sister, in the land of the Huns?"

The fiddler said: "I will tell you the news. No people ever fared better than the two of them are faring now, you may be sure of that, and all their household with them, their kinsmen and their men. They were delighted with our journey when we left them."

The king said: "My thanks for his service, which he has sent me, and for my sister's, since it stands as you say, that the king and his men live in happiness. For I asked the news with a worried heart."

The two young kings had now arrived too; they had only just heard the news. For love of his sister, young Giselher was glad to see the envoys, and he spoke to them warmly: "Envoys, you would be very welcome to us if you cared to ride to the Rhine more often. You would find friends here you would be glad to see. Very little harm would come to you in this land."

"We trust you to do us every honor," said Swemmel. "I could not make you understand, with all the wit I have, how lovingly Etzel sends his greetings, and your noble sister too, whose affairs stand in high honor. The king's wife reminds you of your love and your loyalty, and that your heart and your whole self were always devoted to her. But first and foremost we are sent to the king, to ask you to be so good as to ride into Etzel's land. Mighty Etzel ordered us most strictly to beg this of you, and he sends this message to you all: if you will not let your sister see you, then he would at least like to know what he has done to you, that you stay so distant from him and his lands. Even if the queen had never been known to you, he would still like to earn the favor of your coming to see him. If that should happen, it would make him very glad."

King Gunther said: "In seven days' time I will tell you what I have decided with my kinsmen. Meanwhile, go to your lodgings and take a good rest."

Werbel spoke again: "Might it be possible for us to see my lady first, the mighty Ute, before we settle in to rest?" Noble Giselher answered very courteously: "No one shall stop you. If you want to go to her, you will be doing exactly what my mother wishes. She will be glad to see you, for the sake of my sister Lady Kriemhild. You will be welcome to her."

Giselher brought them to where he found the lady. She was pleased to see the envoys from the land of the Huns, and in her kindness she greeted them warmly. Then the good and courtly envoys told her their news. "My lady sends you her service and her loyalty, truly," said Swemmel. "If it could be that she saw you often, believe me, there would be nothing in the world that could make her happier."

The queen said: "That cannot be. However much I would like to see my dear daughter often, the noble king's wife lives too far from me, to my sorrow. May she and Etzel be blessed forever. Let me know, before you leave here, when you want to go back. I have not been so glad to see messengers in a long time as I am to see you." The squires promised her that they would.

The men from the land of the Huns rode to their lodgings. Meanwhile the mighty king had sent for his kinsmen. Noble Gunther asked his men how they liked the proposal, and many began to say that he could well ride to Etzel's land. The best men he had there advised him so — all but Hagen alone, and he was grimly against it. He said to the king in private: "You are declaring war on yourself. You know very well what we have done. We will always have reason to fear Kriemhild, for I killed her husband with my own hand. How could we dare ride into Etzel's land?"

The mighty king said: "My sister gave up her anger. She kissed us lovingly and forgave us for everything we ever did to her, before she rode away from here — unless, Hagen, the quarrel is with you alone."

"Whatever the envoys from the Huns tell you, do not let them fool you," said Hagen. "If you want to see Kriemhild, you could well lose your honor there, and your lives too. King Etzel's wife has a very long memory for a wrong."

Then Prince Gernot said to the council: "Just because you have good reason to fear death in the Hunnish kingdoms, should we give up seeing our sister on that account? That would be very wrong of us."

Prince Giselher said to the warrior: "Since you know you are guilty, friend Hagen, you should stay here and look after yourself, and let those who dare come with us to my sister."

At that the warrior of Tronje grew angry. "I do not want you taking anyone on this road who would dare to ride to that court more boldly than I. Since you will not give it up, I will show you that plainly enough."

Then Rumold the warrior, the master of the kitchen, spoke: "You could have strangers and friends looked after here just as you please, for you have plenty of everything. As far as I know, you are not Hagen's hostage yet. If you will not take Hagen's advice, then take Rumold's, for I am your loyal and devoted servant: stay here, for my sake, and leave King Etzel over there with Kriemhild.

"How could you ever be more comfortable anywhere in the world? Here you are perfectly safe from your enemies. Dress yourselves in fine clothes, drink the best wine, and make love to lovely women. And besides, you are served the finest food any king in the world ever had. And even if that were not so, you ought to stay for your beautiful wife's sake, before you go risking your life like a child. So I advise you to stay. Your lands are rich; a man can redeem his pledges better here at home than over there among the Huns, where who knows how things stand. Stay at home, my lords. That is Rumold's advice."

"We will not stay," said Gernot, "since my sister and mighty Etzel have invited us so lovingly. Why should we refuse? Anyone who does not want to go can stay at home."

Hagen answered: "Do not take my words amiss, whatever comes of it. I advise you on my loyalty: if you want to stay alive, ride to the Huns well armed. Since you will not give it up, send for your men, the best you can find or can get anywhere, and from among them all I will choose a thousand good knights. Then Kriemhild's malice can do you no harm."

"I will gladly follow that," the king said at once, and he sent messengers riding far and wide through his lands. They brought in three thousand heroes or more. Those men never dreamed they were riding to earn such terrible sorrow.

They rode cheerfully into Gunther's land. Everyone who was to ride out of Burgundy was given horses and clothes, and the king won the goodwill of a great many men. Then Hagen of Tronje had his brother Dankwart bring eighty of their own warriors to the Rhine. They came like knights; those quick men brought armor and clothing with them into Gunther's land. Then bold Volker came, a noble minstrel, with thirty of his men for the journey to court. They wore clothes a king could have worn. He sent word to Gunther that he meant to go to the Huns.

I will tell you who this Volker was. He was a noble lord, and many good warriors in Burgundy were his vassals. Because he could play the fiddle, he was called the minstrel. Hagen chose a thousand men he knew well. He had seen for himself what their hands had done in hard fighting, and everything else they had ever done. No one could say anything of them but that they were brave.

Kriemhild's envoys were growing very weary of the delay, for they were in great fear of their master. Every day they asked for leave to go, and Hagen would not grant it. That was done on purpose. He said to his lord: "We must take care not to let them ride away until we ourselves are ready to set out for Etzel's land seven days after them. If anyone means us harm, we will know it that much sooner. And then Lady Kriemhild will have no time to arrange for anyone to harm us on her advice. But if that is her plan, it may turn out badly for her, for we are taking so many chosen men with us from here."

Shields and saddles and all the clothes they wanted to take into Etzel's land were now fully ready for many a bold man. Then Kriemhild's envoys were told to come before Gunther. When the envoys came in, Gernot said: "The king will do as Etzel has asked us. We will gladly come to his festival and see our sister. You need have no doubt of that."

King Gunther said: "Can you tell us when the festival is to be, and on what days we should arrive?"

Swemmel said: "At next midsummer, that is the truth."

The king gave them leave — something that had not happened before now — to go before Lady Brunhild with his blessing, if they wanted to see her. Volker stepped in to prevent it, and that was done out of kindness to her. "My lady Brunhild is not in such good spirits just now that you could see her," the good knight said. "Wait until tomorrow, and then they will let you see her." But when they thought they would see her, it could not be managed.

Then the mighty prince, who was fond of the envoys, had his gold carried out on the broad shields, out of his own generosity; he had plenty of it. His kinsmen gave them rich gifts as well. Giselher and Gernot, Gere and Ortwin showed plainly that they too were open-handed. They offered the envoys gifts so rich that, for fear of their master, they did not dare accept them.

Then the envoy Werbel said to the king: "Sir King, let your gifts stay here in your own land. We cannot carry them away. My lord forbade us to accept any gifts at all; and besides, we have very little need of them."

The lord of the Rhine was much put out that they wanted to refuse the goods of so mighty a king. In the end they had to accept his gold and his clothes, and they took them home to Etzel's land.

They wanted to see Ute before they left, so quick Giselher brought the minstrels before his mother Ute. The lady sent this message: whatever honors her daughter enjoyed, that was a joy to her. Then the queen had her embroidered braid and her gold given to these same minstrels for Kriemhild's sake, because she loved her, and for King Etzel's. They could gladly accept it: it was given in loyalty.

Now the envoys had taken their leave of the women and the men there, and they rode away happily into Swabia. Gernot had his heroes escort them that far, so that no one would do them harm. When the men who were charged with their care had parted from them, Etzel's power kept the peace for them on every road. No one took horse or clothing from them, and they hurried on at full speed into Etzel's land.

Wherever they knew of any kinsmen of the king, they let them know that the Burgundians would very shortly be coming from the Rhine into the land of the Huns. Bishop Pilgrim was told the news too. As they rode down the road past Pöchlarn, the news was told to Rüdiger — that was not left undone — and to Gotelind, the margrave's wife. That she was going to see them made her very happy.

The minstrels were seen hurrying on with their news. They found Etzel in the city of Esztergom. They told the king of greeting after greeting that had been sent to him, and he flushed red with joy. When the queen learned for certain that her brothers were coming into the land, she was glad at heart. She rewarded the minstrels with very great gifts; that was done for her honor.

She said: "Now tell me, both of you, Werbel and Swemmel, which of my kinsmen mean to be at the festival — the best of those we invited here into this land? And tell me, what did Hagen say when he heard the news?"

He said: "He came to the council early one morning, and he had little good to say about it. When the others agreed to the journey here to the land of the Huns, to grim Hagen it was the same as a sentence of death. Your brothers are coming, all three kings, in high spirits. Who else is coming with them I cannot tell you for certain. Volker, the bold minstrel, promised to ride with them."

"I could easily do without ever seeing Volker," said the king's wife. "But I am fond of Hagen. He is a very fine hero. That we are going to see him here lifts my heart."

Then the queen went to where she saw the king. How lovingly Kriemhild spoke to him! "How do you like this news, my dear lord? What my heart has always desired is going to come about now."

"Your wish is my joy," the king said. "I was never so glad to see my own kinsmen coming into my land as I am for your family's sake. Now that they are coming, my worries are gone."

The king's officers had the great hall and the hall of state everywhere fitted out with seats for the dear guests who were on their way to them. Later those guests would bring the king much bitter weeping.

Chapter 25: How the Lords All Rode to the Huns

Now let us leave the story of how they lived at Etzel's court. Warriors in such high spirits never rode so splendidly into any king's land: they had everything they wanted, both weapons and clothes. The lord of the Rhine fitted out his men — a thousand and sixty knights, as I have heard, and nine thousand squires — for the festival. Those they left at home would weep for it later.

The gear was being carried across the courtyard at Worms when an old bishop from Speyer said to beautiful Ute: "Our friends mean to ride to the festival. May God keep their honor safe there."

Then noble Ute said to her sons: "You should stay here, good heroes. Last night I dreamed of a terrible danger: every bird in this land lay dead."

"Whoever goes by dreams," said Hagen, "does not know what the right thing to say is when his honor is fully at stake. I want my lord to go to court and take his leave. We should be glad to ride to Etzel's land. A good hero's hand can serve kings well there, where we shall see Kriemhild's festival." So Hagen advised the journey; but later he regretted it.

He would have advised against it, if Gernot had not thrown such rough words at him. Gernot reminded him of Siegfried, Lady Kriemhild's husband, and said: "That is why Hagen wants to stay out of the great journey to court."

Hagen of Tronje answered: "I do nothing out of fear. Whenever you give the order, heroes, set to work. Truly, I will gladly ride with you to Etzel's land." Later many a helmet and shield-rim was hewn apart by his hand.

The boats were ready and many men were there. Whatever clothes they had were carried on board. They were busy until evening, and then they left home in the best of spirits. Tents and pavilions were pitched on the grass on the far side of the Rhine. When that was done, the king asked his beautiful wife to stay with him a little longer, and that night she still lay in the arms of her handsome husband. Trumpets and flutes sounded early in the morning, the signal to ride, and they got themselves ready. Whoever held a lover in his arms held her close. Later King Etzel's wife parted many of them in sorrow.

Beautiful Ute's sons had a vassal who was bold and loyal. When they were about to leave, he told the king privately what was on his mind. "I have to grieve," he said, "that you are making this journey to court." His name was Rumold, and he was a hero with his hands. He said: "Who will you leave your people and your lands to? What a shame no one can turn you warriors from your purpose! That news from Kriemhild never seemed good to me."

"The land is in your care, and my little child. Serve the ladies well; that is my wish. If you see anyone weeping, comfort them. Truly, King Etzel's wife will never do us any harm."

The horses stood ready for the kings and their men. Many a man who lived there in high spirits parted with loving kisses. Later many a lovely woman would have to weep for it.

When the swift warriors were seen going to their horses, many ladies could be seen standing there sorrowfully. Their hearts told them that this long parting meant great harm to come; that never sits easily on anyone's heart.

The swift Burgundians set out. There was a great stir in the land; on both sides of the mountains, women and men wept. But whatever their people did behind them, they rode away cheerfully. The Nibelung warriors rode with them, a thousand coats of mail strong; they had left many beautiful women at home whom they never saw again. Siegfried's wounds still pained Kriemhild.

Gunther's men set their course toward the Main, up through eastern Franconia. Hagen led them that way, for he knew the road well. Their marshal was Dankwart, the hero from the land of Burgundy. As they rode from eastern Franconia toward Schwanfeld, you could tell the princes and their kinsmen, those praiseworthy heroes, by their lordly bearing. On the twelfth morning the king came to the Danube.

Hagen of Tronje rode in front of everyone; he was a steadying comfort to the Burgundians — the Nibelungs, as they were called now. The bold warrior got down on the sandy bank and quickly tied his horse to a tree. The river was in flood, the boats hidden away. It was a great worry to the Nibelungs how they would get across; the current was far too wide. Many a fine knight dismounted.

"Harm may well come to you here, lord of the Rhine," said Hagen. "Now you can see for yourself: the river is in flood and its current is very strong. Truly, I think we are going to lose many a good hero here today."

"Why blame me, Hagen?" said the noble king. "For your own honor's sake, discourage us no more. Find us the ford across to the far bank, so that we can get both horses and gear away from here."

"I am not so tired of my life," said Hagen, "that I want to drown in these wide waters. Before that, many a man in Etzel's lands shall die by my hands; I have every intention of it. Stay by the water, you proud good knights. I will look for the ferrymen myself along the river, the ones who will bring us over into Gelfrat's land." Then strong Hagen took up his good shield.

He was very well armed. He carried the shield off with him, his helmet laced on, bright enough; and over his coat of mail he wore a sword so broad that it cut terribly on both edges. He searched up and down for the ferrymen. Then he heard water splashing and began to listen: in a beautiful spring, wise women were cooling themselves and bathing. Hagen noticed them and crept up quietly. When they realized it, they fled in a hurry, and they were very proud of having got away from him. The hero took their clothes, and did them no other harm.

Then one of the mermaids — her name was Hadeburg — said: "Noble knight Hagen, we will tell you here, if you give us our clothes back, bold warrior, how this journey to the Huns' court will go for you."

They floated in front of him on the water like birds, and so their wits seemed to him strong and good. Whatever they wanted to tell him, he believed it all the more. And what he asked them, they told him very clearly. She said: "You may ride to Etzel's land in safety. I pledge my loyalty on it, here and now, that heroes never rode into any kingdom to greater honor than you will. Believe that, truly."

Hagen's heart was glad at those words. He gave them their clothes and waited no longer. But when they had put on their strange garments, they told him the truth about the journey to Etzel's land. The other mermaid — her name was Sieglinde — said: "I want to warn you, Hagen, son of Aldrian. For the sake of the clothes, my aunt has lied to you. If you go to the Huns, you are badly deceived. You should turn back; there is still time for that. For you bold heroes have been invited so that you may die in Etzel's land. Whoever rides there has taken death by the hand."

Hagen said: "You are lying for no reason. How could it come about that we should all stay there dead, because of anyone's hatred?" They began to tell him the story more plainly.

One of them went on: "It must be so: not one of you can survive there, except the king's chaplain. We know this for certain. He will come home safe and sound to Gunther's land."

Bold Hagen said in a grim mood: "That would be a hard thing to tell my lords, that we should all lose our lives among the Huns. Now show us the way over the water, you wisest of all women."

She said: "Since you will not give up the journey: up there by the water stands an inn, and inside is a ferryman, and nowhere else." He asked no more about what he wanted to know.

But one of them called after the troubled warrior: "Wait, Lord Hagen, you are in far too much of a hurry. Hear the rest, how you can get across to the far bank. The lord of this border country is called Else. His brother is the warrior Gelfrat, a lord in Bavaria. It will go hard with you if you mean to pass through his territory; guard yourselves well, and deal very carefully with the ferryman. He is so fierce that he will not let you live unless you keep your wits about you with the hero. If you want him to ferry you over, pay him his fee. He guards this land and is loyal to Gelfrat. And if he does not come in good time, shout across the water and say your name is Amelrich. He was a good hero who left this land because of a feud. The ferryman will come to you when he hears that name."

Arrogant Hagen bowed to the women. He said nothing more, only kept his silence. Then he went along the water, upstream over the sand, until he found an inn on the far bank. He began to shout loudly across the river: "Now come and fetch me, ferryman," said the good warrior, "and I will give you an arm-ring of red gold for your trouble. I truly need this crossing, you should know."

The ferryman was so rich that it did not suit him to serve anyone, and so he very seldom took pay from any man; and his servants were very proud too. Hagen stood there all the while alone on his side of the river. Then he shouted with such force that the whole river rang with it, for the hero's strength was huge: "Now fetch me: I am Amelrich, Else's man, who fled this land because of a bitter feud."

High up on his sword he held out an arm-ring to him, bright and beautiful, of red gold, to be ferried over into Gelfrat's land. The arrogant ferryman took the oar in his own hand. This same ferryman had married only recently. Greed for great wealth comes to a very bad end: he wanted to earn Hagen's red gold, and so he died a grim death by the sword at the warrior's hands.

The ferryman rowed hard across to the bank. When he did not find the man he had heard calling, he grew angry in earnest, and when he saw Hagen he spoke to the warrior in a fury. "You may well be called Amelrich, but you are nothing like the man I expected to find here. He was my brother by father and mother. Now that you have tricked me, you can stay on this side."

"No, by almighty God," said Hagen. "I am a warrior from far away, with warriors in my care. Take my fee today as a friend and ferry me over. I am truly well disposed toward you."

The ferryman answered: "That cannot be. My dear lords have enemies, and that is why I ferry no stranger into this land. If you value your life, step out onto the sand this minute."

"Do not refuse me," said Hagen, "for my heart is heavy. Take this good gold from me as a token of friendship and ferry us over: a thousand horses, and as many men." The grim ferryman said: "That will never be done."

He raised a strong oar, big and broad, and struck Hagen — Hagen was not pleased by that — so that he stumbled to his knees in the boat. So fierce a ferryman had never come the way of the man of Tronje. Then he wanted to anger the arrogant stranger even more. He struck him with a pole so that it broke clean apart over Hagen's head — Hagen was a strong man — and from that Else's ferryman came to great harm. In a grim mood Hagen reached at once for his scabbard, where he found a sword. He struck off the ferryman's head and flung it into the river. The news soon reached the proud Burgundians.

At the very moment he killed the boatman, the boat drifted downstream, which troubled him enough. He began to tire before he could right it again; King Gunther's man pulled with all his strength. With very swift strokes the stranger turned it, until the strong oar snapped in his hand. He wanted to get to the warriors out on a stretch of bank, and there was no other oar. Hey, how quickly he bound it up with a shield-strap, a narrow band! He steered downstream toward a wood, and there he found his lord standing on the bank. Many a handsome man came down to meet him.

The swift good knights received him with a warm greeting. Then they saw blood steaming in the boat from the deep wound he had dealt the ferryman, and the warriors asked Hagen plenty of questions. When King Gunther saw the hot blood sloshing in the boat, how quickly he spoke: "Why do you not tell me, Hagen, where the ferryman has gone? I suspect your great strength has cost him his life."

Hagen answered with a lie: "I found the boat by a wild willow and loosed it with my own hand. I have seen no ferryman here today, and no one has come to any harm here through my fault."

Lord Gernot of Burgundy said: "Today I must fear for the lives of dear kinsmen. Since we have no boatmen ready, how are we to get across? I stand here grieving over that."

Hagen called out loudly: "Lay the gear down on the grass, squires. I remember that I was once the very best ferryman to be found on the Rhine. I am confident I can bring you across into Gelfrat's land."

So that they would cross the river sooner, they drove the horses in, and the swimming went well, for the strong current took not one of them; some drifted a long way downstream as they tired. Then they carried their gold and their clothes to the boat, since there was no way around the crossing. Hagen was the master there, and he ferried many a mighty warrior over onto the sand of the unknown land. First he brought across a full thousand splendid knights, then his own warriors; and there were still more. Nine thousand squires he ferried over to that shore. The hand of the man of Tronje was anything but idle that day.

When he had brought them all safely over the river, the swift good warrior remembered the strange news the wild mermaids had told him before. Because of it the king's chaplain nearly lost his life.

He found the priest beside the chapel baggage, leaning with his hand on the relics. That did him no good at all once Hagen saw him; God's poor priest had to suffer for it. He flung him out of the boat, in a great hurry about it. Plenty of them cried: "Grab him, sir, grab him!" Young Giselher grew angry, but Hagen would not let any of them stop him.

Lord Gernot of Burgundy said: "What good does the chaplain's death do you, Hagen? If anyone else did this, you would take it badly. What has the priest done to you that you have turned on him?"

The priest swam hard; he wanted to survive, if only someone would help him. But that could not be, for strong Hagen was in a rage and pushed him under, which no one thought well of. When the poor priest saw no help coming, he turned back the other way; he had a hard time of it. He could not swim, but God's hand helped him, so that he came safe and sound back onto the bank he had left. There the poor priest stood and shook out his robe. By that, Hagen saw plainly there was no way around what the wild mermaids had told him. He thought: these warriors must lose their lives.

When they had unloaded the boat and carried everything off it that the three kings' men had brought aboard, Hagen smashed it to pieces and threw it into the river. The bold good warriors wondered greatly at that.

"Why are you doing that, brother?" said Dankwart. "How are we going to get across when we ride back from the Huns, home to the Rhine?" Later Hagen told him that could never be. The hero of Tronje said: "I do it on this chance: if we have any coward on this journey who wants to run away from us out of cowardly fear, he will still have to die a shameful death in this water."

They had with them one man from the land of Burgundy, a hero with his hands, whose name was Volker. He spoke his whole mind with great wit. Whatever Hagen did, it seemed good to the fiddler. Their horses were ready, the pack-horses well loaded. On the journey so far they had taken no harm that troubled them — except the king's chaplain, who had to walk back to the Rhine on his own feet.

Chapter 26: How They Fought Else and Gelfrat

When they had all come up onto the sand, the king began to ask: "Who will show us the right roads through this land, so that we do not ride astray?" Strong Volker said: "I will see to that alone."

"Now hold still, all of you," said Hagen, "knights and squires. One should listen to one's friends; truly, that seems right to me. I have grim news to tell you: we shall never come back to the land of Burgundy. Two mermaids told me so early this morning, that we shall not come home again. Now I will advise you what to do: arm yourselves, heroes, and guard yourselves well. We have strong enemies here, so let us ride ready for war. I thought I would catch those wild mermaids in a lie. They swore that not one of us would get home to our land alive, except the chaplain. That is why I wanted so badly to drown him today."

The news flew from company to company, and swift heroes went pale with grief as they began to fear a hard death on this journey to court. Truly, they had good reason. They had crossed at Möringen, where Else's ferryman had lost his life, and now Hagen spoke again: "Since I have earned myself enemies on the road, we shall certainly be attacked. I killed that ferryman early this morning, and they know the story well by now. So set to work, heroes: if Gelfrat and Else fall on our company here today, let it end badly for them. I know them for such bold men that they will not leave it undone. Let the horses go at an easier pace, so that no one thinks we are running away along the road."

"I will follow that advice," said the warrior Giselher. "But who is to guide the company across the land?" They answered: "Let Volker do it. The bold minstrel knows every path and road here." Before the wish was fully spoken, you could see the swift fiddler standing there armed. He laced on his helmet; his battle gear was of a splendid color, and to his spear-shaft he tied a pennon, and it was red. Later he came with the kings into terrible peril.

By now sure word of the ferryman's death had reached Gelfrat, and the very strong Else had heard it too. Both of them grieved for it. They sent for their heroes, who were quickly ready.
