In a very short time — I will let you hear it — men were seen riding toward them who had done great damage in hard wars, a huge force: seven hundred or more came to Gelfrat. As they set off after their fierce enemies, they waited for their lords, who were somewhat too eager; they wanted to take out their anger on the bold strangers. Because of that, more of the lords' kinsmen were lost later.

Hagen of Tronje had arranged things well — how could any hero have guarded his kinsmen better? He took the rear guard with his own men and his brother Dankwart. That was very wisely done. The daylight had run out on them; they had none left. He feared harm and sorrow for his friends. They rode under their shields through the Bavarian land, and a short while later the heroes were attacked.

On both sides of the road, and hard behind them, they heard hooves clattering; the men coming were in too much of a hurry. Bold Dankwart said: "They mean to attack us here. Now lace on your helmets; I think that would be wise." They halted on their way, as they had to. In the dark they saw the gleam of bright shields. Hagen would keep silent no longer: "Who is hunting us on the road?" And Gelfrat had to answer him.

The margrave of Bavaria said: "We are looking for our enemies, and we have ridden hard after them. I do not know who killed my ferryman today. He was a hero with his hands, and I grieve for him badly."

Hagen of Tronje said: "So the ferryman was yours? He would not ferry us over. The fault is mine: I killed the warrior, and truly, I had no choice. I very nearly got my death from his hands. I offered him gold and clothing as his fee, hero, to carry us over into your land. That made him so angry that he struck me with a heavy pole, and I was grim enough about it. Then I got to my sword and fought off his anger with a deep wound. That is how the hero died. I will make amends for it to you, however seems good to you." But it came to a fight; they were hard-set men.

"I knew very well," said Gelfrat, "when Gunther rode through here with his company, that Hagen of Tronje would do us harm. Now he shall not live. The hero must be the pledge here for the ferryman's death."

Gelfrat and Hagen leaned their spears over their shields for the thrust; each of them wanted at the other. Else and Dankwart rode splendidly; they tested who the other was, and there was fierce fighting. How could heroes ever have tested each other better? A mighty joust from Gelfrat's hand sent bold Hagen over the back of his horse. His breast-strap broke, and he learned what fighting was. The crash of shafts rang out from their companies. Then Hagen, who had gone down from the thrust onto the grass, got up again. I think he was in no gentle mood toward Gelfrat.

Who held their horses for them I do not know. Hagen and Gelfrat had both come down onto the sand, and they ran at each other; their companions helped them, and fighting broke out all around. But however fiercely Hagen sprang at Gelfrat, the noble margrave struck such a great piece off his shield that fire flew from it. Gunther's bold vassal very nearly died of it. He began to call to Dankwart: "Help me, dear brother! A hero with his hands has set on me, and he will not let me live." Bold Dankwart said: "I will be the one to settle this."

The hero sprang closer and struck Gelfrat a blow with a sharp weapon, and from it he fell dead. Else wanted badly to avenge the man then, but he and his company left the field with heavy losses. His brother had been killed, and he himself was wounded; a good eighty of his warriors stayed there on the spot in grim death. The lord had to turn and flee from Gunther's men.

As the men of Bavaria gave way and fled, you could hear the terrible blows ringing out behind them. The men of Tronje chased their enemies; those who had never thought they would pay for anything were all in a great hurry now. Still in the pursuit, the warrior Dankwart said: "We should turn back on this road at once and let them ride; they are wet with blood. Let us hurry back to our friends. I advise that in earnest."

When they came back to where the damage had been done, Hagen of Tronje said: "Heroes, you must see what we are missing here, and whom we have lost in this fight through Gelfrat's anger." They had lost four men, and they had to mourn them. But those four were well paid for: against them a hundred of the Bavarians or more lay dead. The shields of the men of Tronje were dull with it and wet with blood.

The bright moon broke partly through the clouds. Hagen spoke again: "No one is to tell my dear lords what we have done here. Let them stay free of worry until morning."

When the men who had been fighting back there caught up with the rest, the whole company was suffering badly from weariness. "How much longer must we ride?" many a man asked. Bold Dankwart said: "We can have no lodging tonight. You must all ride on until it is day." Swift Volker, who had charge of the company, had the marshal asked: "Where are we to spend the night, so that our horses can rest, and my dear lords too?"

Bold Dankwart said: "I cannot tell you that. We cannot rest until it begins to get light. Wherever we find a place then, we will lie down on the grass." When they heard that news, some of them took it hard.

No one gave them away, red with hot blood as they were, until the sun sent its bright shining out over the hills to the morning and the king saw that they had been fighting. The hero said angrily: "What is this, friend Hagen? I think you did not care to have me with you when your mail-rings got wet with blood like this. Who did it?"

He said: "Else did it. He attacked us in the night; we were set upon because of his ferryman. Then my brother Dankwart's hand struck Gelfrat down. Else got away from us after that, and he had good reason to. A hundred of theirs and four of ours were left dead in the fight."

I cannot tell you where they lay down to rest. Afterward all the people of the countryside learned that noble Ute's sons were riding to court. Later they were well received at Passau. The noble kings' uncle, Bishop Pilgrim, was very glad when his nephews came into his land with so many warriors. That he wished them well they learned very soon. Friends welcomed them along the roads, but at Passau there was no way to house them all. They had to cross the river to where they found open ground, and there huts and tents were pitched.

They had to stay there a whole day, and the full night as well, and how well they were looked after! After that they had to ride into Rüdiger's land, and the news of it reached him very soon.

When the road-weary men had taken their rest and were drawing nearer to that land, they found a man asleep on the border, and Hagen of Tronje took a strong weapon from him. The good strong knight's name was Eckewart. He was heavy-hearted about it, that he had lost his weapon through the heroes' coming. They had found Rüdiger's border badly guarded.

"Oh, the shame of this," said Eckewart. "Truly, I grieve bitterly for this journey of the Burgundians. Since I lost Siegfried, my joy has been gone. Oh, Lord Rüdiger, how badly I have served you."

Hagen heard the noble warrior's distress plainly. He gave him back his weapon, and six red-gold arm-rings besides. "Take these, hero, as a token that you are my friend. You are a bold warrior, however alone you lie out here on the border."

"God reward you for your rings," said Eckewart. "But still I grieve bitterly for your journey to the Huns. You killed Siegfried, and you are hated here. Guard yourselves well; I advise you that in all loyalty."

"Now may God protect us," said Hagen. "These warriors, the kings and their men too, have no other worry now than lodging: where in this land we are to find a night's shelter tonight. Our horses are worn out on the hard roads," said the warrior Hagen, "and our food has run out. We find nothing for sale anywhere. We need a host who, out of his own goodness, would give us his bread tonight."

Eckewart answered: "I will show you a host so good that you have seldom been lodged as well in any land as you may be here, if you swift warriors are willing to go and see Rüdiger. He lives right by the road, and he is the best host who ever kept a house. His heart brings forth good things the way sweet May brings the grass its flowers. Whenever he can serve heroes, he is glad at heart."

King Gunther said: "Will you be my messenger, and ask whether my dear friend Rüdiger will take us in for my sake, my kinsmen and our men? I will repay that with my service forever, as well as I ever can."

"I will gladly be your messenger," said Eckewart. With a very good will he set out on the road and told Rüdiger what he had heard. In many a year no news had come to him that pleased him so much.

At Pöchlarn a warrior was seen riding hard. Rüdiger himself recognized him and said: "There comes Eckewart hurrying along the road, one of Kriemhild's men." He thought enemies had done him some harm. He went out in front of the gate, where he found the messenger. Eckewart unbuckled his sword and laid it from his hand. The news he brought was not kept back; it was quickly told to the host and his kinsmen.

He said to the margrave: "Gunther, lord of the land of Burgundy, has sent me to you, and his brother Giselher, and Gernot too. Each of those warriors sends you his service here. Hagen and Volker send the same, loyally and eagerly. And I will tell you more: the king's marshal sends you word by me that the good squires are badly in need of your lodging."

Rüdiger answered with a laugh: "What good news this is for me, that the noble kings are willing to accept my service! It will not be refused them. If they come to my house, I am glad and happy."

"Dankwart the marshal asked me to let you know whom you would have to house along with them: sixty swift warriors, a thousand good knights, and nine thousand squires." At that Rüdiger was glad at heart.

"How lucky I am in these guests," said Rüdiger, "that these warriors are coming to my house, when I have had so little chance to serve them. Now ride out to meet them, kinsmen and vassals alike."

Knights and squires hurried to their horses; whatever their lord commanded seemed right to all of them, and they set about his service all the faster. And still Lady Gotelind knew nothing of it, sitting in her chamber.

Chapter 27: How Rüdiger Welcomed the Kings to His House

Then the margrave went to find the ladies, his wife and his daughter, and told them at once the very welcome news he had heard: their lady's brothers were coming to their house. "My dearest wife," said Rüdiger, "you must give the noble kings a very warm welcome when they come to court here with their retinue. And you must greet Hagen, Gunther's vassal, handsomely too. With them comes a man named Dankwart, and another named Volker, a man of fine manners. You and my daughter are to kiss these six, and treat the warriors with courtesy and kindness."

The ladies promised, and they were ready to do it. They searched the chests for the splendid clothes they would wear to go out and meet the warriors. There was a great deal of effort made by beautiful women that day. You would have found very little false color on their cheeks. On their heads they wore bright bands of gold, rich circlets, so that the wind would not tangle their beautiful hair; that is true, on my honor.

Let us leave the ladies to their busy work. There was a great hurrying across the fields by Rüdiger's kinsmen to where the princes were, and the princes were well received into the margrave's land. When the margrave saw them coming toward him, swift Rüdiger said joyfully: "Welcome, my lords, and your men too, here in my land. I am very glad to see you." The warriors bowed to him in loyalty, without a trace of ill will. He showed them plainly that he wished them well. He greeted Hagen specially, for he had known him before, and he did the same for Volker from the land of Burgundy. He welcomed Dankwart too. The bold warrior said: "Since you are taking us in, who is going to look after the retinue we have brought with us?"

The margrave said: "You shall have a good night, and so shall your whole retinue. Whatever you have brought with you into this land, horses and clothing, I will set such a guard on it that nothing is lost that could do you harm, not so much as a single spur. Pitch the tents on the field, squires. Whatever you lose here, I will make it good. Take off the bridles and let the horses run." No host had ever done that for them before.

The guests were glad of it. When all that was arranged, the lords rode on, and the squires lay down everywhere on the grass and took their ease. I think they never fared so comfortably on this whole journey.

The noble margravine had come out in front of the castle with her very beautiful daughter. Beside her stood the lovely ladies and many a beautiful girl, wearing many arm-rings and splendid clothes. The precious stones shone far out of their rich garments; they were beautiful women. Then the guests came and dismounted at once. Hey, what fine courtesy was found in those Burgundians! Thirty-six girls and many other women, their bodies made as anyone could wish, went to meet them with many a bold man. There was a beautiful greeting given by noble ladies.

The young margravine kissed all three kings, and so did her mother. Hagen was standing right there. Her father told her to kiss him; she looked at him, and he seemed so fearsome that she would gladly have left it undone. But she had to do what the host commanded. Her color came and went, pale and red. She kissed Dankwart too, and then the minstrel; that greeting was given him for his courage. The young margravine took Giselher, the warrior from the land of Burgundy, by the hand, and her mother did the same with Gunther, the bold man. They went in with the heroes in high spirits. The host walked beside Gernot into a wide hall, and knights and ladies sat down there. Good wine was quickly ordered poured for the guests. Truly, heroes could never have been better treated.

Rüdiger's daughter was looked at with loving eyes, she was so lovely. Truly, many a good knight loved her in his thoughts, and she deserved it, for she was high-spirited. They thought whatever they liked, but nothing could come of it. Back and forth went many a look at the girls and the ladies, and there were plenty of them sitting there. The noble fiddler felt kindly toward the host.

Then, as the custom was, they parted; knights and ladies went their separate ways. Tables were set up in the wide hall, and from then on the foreign guests were splendidly served. For the guests' sake the noble margravine came to table, but her daughter she left with the girls, where it was proper for her to sit. The guests could not see her, and truly that irked them.

When they had all drunk and eaten, the beautiful ladies were brought back into the hall, and no playful talk was left unsaid. Volker, a bold and cheerful warrior, spoke a great deal of it. The noble minstrel said out loud: "Mighty margrave, God has dealt very graciously with you: he has given you a wife so truly beautiful, and a delightful life along with her. If I were a prince," said the minstrel, "and was to wear a crown, I would want your beautiful daughter for my wife; that is what my heart wishes. She is lovely to look at, and noble and good besides."

The margrave said: "How could that ever be, that a king would want my dear daughter? My wife and I are exiles here. What good is great beauty to the girl?"

Gernot, the well-bred man, answered: "If I could have a wife after my own heart, I would always be glad of such a woman." And Hagen answered very kindly: "Now my lord Giselher really must take a wife. The margravine's family is so high that I and all his men would gladly serve her if she were to wear the crown in Burgundy."

That speech seemed very good to Rüdiger, and to Gotelind too; truly their hearts rejoiced. Then the heroes brought it about that noble Giselher took her as his wife, as well befitted a king. What is meant to happen, who can stand in its way? They asked the young lady to come to court. They swore to give him the delightful woman, and he in turn promised to love the lovely girl. Castles and land were assigned to the young lady; the noble king's hand swore oaths on it, and Lord Gernot's too, that it would be done.

The margrave said: "Since I have no castles, I will always be loyally devoted to you. With my daughter I give silver and gold, as much as a hundred pack-horses can carry at the most, so that the hero's kinsmen may be pleased with it, as honor requires."

Then, as the custom was, they had the two of them stand inside a ring. Many a young man stood facing them in a cheerful mood, thinking in his mind what the young still like to think. When they began to ask the lovely girl whether she would have the warrior, it was partly painful to her, and yet she meant to take the handsome man. The question embarrassed her, as it has embarrassed many a girl. Her father Rüdiger advised her to say yes and to take him gladly. In a moment noble Giselher was there and put his white arms around her, though in the end she had little joy of him.

The margrave said: "You noble and mighty kings, when you ride back home to Burgundy, as is the custom, I will give you my child so you can take her with you." They promised that. Whatever merriment there was, they had to let it go. The young lady was sent to her chamber and the guests to sleep and rest until day. Then food was prepared; the host looked after them kindly.

When they had eaten, they wanted to ride on toward the land of the Huns. "I will put a stop to that," said the noble host. "You must stay here, for I have seldom had such dear guests." Dankwart answered: "That cannot be. Where would you find the food, the bread and the wine, that you would need for so many warriors for another night?" When the host heard that, he said: "Stop that talk. My dear lords, do not refuse me. Truly, I can feed you for fourteen days, with your whole retinue that has come here with you. King Etzel has taken very little from me yet."

However hard they resisted, they had to stay until the fourth morning. In that time the host's generosity did such things that word of it traveled far: he gave his guests both horses and clothes. It could go on no longer; they had to leave. Bold Rüdiger could hold back very little, so generous was he: whatever anyone wanted to take, he refused to no one, and it pleased them all. Their noble retinue brought the many saddled horses out in front of the gate, and the foreign warriors came out to them with their shields in their hands, for they meant to ride into Etzel's land.

The host had offered his gifts to everyone before the noble guests came out in front of the hall. He knew how to live generously and with great honor. His beautiful daughter he had given to Giselher. Then he gave Gunther, that praiseworthy hero who seldom accepted any gift, a coat of armor, and the noble mighty prince wore it well, with honor. Gunther bowed over noble Rüdiger's hand. Then he gave Gernot a fine sword, which Gernot later carried splendidly in battle. The margrave's wife did not begrudge him the gift at all; and in the end good Rüdiger lost his life by it. Gotelind offered Hagen her loving gift, as was only proper for her: since the king had accepted one, Hagen too should not go from her to the festival without a present from her. Yet he declined it afterwards. "Of everything I have ever seen," said Hagen, "I would want nothing more to carry away from here than that shield there on the wall. I would gladly take that into Etzel's land."

When the margravine heard Hagen's words, it reminded her of her grief, and weeping became her. She thought with great pain of Nudung's death; Witege had killed him, and it had left her in deep sorrow. She said to the warrior: "I will give you the shield. I wish to God in heaven that the man who once carried it in his hand were still alive! He lay dead in battle. I must weep for him forever; that is the distress of a poor woman like me."

The noble margravine rose from her seat. With her white hands she took hold of the shield; the lady carried it to Hagen, and he took it in his hand. The gift was bestowed on the warrior with honor. A cover of bright silk lay over its colors. Daylight never shone on a better shield, set with precious stones; if anyone had wanted to buy it at its cost, it was well worth a thousand marks. Hagen had the shield carried away ahead of him. Then Dankwart came to court, and the margrave's child gave him rich clothes, which he later wore among the Huns in great splendor.

All of the gifts they accepted would never have come into any of their hands except out of love for the host, who offered them so handsomely. Later they became such enemies to him that they had to strike him dead.

Swift Volker went courteously with his fiddle to stand before Gotelind. He played sweet tunes and sang her his songs. With that he took his leave as he parted from Pöchlarn. The margravine had a chest brought to her; now you may hear of friendly gifts. From it she took twelve arm-rings and slid them onto his hand. "You are to take these away from here into Etzel's land, and wear them at court for my sake, so that when you come back I can be told how you served me there at the festival." What the lady asked of him, he carried out well in the end.

The host said to his guests: "You will travel all the more easily, for I will guide you myself and have you well guarded, so that no one can harm you on the road." Then his pack-horses were loaded without delay. The host was well equipped, with five hundred men, with horses and with clothes; he took them with him to the festival in great splendor. Not one of them ever came back to Pöchlarn alive.

The host parted from his home with loving kisses, and so did Giselher, as his good breeding told him to. They held beautiful women in their arms. Later many a young lady had to weep for that. Then the windows were thrown open everywhere; the host and his men were about to go to their horses. I think their hearts told them of the crushing sorrow to come, for many a lady wept there, and many a lovely girl. Plenty of them ached for their dear kinsmen, whom they never saw again at Pöchlarn. Yet the men rode off cheerfully down over the sand, downriver along the Danube and into the land of the Huns.

Then noble Rüdiger, the cheerful knight, said to the Burgundians: "The news that we are coming to the Huns must not be kept back. King Etzel has never heard anything that pleased him more." The messenger rode fast down through Austria, and people everywhere were told that the heroes were coming from Worms across the Rhine. Nothing could have pleased the king's household more. The messengers hurried on ahead with the news that the Nibelungs were in the land of the Huns. "You must welcome them well, Kriemhild, my lady. Your dear brothers are coming to you in great honor."

Lady Kriemhild stood at a window, watching for her kinsmen as friends still watch for friends. She saw many a man from her homeland. The king heard the news too, and began to laugh for joy. "Oh, how happy I am!" said Kriemhild. "Here my kinsmen bring many a new shield and white coats of mail. Whoever wants gold, let him remember my wrongs, and I will always be his friend."

Chapter 28: How the Nibelungs Came to Etzel's Castle

When the Burgundians came into Etzel's land, old Hildebrand of Bern, the Italian city we call Verona, heard of it. He told his lord, and it grieved Dietrich deeply; he asked Hildebrand to give the bold, high-spirited knights a good welcome. Swift Wolfhart had the horses brought, and many a strong warrior rode out with Dietrich to greet them on the field, where the guests had pitched many a splendid tent.

When Hagen of Tronje saw them riding toward him from far off, he said courteously to his lords: "Now, swift warriors, you should get up from your seats and go to meet these men who want to welcome you. A company is coming this way that I know well. They are swift warriors from the land of the Amelungs, and the lord of Bern leads them; they are proud men. Do not scorn whatever service they offer you."

Then many a knight and squire got down from his horse beside Dietrich, as was only right. They went over to the guests, to where the heroes were, and greeted the men from the land of Burgundy warmly.

When Lord Dietrich saw them coming toward him, he spoke to Ute's sons, and now you may like to hear what the warrior said. Their journey grieved him; he thought Rüdiger knew about the danger and had told them. "Welcome, my lords Gunther and Giselher, Gernot and Hagen, and Lord Volker too, and Dankwart the swift. Do you not know this? Kriemhild still weeps bitterly for the hero of the Nibelung land."

"She can weep a long time," said Hagen. "He has lain dead for many a year now. She should love the king of the Huns instead. Siegfried is not coming back; he was buried long ago."

"Let us leave Siegfried's wounds alone for now. As long as Lady Kriemhild is alive, harm may still be done." So spoke Dietrich, the lord of Bern. "You are the Nibelungs' mainstay; guard yourself against it."

"How am I supposed to guard myself?" said the noble king. "Etzel sent us messengers — what more should I ask? — to say we should ride here to him in this land. And my sister Kriemhild has sent us many messages too."

"I can give you good advice," said Hagen. "Ask Lord Dietrich and his good heroes to tell you the news in full, so that they let you know what Lady Kriemhild has in mind."

Then the three mighty kings went aside to talk: Gunther and Gernot, and Lord Dietrich too. "Now tell us, noble good knight of Bern, what you know of the queen's state of mind."

The lord of Bern said: "What more can I tell you? Every morning I hear Etzel's wife weeping and lamenting, in pitiful grief, to the mighty God of heaven over strong Siegfried's death."

"What we have heard cannot be changed now," said the bold man, Volker the fiddler. "We must ride to court and let it be seen what may happen to us swift warriors there among the Huns."

The bold Burgundians rode on to court. They came in splendor, after the custom of their land, and many a bold man among the Huns wondered about Hagen of Tronje, what he might look like. It was enough that the story was told — that he had killed Siegfried of the Netherlands, the strongest of all warriors, Kriemhild's husband — and so there was a great deal of asking about Hagen at court. The hero was well built, that is the plain truth: broad in the chest, his hair streaked with gray, his legs long, his gaze terrible; and he walked like a lord.

Then the Burgundian men were shown to their lodgings, and Gunther's household was quartered apart from them. The queen, who bore him so much hatred, had advised this; and that is why the squires were later killed in their lodgings.

Dankwart, Hagen's brother, was the marshal. The king earnestly put his household in his charge, to look after them well and give them plenty. The hero of Burgundy wished them all well.

Beautiful Kriemhild went with her attendants to where she received the Nibelungs with a false heart. She kissed Giselher and took him by the hand. Hagen of Tronje saw it, and he laced his helmet tighter.

"After a greeting like that," said Hagen, "swift warriors may well think again. Kings and their men are greeted differently here. We have not made a good journey to this festival."

She said: "Be welcome to anyone who is glad to see you. I do not greet you out of any friendship I feel for you myself. Tell me what you bring me from Worms across the Rhine, that you should be so very welcome to me."

"If I had known," said Hagen, "that warriors were supposed to bring you gifts — I am rich enough — I would have thought ahead and brought you my gift here to this land."

"Now you shall tell me more. The Nibelung treasure: what have you done with it? It was mine, as everyone knows. You should have brought it to me here in Etzel's land."

"Truly, my Lady Kriemhild, it is many a day since I had the keeping of the Nibelung treasure. My lords had it sunk in the Rhine, and there it must truly stay until the Last Day."

The queen said: "I thought as much. You have brought me very little of it here into this land, though it was my own and I once had it in my keeping. That gives me many a sad day, all my life."

"The devil is what I bring you," said Hagen. "I have enough to carry with my shield and my coat of mail; my helmet is so bright, and the sword in my hand — that I will not bring you."

Then the queen said to all the warriors: "No one may carry weapons into the hall. You heroes, hand them over to me; I will have them kept safe."

"Truly," said Hagen, "that will never be done. I do not want the honor, generous lady, of having you carry my shield and my other weapons to the lodgings. You are a queen. My father never taught me that; I will be my own chamberlain."

"Oh, what grief is mine," said Lady Kriemhild. "Why will my brother and Hagen not let their shields be kept? They have been warned. If I knew who did it, he would have to die."

Prince Dietrich answered her angrily: "It is I who warned the noble mighty kings and bold Hagen, the Burgundians' man. Go on, she-devil — you shall not make me pay for it."

Etzel's wife was deeply ashamed at that; she bitterly feared Dietrich. She went away from him quickly without a word, only casting fierce looks at her enemies.

Then two warriors took each other by the hand: one was Lord Dietrich, the other Hagen. The high-spirited warrior said courteously: "Your coming to the Huns truly grieves me, because of what the queen has said." Hagen of Tronje said: "It will all come right." So the two bold men talked together, and King Etzel began to ask about it.

"I would like to know," said the mighty king, "who that warrior is whom Lord Dietrich is welcoming so warmly over there. He carries himself proudly. Whoever his father may be, he could well be a good hero."

One of Kriemhild's men answered the king: "He was born at Tronje; his father's name was Aldrian. However cheerful he may act here, he is a grim man. I will let you see that I have told no lie."

"How am I to tell that he is so grim?" asked the king. At that time the king did not yet know the many evil tricks that the queen later played on her kinsmen, so that she let not one of them leave the Huns alive.

"I knew Aldrian well," Etzel went on. "He was my vassal. He won praise and great honor here with me. I made him a knight and gave him my gold. Loyal Helche was fond of him with all her heart. And that is how I know for certain who they are. Two handsome boys became my hostages, he and Walther of Spain. They grew to manhood here. Hagen I sent home again; Walther escaped with Hildegund."

He was thinking of old stories from long ago. He had clearly recognized his friend from Tronje, the man who had done him great service in his youth. Later, in Etzel's old age, Hagen killed many of his dear friends.

Chapter 29: How Hagen and Volker Sat Before Kriemhild's Hall

Then the two praiseworthy warriors, Hagen of Tronje and Lord Dietrich, went their separate ways. Gunther's vassal looked over his shoulder for a comrade-in-arms, and he found one quickly enough. He saw Volker, the skilled fiddler, standing beside Giselher, and asked him to come along, for he knew the fiddler's grim temper well. In every way Volker was a bold and good knight.

They left the lords still standing in the courtyard. Only those two were seen walking off, far across the courtyard, toward a wide palace. Those chosen warriors feared no one's hatred. They sat down on a bench in front of the building, facing a hall that belonged to Kriemhild, and their splendid clothes shone on their bodies. Plenty of the people there would gladly have known who they were. The Huns gaped at the two proud heroes as if they were wild animals. Etzel's wife saw them too, through a window, and beautiful Kriemhild was troubled all over again. It reminded her of her sorrow, and she began to weep. Etzel's men wondered greatly what had darkened her mood so suddenly. She said: "Hagen did that, you bold and good heroes."

They said to the lady: "How did that happen? Only a moment ago we saw you glad. No one has ever been so bold that he could do this to you and keep his life, if you tell us to avenge it."

"I would serve forever whoever avenged my wrongs. Whatever he asked for, I would be ready to give it. I throw myself at your feet," said the king's wife. "Avenge me on Hagen, so that he loses his life."

Then sixty bold men armed themselves at once. For Kriemhild's sake they meant to go over and kill Hagen, that very bold man, and the fiddler as well; it was all planned out. But when the queen saw how small her band was, she said to the heroes in a grim mood: "Give up what you have in mind. With so few, you can never take on Hagen. However strong and bold Hagen of Tronje may be, the man sitting beside him, Volker the fiddler, is far stronger still. He is a dangerous man. Truly, you should not take these heroes on so lightly."

When they heard that, more of them armed themselves — four hundred swift warriors. The noble queen was set on doing them harm. Later that brought great trouble on many warriors. When she saw her men well armed, the queen said to the swift warriors: "Now wait a while; you must stand quite still. I will go to my enemies wearing my crown, and hear me charge Hagen of Tronje, Gunther's vassal, with what he has done to me. I know he is so arrogant that he will not deny a word of it. And I do not care what happens to him for it."

Then the fiddler, that bold minstrel, saw the noble queen coming down a flight of steps from a building. When bold Volker saw that, he said to his comrade-in-arms: "Now look, friend Hagen, there she comes, the woman who invited us into this land without a trace of loyalty. I never saw so many men with swords in their hands walking so ready for a fight beside any king's wife. Do you know, friend Hagen, whether she hates you? If she does, I advise you to guard your life and your honor all the more carefully. Truly, that seems wise to me. As far as I can tell, they are in an angry mood, and some of them are so broad in the chest that anyone who wants to protect himself had better do it in good time. I think they are wearing bright coats of mail underneath. Who they mean it for, I cannot tell you."

Then bold Hagen said angrily: "I know very well that all this is aimed at me, that they carry those bright weapons in their hands. For all they could do, I could still ride home to the land of Burgundy. Now tell me, friend Volker, will you stand by me if Kriemhild's men want to fight me? Let me hear it, as you love me. I will stand by you forever in loyal service."

"I will help you, you may be sure," said the minstrel. "If I saw the king himself coming at us with all his warriors, as long as I live I would not yield one foot from your side out of fear."

"May God in heaven reward you, noble Volker. If they fight me, what more do I need? Since you will help me, as I have just heard, let these warriors come on, fully armed."

"Now let us rise from our seats," said the minstrel. "She is a queen; let her pass by, and show her honor. She is a noble woman, and it will do each of us honor too."

"No — for my sake, no," said Hagen. "These warriors would think I did it out of fear if I went away from here. I will never rise from this seat for any one of them. Truly, it suits us both better to leave it alone. Why should I honor someone who hates me? I will never do that as long as I live. And I do not care how much King Etzel's wife hates me."

Arrogant Hagen laid a gleaming sword across his knees. In its pommel shone a bright jasper, greener than grass. Kriemhild knew it well: it was Siegfried's. When she recognized the sword, grief overcame her. Its hilt was gold and its scabbard bound with a red braid. It reminded her of her sorrow, and she began to weep. I think bold Hagen had done it for exactly that reason.

Swift Volker drew a strong fiddle bow closer to him on the bench, huge and long, very like a sword, sharp and broad. And there the two proud warriors sat without fear. The two bold men thought so much of themselves that they would not rise from their seat for fear of anyone. So the noble queen walked right up to their feet, and gave them a hostile greeting.

She said: "Tell me, Hagen, who sent for you, that you dared to ride here into this land when you know very well what you have done to me? If you had any sense, you would rightly have stayed away."

"No one sent for me," said Hagen. "Three warriors were invited into this land. They are called my lords, and I am their vassal. I have seldom stayed behind when they rode to any court."

She said: "Now tell me more. Why did you do what earned you my hatred? You killed Siegfried, my dear husband. I will have cause enough to weep for that until the day I die."

He said: "What more is there to say? Enough has been said. Yes, I am Hagen, who killed Siegfried, that hero with his hands. How dearly he paid for it that beautiful Kriemhild insulted Lady Brunhild. There is no denying it, mighty queen. All the guilt for that ruinous harm is mine. Let whoever wants to avenge it, woman or man. I would be lying if I said anything else: I have done you great wrong."

She said: "Now listen, warriors, how he denies not one of my wrongs. Whatever happens to him for it, men of Etzel, I do not care." The arrogant warriors looked at one another. If anyone had started the fight there, it would have ended with the honor going to those two comrades, for they had proved themselves in battle many times. What the Huns had boasted they would do, they had to leave undone, out of fear.

One of the warriors said: "Why are you looking at me? What I promised before, I take back. I will not lose my life for anyone's reward. King Etzel's wife is trying to lead us to ruin."

Another beside him said: "I feel the same. If someone gave me towers of good red gold, I would not take on this fiddler, not after the fierce looks I have seen in his eyes. And I have known Hagen since his young days; no one needs to tell me much about that warrior. I have seen him in twenty-two battles, where many a woman came to heartbreak. He and the man from Spain dealt many a thrust when they fought all those battles here with Etzel, for the king's honor. That happened often, and so Hagen rightly deserves honor. In those days the warrior was still a boy in years. How gray they are now, the ones who were young then! Now he has come to his full wits, and he is a grim man. And he carries Balmung, which he won by evil means."

So the matter was settled: no one fought. That grieved the queen to the heart. The heroes turned away; truly, they feared death at the fiddler's hands, and they had good reason to.

Then the fiddler said: "We have seen plainly that we shall find enemies here, as we heard said before. Let us go to court, to the kings; then no one will dare attack our lords. How often a man leaves a thing undone out of fear — when friend stands by friend as a friend should, and he has the good sense not to do it. Many a man's harm is well warded off by good sense."

"Now I will follow you," said Hagen. They went to where they found the splendid warriors standing in the courtyard, surrounded by a great crowd of welcomers. Bold Volker began to speak loudly to his lords: "How long are you going to stand here and let yourselves be jostled? You should go to court and hear from the king what mood he is in." Then the bold and good heroes were seen pairing off.

The prince of Bern took mighty Gunther of Burgundy by the hand. Irnfried took bold Gernot, and Rüdiger was seen walking to court with Giselher. But however anyone else paired off and went to court, Volker and Hagen never parted, except in one battle, at the hour of their death. Later noble women had to weep bitterly for that.

With the kings a thousand bold men of their noble household were seen going to court, and sixty warriors besides who had come with them; bold Hagen had brought those from his own land. Hawart and Iring, two chosen men, were seen walking in company beside the kings. Dankwart and Wolfhart, a splendid warrior, were seen showing their good breeding before all the others.

When the lord of the Rhine entered the great hall, mighty Etzel waited no longer. He sprang from his seat when he saw him coming. No king ever gave so fine a greeting. "Welcome, Lord Gunther, and Lord Gernot too, and your brother Giselher. I sent you my service, loyally and eagerly, to Worms across the Rhine, and all your household shall be welcome to me. Now be very welcome, you two warriors, Volker the bold and Hagen too, to me and to my lady here in this land. She sent you many a messenger to the Rhine."

Hagen of Tronje said: "I have heard a great deal about that. If I had not come to the Huns for my lords' sake, I would have ridden into this land in your honor." Then the noble host took his dear guests by the hand and led them to the seat where he himself sat. Drink was poured for the guests, and poured generously, in wide golden bowls: mead, wine, and morat, the mulberry drink. And the strangers so far from home were bidden a hearty welcome.

King Etzel said: "I will tell you this: nothing in the whole world could please me more than that you heroes have come to me. It has taken a great sorrow off the queen. I wonder what I ever did to you — I have won so many noble guests — that you never cared to come into my lands. Now that I have seen you, it is turned to joy for me."

Rüdiger, a knight of high spirit, answered: "You may well be glad to see them. Their loyalty is good; my lady's kinsmen know how to keep it well. They bring many a handsome warrior to your house."

The lords had come to mighty Etzel's court on midsummer's eve. Seldom has anyone heard of so high a greeting as the one he gave the heroes. Now it was time to eat, and the host went with them to the table. A host never sat more handsomely among his guests. They were given food and drink in plenty. Whatever they wanted was ready for them, for great wonders had been told about those heroes.

Chapter 30: How the Kings Went to Bed, and What Happened in the Night

The day was over now and night was coming on. The travel-weary warriors had one worry on their minds: when they could rest and go to their beds. Hagen raised the matter, and they were soon given their answer.

Gunther said to the host: "God keep you well. We want to go to sleep now; please give us your leave. Whenever you command it, we will come back early tomorrow." Etzel parted from his guests in the best of spirits.

Everywhere you looked, the guests were being jostled. Bold Volker said to the Huns: "How dare you get under these warriors' feet? If you do not stop, you will get hurt. I will give one of you such a heavy stroke of my fiddle bow that if he has a loyal friend anywhere, that friend will have cause to weep for it. Why not get out of our way, warriors? That seems the wise thing to me. You are all called warriors, but you are not all of the same mettle."

When the fiddler spoke so angrily, bold Hagen looked back over his shoulder. "The bold minstrel is giving you good advice," he said. "Go to your lodgings, you heroes of Kriemhild. What you have in mind, I do not think any of you will do. If you want to start something, come to us early tomorrow, and tonight let us strangers have our rest. I do not think heroes ever went about things with intentions like yours."

Then the guests were brought into a wide hall. They found it made ready for the warriors from end to end with rich beds, long and broad. Lady Kriemhild was plotting the very worst of harm against them. You could see many a finely worked quilt from Arras there, of bright silk, and many a bedspread of Arabian silk, the best there could be, with borders on them that shone splendidly. Many coverlets of ermine and of black sable could be seen, and under them the men were to take their rest that night until the bright day came. No king with his household ever lay down so splendidly.

"Alas for this night's lodging," said young Giselher, "and alas for my kinsmen who have come here with us. However kindly my sister has received us, I am afraid that through her we shall all lie dead."

"Put your worries aside now," said Hagen the warrior. "I will keep the shield-watch myself tonight. I am confident I can guard us well until day comes. Have no fear on that score; after that, let whoever can save himself."

They all bowed to him and thanked him for it. Then they went to their beds, and it was not long before the handsome men had lain down. Bold Hagen, the hero, began to arm himself.

Then Volker the fiddler, the warrior, said: "If it is not beneath you, Hagen, I would like to keep the shield-watch with you tonight, until early morning." The hero thanked Volker warmly. "God in heaven reward you, dear Volker. In all my troubles I would want no one but you alone, wherever I was in peril. I will repay you well for it, unless death prevents me."

Then the two of them put on their bright armor. Each took his shield in his hand, and they went out of the house and stood before the door. There they kept watch over the guests; that was done out of loyalty. Swift Volker laid his good shield down against the wall of the hall. Then he went back inside and fetched his fiddle, and served his friends as well suited the hero. He sat down on the stone step in the doorway of the house. A bolder fiddler there never was. When the music of his strings rang out so sweetly, the proud warriors, so far from home, thanked Volker for it.

His strings rang until the whole house echoed with them; his strength and his skill were both great. Then he played sweeter and sweeter, and lulled many an anxious man to sleep in his bed. When they had fallen asleep and he could see it, the warrior took up his shield again in his hand, went back to the room and stood in front of the door, and guarded the men far from home against Kriemhild's men.

Toward the middle of the night — I do not know, it may have been earlier — bold Volker saw a helmet gleam far off in the dark. Kriemhild's men would gladly have done the guests harm. The fiddler said: "Hagen, my friend, this is a worry we should carry together. I see armed men standing near the house, and if I am not mistaken, I think they mean to attack us."

"Then keep quiet," said Hagen, "and let them come closer to us before they notice us. Then helmets will be knocked askew by the swords in our two hands, and they will be sent back to Kriemhild in a sorry state."

One of the Huns' warriors saw very quickly that the door was guarded. How fast he spoke then: "What we had in mind cannot be done. I see the fiddler standing on the shield-watch. He wears a gleaming helmet on his head, bright and hard, strong and whole, and the rings of his mail shine like fire. Hagen is standing beside him. The guests are well guarded."

They turned back at once. When Volker saw it, he spoke angrily to his companion: "Now let me go out to those warriors in front of the house. I want to ask Lady Kriemhild's men a question or two."

"No, for my sake," said Hagen. "If you leave the house, those swift warriors will very likely bring you into such peril with their swords that I would have to help you, even if it meant the death of all my kinsmen. And once the two of us were caught up in the fight, two or four of them would run into the house in no time and do such harm to the sleeping warriors that we could never grieve for it enough."

Volker answered: "Then at least let us make sure they know I have seen them, so that Kriemhild's men cannot deny they would gladly have done a treacherous thing." And at once Volker called out to them: "Why are you walking about in armor like that, you swift warriors? Are you riding out to rob somebody, you men of Kriemhild? Then you should take me and my comrade along to help."

No one answered him. He was furious. "Shame on you, you miserable cowards!" said the good hero. "Did you mean to murder us in our sleep? That has very seldom been done to heroes as good as these."

The queen was told exactly how her messengers had come back with nothing done. She had good reason to be sorry, and she arranged it another way, for her mood was grim. Because of that, bold good heroes would die in the end.

Chapter 31: How the Lords Went to Church

"My coat of mail is growing cold," said Volker. "I think the night is almost over. I can feel it in the air: it will soon be day." Then they woke the many men who still lay sleeping.

The bright morning shone into the hall on the guests. Hagen went about waking the knights, asking whether they wanted to go to the cathedral for Mass. The bells were already ringing, in the Christian way. Christians and pagans sang differently there, and that showed clearly enough: they were not of one mind. Gunther's men wanted to go to church, and they had all risen from their beds together.

The warriors laced themselves into such fine clothes that no heroes ever brought better ones into any king's land. That troubled Hagen. He said: "Heroes, you ought to be wearing other clothes here. You know the news well enough by now. Instead of roses, carry weapons in your hands; instead of jeweled garlands, your bright good helmets, since we know only too well what wicked Kriemhild has in mind. We will have to fight today, I tell you. Instead of silk shirts, wear your coats of mail; instead of rich cloaks, your good broad shields, so that if anyone turns on you in anger, you are fully armed.

"My dear lords, and my kinsmen and vassals, go to church of your own free will, and lay your worry and your peril before almighty God; and know for certain that death is drawing near us. Do not forget whatever you have done, either, and stand before God with all your heart. I warn you of this, noble warriors: unless God in heaven wills otherwise, you will never hear Mass again."

So the princes and their men went to the cathedral. In the holy churchyard bold Hagen made them halt, so that they would not get separated. He said: "No one knows yet what will happen to us at the hands of the Huns. Set your shields down in front of your feet, my friends, and if anyone offers you a cold greeting, pay him back with deep and deadly wounds. That is Hagen's advice: that you are found standing as your honor demands."

Volker and Hagen, the two of them, went and stood in front of the wide cathedral door. They did it so that the king's wife would have to push past them; truly, she was in a grim mood. Then the lord of the land came, and his beautiful wife with him, her body decked in rich clothes, and many swift warriors could be seen walking with her. You could see the dust rising high from Kriemhild's company.

When the mighty king saw the kings and their followers armed like that, how quickly he spoke: "Why do I see my friends going about in helmets? On my loyalty, it grieves me if anyone has done them any harm. I will gladly make it good, however they think best. If anyone has weighed down their hearts or their spirits, I will show them plainly that it grieves me very much. Whatever they ask of me, I am ready to do it all."

Hagen answered: "No one has done us any harm. It is my lords' custom to go armed at every festival for three full days. If anyone did anything to us here, we would tell Etzel."

Kriemhild heard very well what Hagen said. How full of hate was the look she gave him from under her brows! But she would not reveal the custom of her own land, though she had known it long enough in Burgundy. However grim and strong her hatred of them, if anyone had told Etzel the truth of the matter, he would surely have prevented what happened there later. Out of their great arrogance, not one of them told him.

A great crowd went past with the queen, but those two would not step aside for them by so much as two hands' breadth. That annoyed the Huns: they had to shove their way past the proud heroes. Etzel's chamberlains did not like it at all. They would have liked to pick a quarrel with the warriors, but they did not dare in front of the noble king. So there was a great deal of shoving, and nothing more.

When they had served God and were ready to leave, many a Hun hurried to his horse. Many beautiful girls stood beside Kriemhild, and a good seven thousand warriors rode with the queen. Kriemhild sat down at the windows with her ladies, beside mighty Etzel; he was glad of that. They wanted to watch the proud heroes ride. Hey, what a crowd of foreign warriors rode in the courtyard before them!

The marshal had come too, with the squires: bold Dankwart had brought his lord's household with him from the land of Burgundy. The horses were found well saddled for the bold Nibelungs. When the kings and their men had reached their horses, strong Volker advised them to ride a buhurt after the custom of their own land. Later the heroes rode it magnificently. They did not mind at all what the hero had advised. The buhurt and the noise of it both grew loud; many a man came out into the wide courtyard, and Etzel and Kriemhild watched it themselves.

Six hundred of Dietrich's warriors rode into the buhurt to face the guests. They wanted some sport with the Burgundians, and if he had allowed it they would gladly have had it. Hey, what good warriors came riding after them! But Lord Dietrich was told of it, and he forbade them the game with Gunther's men. He was afraid for his men, and he had good reason to be.

When the men of Bern had ridden off, Rüdiger's men came from Pöchlarn, five hundred of them under their shields, riding in front of the hall. The margrave would have been glad if they had let it be. He rode to them wisely through the throng and told his warriors to be aware that Gunther's men were in a dangerous temper; if they left the buhurt alone, he would take it kindly.

When those proud heroes had parted from them, the men of Thuringia came, as we have been told, and the men of Denmark, a good thousand bold men. You could see the splinters of broken lances flying from the thrusts. Irnfried and Hawart rode into the buhurt. The men from the Rhine were waiting for them proudly, and they offered the Thuringians many a joust. Many a lordly shield-rim was pierced through by their thrusts.

Then Lord Blödel came with three thousand. Etzel and Kriemhild watched him closely, for the knightly sport was taking place right in front of them both. The queen watched it gladly, hoping for harm to the Burgundians. Schrutan and Gibeche, Ramung and Hornboge rode into the buhurt in the Hunnish style. They held their ground against the heroes from the land of Burgundy, and the lance-shafts whirled high over the wall of the king's hall.

Whatever anyone did there, it was nothing but noise. You could hear the great hall and the palace ringing loudly with the shields Gunther's men struck. His household won praise there with great honor. Their sport was so hard and so long that the white sweat ran out from under the trappings of the good horses the heroes rode. They tried their luck against the Huns in a very high-handed way.

Then the bold warrior Volker the minstrel said: "I do not think these warriors dare to stand against us. I have always heard it said that they hate us; truly, it could never come about better for them than now. Have the horses led to our lodgings," Volker went on, "and let us ride again toward evening, when the time comes. Perhaps the queen will give the prize to the Burgundians."

Then they saw a knight riding up so showily that none of all the Huns could match him. He surely must have had a sweetheart at the time; he was dressed as finely as a noble knight's bride. Volker said again: "How could I let that pass? That ladies' darling is going to get a knock. No one can stop it: it will cost him his life. I do not care if King Etzel's wife is angry about it."

"No, for my sake," the king said at once. "People will blame us if we attack them. Let the Huns begin it; that will suit us far better." King Etzel was still sitting beside the queen.

"I will add to the buhurt," said Hagen. "Let the ladies and the warriors see how we can ride; that is a good thing to do. They will give no prize to King Gunther's men anyway."

Swift Volker rode back into the buhurt. Later that brought many a woman great grief: he drove his spear through the rich Hun's body. Both maid and wife were seen weeping over it later. Hagen and his men, sixty of his warriors, came riding hard after the fiddler, to where the game was being played. Etzel and Kriemhild saw it quite plainly. The three kings would not leave their minstrel unguarded among the enemy. A thousand heroes rode there with great force; they did what they wanted, in their high-handed way.

When the rich Hun was struck dead, you could hear his kinsmen shouting and lamenting. The whole household cried out, "Who did this?" and the answer came: "The fiddler did it, Volker, the bold minstrel." The margrave's kinsmen from the land of the Huns called at once for swords and shields; they wanted to strike Volker dead. The host came hurrying down from a window as fast as he could.

An uproar rose from the people on every side. The kings and their followers dismounted in front of the hall, and the Burgundians pushed their horses to the rear. Then King Etzel came, and the lord set about parting them. He wrenched a strong weapon out of the hand of one of the Hun's kinsmen whom he found near him, and drove them all back, for he was very angry. "How I would have wasted all my service on these heroes if you killed this minstrel here beside me," said King Etzel. "That would be a crime. I saw very well how he rode when he ran the Hun through: it happened by a stumble, through no fault of his. You must leave my guests in peace." So he became their escort. The horses were led away to the lodgings; they had many squires there, ready and eager for every kind of service.

The host went into the great hall with his friends, and he let no more anger break out there. Then the tables were set and water was brought for them. The men from the Rhine had plenty of strong enemies in that room.

It was a long while before the lords sat down. Kriemhild's worry was pressing her too hard. She said: "Prince of Bern, I come to you for advice, for help and favor. My affairs stand in a fearful state."

Hildebrand, that praiseworthy warrior, answered her: "Whoever strikes at the Nibelungs does it without me, whatever treasure he is offered for it. He may come to regret it: those swift proud knights are still unbeaten."

Then Lord Dietrich said, with his courteous manners: "Let that request be, mighty queen. Your kinsmen have done me no such wrong that I should want to fight those bold warriors. The request does you little honor, noble prince's wife, that you plot against your own kinsmen's lives. They came into this land trusting in good faith. Siegfried will not be avenged by Dietrich's hand."

When she found no treachery in the man of Bern, she quickly promised into Blödel's hand a rich march that Nudung had once held. Later Dankwart killed him, so that he quite forgot the gift. She said: "You must help me, Lord Blödel. My enemies are in this house, the men who killed Siegfried, my dear husband. Whoever helps me avenge that, I will be in his debt forever."

Blödel answered her: "Lady, you should know that I dare not advise any hatred against them, for fear of Etzel. He is very glad to see your kinsmen here. If I did them any harm, the king would never forgive me."

"Not so, Lord Blödel; I will always be your friend. I will give you silver and gold as your reward, and a beautiful girl, Nudung's bride, so that you can take your pleasure in that lovely body of hers. The land and the castles, I will give you all of it, so that you can live in joy forever, noble knight, if you win the march where Nudung sat. Whatever I promise you today, I will keep in loyalty."

When Lord Blödel heard the reward, and that the lady would suit him well for her beauty, he thought to serve the lovely woman by fighting. For that the warrior had to lose his life. He said to the queen: "Go back into the hall. Before anyone notices, I will raise an uproar. Hagen shall pay for what he did to you; I will hand King Gunther's man over to you bound."

"Now arm yourselves," said Blödel, "all my men. We are going to the enemy in their lodgings. Etzel's wife will not let me out of this, and so we heroes must all put our lives at stake."

When the queen had left Blödel set on fighting, she went to the table with King Etzel and his men. She had laid savage plans against the guests.

Since the fight could be started in no other way — Kriemhild's old grief lay buried in her heart — she had Etzel's son carried to the table. How could a woman ever do anything more terrible for revenge? Etzel's men went at once and carried Ortlieb, the young king, to the princes' table, where Hagen was sitting too. Because of that the child had to die, through Hagen's murderous hatred.

When the mighty king saw his son, he spoke kindly to his wife's kinsmen: "Now look, my friends: this is my only son, and your sister's too. That may be a good thing for all of you. If he takes after his family, he will grow into a bold man, rich and very noble, strong and handsome. If I live a while yet, I will give him twelve lands; then noble Ortlieb's hand may serve you well. So I ask you gladly, my dear friends: when you ride home to your lands on the Rhine, take your sister's son with you, and treat the child with great kindness, and bring him up in honor until he becomes a man. If anyone in your lands has done you any wrong, he will help you avenge it, once he is grown." Kriemhild, King Etzel's wife, heard those words too.

"These warriors might well put their trust in him," said Hagen, "if he grew to be a man. But the young king looks so marked for death that you will seldom see me going to court to wait on Ortlieb."

The king looked at Hagen; the words grieved him. Though the noble prince said nothing about it, it troubled his heart and weighed on his spirit. Hagen was in no mood for amusement. It pained all the princes, and the king with them, that Hagen had spoken that way about his child. That they had to put up with it sat badly with them. They did not yet know what would happen to them later through that warrior.

Chapter 32: How Blödel Was Killed

Blödel's warriors were all ready. A thousand coats of mail strong, they made their way to where Dankwart sat at table with the squires. There the deadliest hatred of all broke out between heroes. When Lord Blödel walked up to the tables, Dankwart the marshal greeted him with every courtesy: "Welcome to this house, my lord Blödel. Truly, your coming surprises me. What is this about?"

"You need not greet me," said Blödel, "for my coming here means your end. Because of your brother Hagen, who killed Siegfried, you will pay for it here among the Huns, and plenty of other warriors with you."

"No, Lord Blödel," said Dankwart. "If that is so, we may soon regret this journey to court. I was a small child when Siegfried lost his life. I do not know what King Etzel's wife holds against me."

"I have nothing more to tell you about it. Your kinsmen Gunther and Hagen did the deed. Now defend yourselves, you men far from home; you cannot survive. Your lives are the forfeit Kriemhild demands."

"Since you will not turn back," said Dankwart, "I am sorry I pleaded with you. I should have saved my breath." The swift, bold warrior sprang up from the table and drew a sharp weapon, big and long. He struck Blödel a swift sword-stroke, and in a moment Blödel's head lay at his feet. "Let that be your morning gift," said Dankwart the warrior, "for Nudung's bride, the one you wanted to take in love. Tomorrow they can marry her to some other man, and if he wants the bride-price, he will get the same." A very loyal Hun had told him what great wrongs the queen was plotting against them.

When Blödel's men saw their lord lying dead, they would not stand for it from the guests any longer. With raised swords they rushed at the young men in a grim fury. Many of them came to regret it later.

Dankwart shouted to the whole company: "You can see, noble squires, how this is going to go. Now defend yourselves, you men far from home! Truly, we have no choice, however kindly noble Kriemhild invited us here."

Those who had no swords reached down in front of the benches and lifted many a long footstool by its legs. The Burgundian squires were not going to put up with it, and with the heavy stools they beat many a dent through a helmet. How fiercely those young strangers defended themselves! At last they drove the armed men out of the house, but five hundred of them or more stayed behind inside, dead. The company was red and wet with blood.

The grim news was carried to Etzel's warriors. It was bitter grief to them that Blödel and his men had been killed, and that Hagen's brother and the squires had done it. Before the queen even heard of it, the Huns in their hatred armed two thousand men or more. They went for the squires — there was no stopping it now — and left not one of that company alive.

The treacherous Huns brought a great army up before the house. The squires, far from home, stood their ground well; but what good was their quick courage? They had to lie dead. And within a short while after that, a terrible calamity began. Here you can hear wonders told of a monstrous thing: nine thousand squires lay dead, and with them twelve knights, Dankwart's own men. He was seen standing all alone among the enemy.

The uproar had died down, the din had gone quiet, when Dankwart the warrior looked back over his shoulder. "Alas for the friends I have lost," he said. "Now, to my grief, I must stand alone among my enemies."

The swords fell thick and fast on his one body; later many a hero's wife would weep for it. He raised his shield higher and lowered the strap, and he made many a mail-ring run wet with blood. "Oh, the sorrow of it," said Aldrian's son. "Now give way, you Hunnish warriors. Let me out into the wind, so the air can cool a man worn out with fighting." And then they saw the warrior walk out, every inch a lord.

As the battle-weary man sprang out of the house, how many fresh swords rang on his helmet! Those who had not seen what wonders his hand could do came rushing at the man from Burgundy. "I wish to God," said Dankwart, "that I had a messenger who could let my brother Hagen know that I am standing in such peril in front of these warriors. He would get me out of here, or lie dead beside me."

The Hunnish warriors said: "You will have to be the messenger yourself, when we carry you dead to your brother. Then Gunther's vassal will know grief for the first time. You have done King Etzel great harm here."

"Stop your threatening," he said, "and stand farther back. I will make the mail wet on a few more of you yet. I will carry the news to court myself, and I will tell my lords my great trouble."

He made himself so feared among Etzel's men that they dared not face him with swords. Instead they shot so many spears into his shield that for sheer weight he had to let it fall from his hand. Now that he carried no shield, they thought they would overpower him. Hey, what deep wounds he struck through their helmets! Many a bold man had to stagger before him, and strong Dankwart won great praise for it.

They sprang at him from both sides, and truly, every one of them came into that fight too soon. He went before his enemies like a wild boar heading for the woods in front of the hounds. How could a man be braver? His path was wet all over again with hot blood. No single warrior could ever have fought better against his enemies than he did. And so they saw Hagen's brother walk to court like a lord.

The stewards and cupbearers heard the clang of swords. Many of them flung down the drink from their hands, and the dishes they were carrying to court. In front of the stairs he met plenty of strong enemies. "What is this, you stewards?" said the weary warrior. "You should be looking after the guests, and carrying good food to the lords, and letting me tell my lords the news as it really is."

Whoever was brave enough to spring at him on the stairs, he dealt some of them such a grim sword-swing that out of fear they had to back up higher. His great courage had worked great wonders that day.

Chapter 33: How the Burgundians Fought the Huns

When bold Dankwart came in through the door, he told Etzel's household to step back out of his way. All his clothing was running with blood, and he carried a mighty weapon bare in his hand. Dankwart shouted to the warrior: "You have been sitting here far too long, brother Hagen. To you and to God in heaven I cry out our distress: the knights and the squires lie dead in their lodgings."

Hagen called back to him: "Who did this?"

"Lord Blödel did it, he and his men. And he has paid dearly for it, I can tell you that: with my own hands I struck his head off."

"That is small harm," said Hagen, "when the story told of a warrior is that he lost his life at a warrior's hands. Lovely women will have all the less cause to mourn him. Now tell me, brother Dankwart, why are you so red? I think your wounds are giving you great trouble. If the man who did this to you is anywhere in this land, it will cost him his life, unless the foul devil saves him."

"You see me unhurt. My clothes are wet with blood, but it is other men's wounds that did that — I have killed so many of them today that if I had to swear to the number, I could never tell it."

"Brother Dankwart," he said, "then guard the door for us, and do not let a single Hun out through it. I want to speak with these warriors, since our need forces us to. Our household lies dead in front of them, and they never deserved it."

"If I am to be the chamberlain," said the bold man, "I know how to serve such mighty kings. I will keep the stairs as my honor requires." Nothing could have been less welcome to Kriemhild's warriors.

"I wonder very much," Hagen went on, "what the Hun warriors in here are whispering about. I think they would gladly do without the man standing at that door — the one who has just brought the Burgundians the news from court. For a long time now I have heard it said of Kriemhild that she would not let her heartbreak go unavenged. Let us drink to the dead, and pay for the king's wine! The young lord of the Huns shall be the first."

Then Hagen, that good hero, struck the child Ortlieb, so that the blood ran down the sword to his hand and the head sprang into the queen's lap. At that a murderous slaughter broke out among the warriors, grim and great. Next, with both hands, he dealt the tutor who had charge of the child a swift blow, so that his head quickly lay on the floor in front of the table. It was a wretched reward he paid the tutor. He saw a minstrel sitting at Etzel's table. Hagen in his rage went straight for him and struck off his right hand on the fiddle. "Take that as your message to the land of the Burgundians."

"Oh, my hand!" said Werbel the minstrel. "Sir Hagen of Tronje, what did I ever do to you? I came in good faith to your lords' land. How am I to play the tunes now that I have lost my hand?"

Hagen cared little whether he ever fiddled again. Then he dealt out murderous wounds in that hall to Etzel's warriors, and killed a great many of them; he brought people enough in that house to their deaths. Volker the swift sprang up from the table; his fiddle bow rang out loud in his hand. Gunther's minstrel played roughly. Ha, what enemies he made himself among the bold Huns!

The three noble kings sprang up from the tables too. They would gladly have stopped the fight before more harm was done, but with all their wits they could not prevent it, once Volker and Hagen had begun to rage so terribly. When the lord of the Rhine saw that the fight could not be parted, the prince himself cut many a wide wound through his enemies' bright mail rings. He was a hero with his hands, and he made that very plain. Strong Gernot came into the fight as well, and truly he struck many a Hun hero dead with a sharp sword that Rüdiger had given him. He did Etzel's warriors terrible harm. Lady Ute's young son sprang into the fight; his weapon rang splendidly through the helmets of Etzel's warriors from the land of the Huns. Bold Giselher's hand did great wonders there. Brave as they all were, the kings and all their men, still it was Giselher you saw standing in front of them all against the enemy. He was a good hero: with the wounds he gave, he made many a man fall down in the blood.

Etzel's men defended themselves hard too. But you could see the guests go hewing their way with their bright swords through the king's hall, and on every side you could hear the great clash of weapons. Those outside wanted to be in there with their kinsmen, and at the tower doorway they got very little for their trouble; those inside would gladly have been out of the hall, but Dankwart let none of them up the stairs or down them. So a mighty press grew up in front of the tower, and a great ringing of helmets under the swords. Bold Dankwart came into great peril from it, and his brother worried for him, as his loyalty required.

Hagen called loudly to Volker: "Do you see my brother standing over there, comrade, in front of the Hun warriors under heavy blows? Friend, save my brother for me, before we lose the warrior."

"I will, for certain," said the minstrel, and he went fiddling his way through the great hall, a hard sword ringing often in his hand. The warriors from the Rhine thanked him warmly. Bold Volker said to Dankwart: "You have had a very hard time of it today. Your brother asked me to come to your help. If you will hold the outside, I will stand inside."

Dankwart the swift stood outside the door and defended the stairs against anyone who came up them; you could hear the weapons ring in the heroes' hands. Inside, Volker of the land of Burgundy did the same. The bold fiddler shouted over the crowd: "The hall is well locked, friend Hagen. Truly, Etzel's door is barred by two heroes' hands as well as a thousand bolts could bar it."

When Hagen of Tronje saw the door so well guarded, the famous good hero slung his shield onto his back. Only now did he begin to avenge what had been done to him. His enemies had no hope of any kind of coming out alive.

When the lord of Bern saw clearly that strong Hagen was breaking so many helmets, the king of the Amelungs sprang up on a bench and said: "Hagen is pouring the very worst drink of all here."

The host was in great fear, as he had good reason to be, with so many dear friends being snatched from him before his eyes; he himself barely got away from his enemies alive. He sat there in dread. What good did it do him that he was king?

Mighty Kriemhild cried out to Dietrich: "Help me, noble knight, help me get out of here alive, by the virtue of all the princes of the Amelung land! If Hagen reaches me, I am as good as dead."

"How am I to help you, noble queen?" said Lord Dietrich. "Now I am afraid for myself. Gunther's men are so bitterly enraged that at this moment I cannot protect anyone."

"No, no, Lord Dietrich, noble good knight! Show your knightly spirit today and help me out of here, or I shall be left here dead." Kriemhild's fear was driving her very hard.

"I will try, and see if I can help you. For in many a year I have never seen so many good knights so bitterly enraged." Truly, he saw the blood spring from the swords through the helmets.

The chosen warrior began to shout with all his strength, so that his voice rang out like a bison's horn and the castle far and wide resounded with the force of it. Dietrich's strength was beyond all measure.

Gunther heard the man shouting in the hard fighting, and he began to listen. He said: "Dietrich's voice has reached my ears. I think our warriors have taken someone from him here. I see him up on the table; he is waving with his hand. You friends and kinsmen from the land of Burgundy, hold off from the fighting! Let us hear and see what my men have done to the warrior."

When King Gunther asked it and commanded it, they held their swords back in the heat of the fight. That was power indeed, that no one struck a blow there. Then he asked the man from Bern for his news quickly enough. He said: "Most noble Dietrich, what has been done to you here by my kinsmen? I am willing to make amends to you, and I am ready for peace. Whatever anyone may have done to you, it would grieve me to the heart."

Then Lord Dietrich said: "Nothing has been done to me. Let me go out of this house under your safe conduct, away from this hard fight, with my household. I will serve you forever for it, I promise you."

"Why are you running away so soon?" said Wolfhart. "Truly, the fiddler has not barred the door so tight that we cannot open it wide enough to get out through it."

"Hold your tongue," said Lord Dietrich. "You have done the devil's own work."

Then King Gunther said: "I will allow it. Lead out of this house whoever you like, few or many — all but my enemies. They must stay here. They have done me such bitter wrong here among the Huns."

When Dietrich heard that, he put his arm around the noble queen, whose fear was great. With his other arm he led Etzel out with him, and six hundred fine men went out with Dietrich too.

Then the margrave, noble Rüdiger, said: "If any more of those who would gladly serve you are to come out of this house, let us hear it. Then a lasting peace will stand between good friends, as it should."

Giselher of the land of Burgundy answered him: "Peace and friendship you shall have from us, since you are steadfast in your loyalty, you and your men. You may go out of here with your kinsmen without any fear."

When Lord Rüdiger left the hall, five hundred men or more followed him out, kinsmen and vassals of the lord of Pöchlarn. Later Rüdiger suffered great harm from the king.

Then a Hun warrior saw Etzel walking close beside Dietrich, and wanted to join them. The fiddler gave him such a blow that his head soon lay at Etzel's feet.

When the lord of the land had come out of the house, he turned around and looked at Volker. "Oh, these guests of mine! This is a grim calamity, that all my warriors are to die before them. Oh, this festival!" the noble king said. "There is one fighting in there called Volker — he fights like a wild boar, and he is a minstrel. I thank my luck that I escaped that devil. His tunes sound ugly, and his bow-strokes are red. Truly, his music strikes many a hero dead. I do not know what this minstrel has against us, but I never had a guest I liked so little."

They had let out of the hall the ones they wanted to let out. Then a tremendous din rose inside. The guests took hard revenge for what had been done to them before. Bold Volker — ha, what helmets he broke!

Noble King Gunther turned toward the noise. "Do you hear the tunes, Hagen, that Volker is fiddling over there with the Huns, whoever comes to the tower door? It is a red rosin he has on his fiddle bow."

"I regret beyond all measure," said Hagen, "that I ever took a seat in the hall above that warrior.
