I was his comrade and he was mine, and if we ever get home again, we shall still be comrades, in all loyalty. Now look, noble king: Volker is devoted to you. He earns your silver and your gold with a will. His fiddle bow cuts through the hard steel; he breaks the bright, shining ornaments off the helmets. I never saw a fiddler stand so splendidly as the warrior Volker has stood today. His tunes ring through helmet and shield-rim. Truly, he deserves to ride good horses and wear splendid clothes."

Of all the Huns' kinsmen who had been in the hall, not one inside it was left alive now. So the din died away, since no one was fighting with them anymore, and the bold, high-hearted warriors laid their swords down out of their hands.

Chapter 34: How They Threw the Dead from the Hall

Worn out, the lords sat down. Volker and Hagen went out in front of the hall and the two proud men leaned on their shields, and there was a good deal of sharp talk between them.

Then Giselher of Burgundy, the warrior, said: "Dear friends, you cannot rest yet. You must carry the dead out of the hall. We are going to be attacked again, I tell you truly. They must not lie here under our feet any longer. Before the Huns overwhelm us by storm, we will hew some wounds yet, and that will do me good. I have my mind firmly set on it," said Giselher.

"Lucky me, to have such a lord," said Hagen. "The advice my young lord has given us today would suit no one but a warrior. Every one of you Burgundians can be glad of it."

They followed his advice and carried seven thousand dead to the door and threw them out. They fell to the ground in front of the hall stairs, and a great cry of grief went up from their kinsmen. Some of them were so lightly wounded that, handled more gently, they would have recovered; the high fall left them dead. Their friends grieved for that, and truly they had cause.

Then Volker the fiddler, a gallant hero, said: "Now I see for myself the truth of what I was told. The Huns are cowards; they wail like women. They would do better to look after the badly wounded."

A margrave thought he meant that kindly. He saw one of his kinsmen lying in the blood, wrapped his arms around him and started to carry him away. The bold minstrel shot him dead over the body. When the others saw that, they turned and ran, and all of them cursed that same minstrel. He snatched up a javelin, very sharp and hard, that a Hun had thrown up at him, and hurled it with all his strength out across the courtyard, far over the heads of Etzel's men. That sent them looking for a place to stand farther from the hall. Everywhere, people feared his great strength.

Many thousands of men were standing in front of the hall. Now Volker and Hagen began to tell King Etzel everything that was on their minds; the bold good heroes came to grief for it later.

"It would suit the leaders of this people very well," said Hagen, "to fight in the front rank, the way every one of my lords here does. They hew through the helmets, and the blood runs after their swords."

Etzel was so bold that he grabbed his shield. "Go carefully now," said Lady Kriemhild, "and offer the warriors gold over your shield-rim instead. If Hagen gets to you over there, you have death in your hand." The king was so brave that he would not turn back — a rare thing nowadays from so mighty a prince. They had to drag him back by his shield-strap. And grim Hagen began to taunt him again.

"That was a distant kinship," said Hagen the warrior, "that Etzel and Siegfried shared. He loved Kriemhild before she ever laid eyes on you. Wretched king, why are you plotting against me?"

The noble king's wife heard what he said, and it tormented Kriemhild that he dared insult her like that in front of Etzel's men. So she began plotting against the guests once more. She said: "Whoever kills Hagen of Tronje for me and brings me his head, I will fill Etzel's shield with red gold for him, and give him fine castles and lands besides as his reward."

"Now I know what they are waiting for," said the minstrel. "I never saw heroes stand there like such cowards, with pay that high on offer. Truly, Etzel should never be kind to them again. The men who eat the prince's bread so shamefully and now fail him in his greatest need — I see plenty of them standing here like cowards, and they still want to be counted as brave. Shame on them forever."

Chapter 35: How Iring Fought Hagen

Then Margrave Iring of Denmark called out: "I have set my whole life on honor for a long time now, and in the storm of battle I have done as well as the best. Now bring me my weapons. Truly, I mean to take on Hagen."

"I would advise against that," said Hagen. "Better tell the Huns' warriors to stand further back. If two or three of you jump into the hall, I will send them back down the stairs in a very sorry state."

"That will not make me give it up," said Iring. "I have tried things just as dangerous before. Truly, I will take you on with my sword, you alone. What good is all the bragging you have done with words?"

Then the warrior Iring was quickly armed, and Irnfried of Thuringia, a bold young man, and the very strong Hawart, with a good thousand men. Whatever Iring set out to do, they all meant to stand by him in it. The fiddler saw a very large company coming up in arms with Iring, with many a good helmet laced on their heads, and bold Volker grew more than a little angry at that.

"Do you see Iring walking over there, friend Hagen, the one who vowed to take you on alone with his sword? How does lying suit a hero? I call it a disgrace. A good thousand warriors or more are coming with him, armed."

"Do not call me a liar," said Hawart's vassal. "I will gladly do what I have vowed, and I will not back out of it from any fear. However terrible Hagen may be, I will take him on alone."

Iring went down on his knees to his kinsmen and his men, asking the warriors to let him fight alone. They did it unwillingly, for they knew arrogant Hagen of the Burgundian land only too well. But he begged them so long that in the end it happened: when his household saw what he wanted, and that he was striving for honor, they let him go. And then those two fought a grim fight.

Iring of Denmark carried his spear high and covered himself with his shield, that splendid, noble warrior, and ran up at Hagen in front of the hall, and a great roar went up from the warriors. They hurled their spears with all the strength of their arms, through the strong shields and onto the bright armor beneath, so that the shafts went spinning high in the air; and then the two grim, bold men reached for their swords. Bold Hagen's courage was very great; but Iring struck at him so hard that the whole building rang, and hall and towers echoed with their blows. Still, the warrior could not get what he wanted.

Iring left Hagen standing there unwounded and rushed at the fiddler instead. He thought he could beat him down with his hard blows, but the warrior Volker knew well how to defend himself. Then the fiddler struck a blow that sent the clasps of Iring's shield flying over its rim, and Iring let him be — he was a dangerous man — and ran at Gunther of Burgundy. Each of them was strong enough for a fight, but whatever blows Gunther and Iring gave each other, they drew no flowing blood from any wound. Their armor prevented that; it was strong and good.

He left Gunther and ran at Gernot, and began hewing fire out of the rings of his mail; and strong Gernot of Burgundy came very near to striking bold Iring dead. But Iring sprang away from the prince — he was quick enough — and in a flash the hero killed four Burgundians, men of the noble household of Worms on the Rhine. Giselher had never been angrier in his life.

"God knows, Lord Iring," said young Giselher, "you shall pay me for the men who have just fallen dead in front of you." Then he ran at him and struck the Dane so that he could not move from the spot. He went down into the blood in front of Giselher's hands, and they all believed the good hero would never strike another blow in battle. But Iring lay there in front of Giselher without a wound. From the crash of the sword and the ringing of his helmet, his senses had gone so weak that the bold warrior no longer knew he was alive. Strong Giselher's strength had done that to him.

When the ringing he had taken from that great blow began to clear from his head, he thought: "I am still alive, and not wounded anywhere. Now for the first time I know what Giselher's courage is." He heard his enemies standing on both sides of him; if they had known the truth, more would have been done to him. He could hear Giselher close by him too, and he thought about how he could get away from his enemies. How wildly he sprang up out of the blood! He had his quickness to thank for it. He ran out of the house to where he found Hagen again, and struck him grim blows with his strong arm.

Hagen thought: "You are going to die. Unless the foul devil protects you, you cannot come out of this alive." But Iring wounded Hagen through the hood of his helmet; the hero did that with Waske, a sword that good. When Lord Hagen felt the wound, the sword began to swing terribly in his hand, and Hawart's vassal had to give way before him, down the stairs, with Hagen coming after him. Bold Iring swung his shield over his head, and had that staircase been three staircases long, Hagen would not have let him land a single blow the whole way. Hey, what red sparks flew above his head!

Iring got back to his own men in one piece, and Kriemhild soon heard the whole story of what he had done to Hagen of Tronje in the fight. The queen began to thank him warmly for it. "God reward you, Iring, you famous good hero. You have put heart and courage back into me. Now I see Hagen's clothes red with blood." And out of sheer joy Kriemhild took the shield from his hand herself.

"Go easy with your thanks to him," said Hagen. "If he wanted to try it again, that would be worthy of a warrior; and if he came away from it alive, then he would be a bold man. The wound I took from him does you little good. Seeing the rings of my mail red from my wound has only roused me to the deaths of many men. Now for the first time I am angry, like a man who knows no fear. The warrior Iring has done me very little harm so far."

Iring of Denmark stood in the wind, cooling himself in his mail, and unlaced his helmet. Everyone there said his courage was good, and the margrave's spirits rose high at that. Then Iring spoke again: "My friends, be sure of this: arm me again quickly. I mean to try harder, and see whether I can still beat that arrogant man." His shield was hacked to pieces, and he got a better one.

The warrior was very soon armed again, and better than before. In his hatred he took a very strong spear, meaning to take on Hagen with it once more, over there; and the murderous, grim man waited for him like an enemy. The warrior Hagen could not bear to wait for him. He ran to meet him, throwing and striking, right to the foot of the stairs; his rage was great. Iring's strength did him very little good then. They struck through each other's shields until it began to blaze in fire-red gusts, and Hawart's vassal was gravely wounded by Hagen's sword, through shield and through mail. He never recovered from it.

When the warrior Iring felt the wound, he pulled his shield up higher over his helmet straps. He thought the harm he had taken there was the full measure of it; but King Gunther's vassal did him worse afterward. Hagen found a spear lying at his feet. He hurled it at Iring, the hero of Denmark, so that the shaft stood out of his head. The warrior Hagen had brought him to a grim end.

Iring had to fall back to the men of Denmark. Before they had even unlaced the warrior's helmet, they broke the spear out of his head, and death was already drawing near him. His kinsmen wept at that; they had reason enough. Then the queen came and bent over him. She began to lament strong Iring and wept over his wounds; she grieved for him bitterly. And in front of his kinsmen the bold, brave warrior said:

"Let the lamenting be, noble lady. What good is your weeping? Truly, I must lose my life from the wounds I have taken. Death will not let me serve you and Etzel any longer." To the men of Thuringia and the men of Denmark he said: "None of you must take the queen's gift, her bright red gold, in return for this. If you take on Hagen, you will have to look death in the face."

His color had drained away; bold Iring bore the mark of death, and it was grief enough to them. Hawart's vassal could not live, and then the men of Denmark had to go to battle. Irnfried and Hawart sprang toward the hall with a good thousand heroes. A huge, raging din was heard on every side, powerful and loud. Hey, what sharp spears were hurled at the Burgundians!

Bold Irnfried ran at the minstrel, and got great harm at his hands: the noble fiddler struck the landgrave through his strong helmet — truly, he was grim enough. Then Lord Irnfried struck the bold minstrel so hard that the clasps of his mail burst and his coat of mail was showered with fire-red sparks. All the same, the landgrave fell dead at the fiddler's hands.

Hawart and Hagen had come together. Anyone watching would have seen wonders: the swords fell thick and fast in the heroes' hands. Hawart had to die at the hands of the man from the Burgundian land. When the Danes and the Thuringians saw their lords dead, a terrible struggle rose in front of the hall before they won the door with brave hands; many a helmet and shield-rim was hacked apart in that fight.

"Give way," said Volker, "and let them come in. What they have in mind will never be finished otherwise. They will have to die in here in a very short time; with their deaths they will earn what the queen is giving them."

When the arrogant men came into the hall, many a head was bowed so low that its owner had to die from the Burgundians' swift blows. Bold Gernot fought well, and so did the warrior Giselher. A thousand and four came into the house, and many a swift sword-stroke was seen flashing; in the end the warriors were all slain inside. Great wonders could be told of the Burgundians.

After that a silence fell, as the noise died away. The blood of the dead men ran out everywhere through the openings and down to the gutter-stones. The men from the Rhine had done that with their great courage. Then the men of Burgundy sat down to rest again. They laid their weapons and their shields out of their hands. But the bold minstrel still stood in front of the tower, watching in case anyone else wanted to come at them in battle.

The king grieved bitterly, and so did his wife; the girls and the ladies tormented themselves with grief. I think Death itself had sworn an oath against them: many more of the warriors were still to be lost there at the guests' hands.

Chapter 36: How the Queen Had the Hall Set on Fire

"Now unlace your helmets," said Hagen the warrior. "My comrade and I will keep watch over you, and if Etzel's men want to try us again, I will warn my lords the moment I can."

Then many a good knight bared his head. They sat down on the wounded, who had fallen into the blood in front of them, killed by their own hands. The noble guests were watched with evil eyes. Before evening came, the king and the queen too saw to it that the Hunnish warriors made a better try of it. A good twenty thousand of them could be seen standing before the guests; they had to go into the fight. At once a hard storm broke against the guests. Dankwart, Hagen's brother, that very swift man, sprang from his lords' side to meet the enemy outside the door. Everyone thought he was dead, but he came back through unharmed. The hard fight lasted until night put an end to it. The guests defended themselves against Etzel's men the whole summer-long day, as good heroes should. Oh, how many bold warriors lay dead before them! It was at midsummer that this great murder happened, when Lady Kriemhild avenged her heart's grief on her closest kinsmen and on many another man; and King Etzel never knew joy again because of it.

The day had run out on them, and they were in fear and peril. They thought a quick death would be better for them than to be tormented a long time with monstrous suffering. So those proud, fine knights asked for a truce. They asked that the king be brought to them. Out of the hall stepped the heroes, the color of blood and the color of their armor, the three noble kings. They did not know who to complain to about their very great hurt. Etzel and Kriemhild both came there. The land was theirs, so their numbers kept growing. Etzel said to the guests: "Now tell me, what do you want of me? You think you can win a truce here; that would be hard to do, after harm as great as you have done me. You shall get nothing by it, as long as I have my life. My child, whom you killed, and so many of my kinsmen — for that, truce and reconciliation shall be flatly denied you."

Gunther answered: "Great need forced us to it. All my household lay dead in the lodgings before your heroes. How had I deserved that? I came to you in good faith; I thought you were my friend."

Then young Giselher of Burgundy said: "You heroes of Etzel's who are still alive here — what do you blame me for, warriors? What have I done to you? I rode into this land as a friend."

They said: "Your friendliness has filled this whole castle with misery, and the land with it. Truly, we would have been glad if you had never come from Worms across the Rhine. You and your brothers have left this land full of orphans."

Then Gunther the warrior said angrily: "If you would set this bitter hatred aside and make peace with us warriors who are so far from home, that would be good for both sides. We have done nothing to deserve what Etzel is doing to us."

Then the host said to his guests: "My grief and yours are not alike at all. The great hardship, the loss and the shame on top of it, that I have taken here — for that, not one of you shall ever leave here alive."

Then strong Gernot said to the king: "Then may God command you to act with honor. Kill us, who are far from home; but let us first come down to you into the open. That would do you honor. Whatever is going to happen to us, let it happen quickly. You have so many men still unhurt, who dare to take us on; they will not let us battle-weary men live. How long must we warriors go on in this hardship?"

Etzel's warriors had almost agreed to it; they were going to let them come out of the hall. Kriemhild heard that, and it was deeply hateful to her. So the men far from home were refused their truce too soon.

"No, no, you Hunnish warriors! What you have in mind — I advise you in true loyalty, do not do it. Do not let those murderers out of the hall, or your kinsmen will suffer a deadly fall. If none of them were left alive but Ute's sons, my noble brothers, and they got out into the breeze and cooled their mail rings, you would all be lost. Bolder warriors were never born into the world."

Then young Giselher said: "My beautiful sister, my trust was badly repaid when you invited me from across the Rhine here to this land, into this great peril. How have I earned death here from the Huns? I was always loyal to you; I never did you any wrong. I rode here to this land on one hope: that you still loved me, my dear sister. Think of us with mercy; there is nothing else to be done now."

"I cannot show you mercy. I have none. Hagen of Tronje has done me such great wrong that it can never be made right as long as I live. You must all pay for it," said King Etzel's wife. "If you will give me Hagen alone as a hostage, I will not refuse to let you live, for you are my brothers and children of one mother. Then I will speak of peace with these heroes who are here."

"God in heaven forbid," said Gernot. "If there were a thousand of us, we would all lie dead, we of your own blood, before we gave you a single man as hostage. It will never be done."

"We have to die in any case," said Giselher. "No one is going to part us from a knight's defense. Whoever wants to fight us — well, we are still here. For I never failed a friend of mine in loyalty."

Then bold Dankwart spoke; it would not have suited him to keep quiet. "Truly, my brother Hagen does not stand alone. Those who refuse us the truce here may yet be sorry for it. I will make you well aware of that, I tell you truly."

Then the queen said: "You fine heroes, now go closer to the stairs and avenge my wrongs. I will always repay you for it, as by right I should. I will pay Hagen back for his arrogance. Do not let a single one of them out of the house." She ordered the hall to be set on fire at all four ends. "Then all my wrongs will be well avenged." Etzel's warriors were quickly ready.

Those of the guests who still stood close by outside were driven into the hall with blows and missiles; the noise of it was very great. But the princes and their men would not be parted; for their loyalty they could not leave one another. Then King Etzel's wife had the hall set alight, and the warriors' bodies were tortured there with fire. A wind caught the house and very soon the whole of it was burning. I do not think any people ever knew greater fear.

Many inside cried out: "Oh, this misery! We would far rather have died in the fighting. God could have pity on it, how we are all lost! Now the queen is wreaking her anger on us without measure."

One of them inside said: "We are going to lie dead here. What good to us now is the greeting the king sent us? The thirst from this terrible heat hurts me so badly that I think my life will soon end in this misery."

Then Hagen of Tronje said: "You noble good knights, whoever is tormented by thirst, let him drink the blood here. In heat like this it is better than wine. There is nothing better to be had just now."

Then one of the warriors went to where he found a dead man. He knelt down by the wound, unlaced his helmet, and began to drink the flowing blood. Unused to it as he was, it seemed very good to him.

"God reward you, Sir Hagen," said the weary man, "that I have drunk so well at your advice. I have seldom been served better wine. If I live any while longer, I will always be your friend."

When the others heard that it had seemed good to him, there were many more of them who drank the blood too. Some of them got great strength from it. Later many a lovely woman paid for that in dear kinsmen.

The fire kept falling on them in the hall, and they turned it aside with their shields and down to the floor. The smoke and the heat both hurt them badly. I do not think such misery will ever come to heroes again.

Then Hagen of Tronje said: "Stand close to the walls of the hall. Do not let the burning brands fall on your helmet straps; tread them down into the blood with your feet. It is an evil festival the queen is giving us."

In such suffering the night at last wore away. Still the bold fiddler stood outside the house, and Hagen his comrade with him, leaning on the rims of their shields. They expected more harm from the men of Etzel's land.

Then the fiddler said: "Now let us go into the hall. Then the Huns will think we are all dead of the torment that has been done to us. They will yet see a good few of us warriors standing against them in the fight."

Then young Giselher of Burgundy said: "I think it is going to be day; a cooler wind is rising. Now may God in heaven let us live to see a happier time. My sister Kriemhild has given us a wretched festival."

Then another said: "I can make out the day. Since nothing can be better for us now, arm yourselves, heroes, and think of your lives. Truly, King Etzel's wife will be at us again soon."

The host fully expected that the guests were dead from their hardship and the terror of the fire. But six hundred bold men were still alive in there, and no king ever had better warriors. Those set to guard the men far from home had seen clearly that the guests still lived, however much harm and grief had been done to the lords and their men. They could be seen standing in the hall, still sound and well. Kriemhild was told that a great many of them had survived. The queen said: "That can never be, that any of them should have lived through the terror of that fire. I would sooner believe they all lie dead."

The princes and their men would still gladly have lived, if anyone had been willing to show them mercy. But they could find none among the men of the Huns' land. So they avenged their own dying with very ready hands.

That day, toward morning, they were offered a greeting of hard battle, and it brought heroes into peril. Many a hard spear was flung at them, and the bold, proud warriors defended themselves like knights. Etzel's household were in high spirits over it: they meant to earn Kriemhild's gold, and to carry out, besides, what the king commanded. Many of them would soon have to look death in the face for it. Of the promises and the gifts there are wonders to tell. She had the red gold carried in on shields, and she gave it to anyone who cared for it and would take it. Truly, greater pay was never offered against enemies. A great force of warriors went armed toward the hall.

Then bold Volker said: "Well, we are still here. I never saw heroes come more gladly to a fight than these who have taken the king's gold to do us harm."

Then plenty of them called out: "Closer, heroes, closer! Let us earn what we are to earn, and do it in good time. No one stays here except the man who has to die anyway." Soon their shields could be seen stuck full of thrown spears.

What more can I say? A good twelve hundred men tried them hard, surging back and forth. The guests cooled their fury well with wounds. No one could part the fight, and the blood could be seen flowing. Many a man there was struck with a deadly deep wound. Each man could be heard grieving bitterly for his friends. All the great king's brave warriors died, and their loving kinsmen had terrible sorrow for them.

Chapter 37: How Rüdiger Was Killed

The strangers had done well toward morning. Gotelind's husband came to court, and when he saw the grievous losses on both sides, good Rüdiger wept from his heart. "How wretched I am," said the warrior, "that I was ever born, since no one can stop this great misery. However much I want to make peace, the king will not do it, for he sees the sufferings of his people growing greater and greater."

Good Rüdiger sent to Dietrich to ask whether they could still turn the fate of the noble kings. The lord of Bern sent back his answer: "Who could stop it? King Etzel will let no one part the fighting."

A Hun warrior saw Rüdiger standing there with weeping eyes — he had shed many tears — and said to the queen: "Now look how he stands there, the man who holds the greatest power here at Etzel's side, the man whom people and lands all serve. How is it that so many castles have been turned over to Rüdiger, when he holds so many of them from the king? In all this fighting he has not struck one praiseworthy blow. It seems to me he does not care how things go here, since he has all he wants. People say he is braver than anyone could be. That has shown itself badly in this trouble."

In sorrow the loyal man looked at the one he heard say this. He thought: "You will pay for that. You say I am a coward; you have told your tale too loudly at court." He clenched his fist and ran at him and struck the Hun so hard that he lay dead at his feet in an instant. And so King Etzel's distress was greater again.

"Away with you, you notorious coward," said Rüdiger. "I have grief and pain enough already. Why do you reproach me for not fighting here? Truly I have good cause to hate the guests, and I would have done everything I could against them — except that I am the one who brought these warriors here. I was their escort into my lord's land, and so this wretched hand of mine shall not fight them."

Then King Etzel said to the margrave: "A fine help you have been to us, noble Rüdiger! We have so many doomed men in this land already that we had no need of more. You have done very wrong."

The noble knight answered: "He insulted me, and threw in my face the honor and the goods I have taken in such plenty from your hands. That turned out a little unlucky for the liar."

Then the queen came; she too had seen what the hero's anger had done to the Hun. She lamented it beyond measure, and her eyes grew wet. She said to Rüdiger: "How have we deserved this, that you add to the sorrows of the king and me? Until now, noble Rüdiger, you have always told us you would risk your honor and your life for us. I have heard many warriors give you the highest praise. I remind you of the favor you promised me, and of what you swore to me, chosen knight, when you counseled me to take Etzel: that you would serve me until one of us died. Never has a poor woman been in such need of it as I am now."

The margrave answered: "There is no denying it. I swore to you, noble lady, that I would risk my honor and my life for your sake. That I would lose my soul, I never swore. It was I who invited the highborn princes to this feast."

She said: "Remember, Rüdiger, your great loyalty, your constancy and your oaths: that you would always be the first to right my wrongs, and avenge every grief of mine." The margrave said: "I have seldom refused you anything."

Mighty Etzel began to plead as well, and the two of them fell at the warrior's feet. The noble margrave stood there in anguish, and the loyal warrior spoke in the bitterest misery: "Oh, God help me, wretched man that I am, that I ever lived to see this! I must give up all my honor, and the loyalty and the good breeding that God commanded of me. Oh, God in heaven, that death does not come and turn this from me! Whichever I leave undone and whichever I take up, I shall have acted badly and wickedly; and if I leave them both, the whole world will curse me. May he who gave me life show me what to do."

The king and his wife kept begging him, hard. Because of it, warriors would soon lose their lives at Rüdiger's hands, and there the hero died himself. You will hear now how wretchedly he went about it. He knew he would win nothing but harm and monstrous grief. He would gladly have refused the king, and the queen too; he feared bitterly that if he killed a single one of them, the whole world would hate him.

Then the brave man said to the king: "Sir King, take back everything I hold from you — the land and the castles. Let none of it remain to me. I will go on foot into exile."

King Etzel said: "Who would help me then? The land and the people — I give it all to you outright, so that you avenge me on my enemies, Rüdiger. You shall be a mighty king beside Etzel."

Rüdiger answered: "How can I set about it? I invited them home to my own house; I offered them food and drink as a friend, and I gave them my gifts. How can I plot their deaths? People may easily think I am a coward. I have never refused the noble princes and their men any service of mine; and now the friendship I made with them grieves me. I gave my daughter to the warrior Giselher. She could not have been better bestowed in all this world — for breeding and for honor, for loyalty and for wealth. I never saw so young a king so nobly minded."

Kriemhild said again: "Most noble Rüdiger, take pity on the suffering of us both, mine and the king's. Think well on this: no host ever had such deadly guests."

The margrave answered the noble woman: "Today Rüdiger's life must pay for whatever kindness you and my lord have ever shown me. For that I must die; it can be put off no longer. I know well that this very day my castles and my lands will fall back to you by the hand of one of those men. I commend my wife and my children to your mercy, and the strangers too, the exiles who are at Pöchlarn."

"Now God reward you, Rüdiger," said the king, and he and the queen were both glad. "Your people shall be well cared for by us. And I trust my own good fortune that you yourself will come through alive."

So he laid soul and body in the balance. Etzel's wife began to weep. He said: "I must keep my word to you as I promised. Alas for my friends, whom I attack so unwillingly!"

He was seen going from the king in deep sorrow. He found his warriors standing close by, and said: "Arm yourselves, all my men. To my grief, I must fight the bold Burgundians."

They had the squires run at once to where their weapons were kept; helmet or shield-rim, the household brought it all out to them. Soon the proud exiles heard sorrowful news. Rüdiger was armed now, with five hundred men, and besides them he won twelve warriors to his help, who wanted to earn fame in the peril of battle. They did not know that death was so close to them.

Then Rüdiger was seen going under his helmet. Rüdiger's men carried sharp swords, and in front of them their bright broad shields. The fiddler saw it, and it grieved him deeply.

Young Giselher saw his father-in-law coming with his helmet laced on. How could he understand what he meant by it, except that he meant all good? The noble king's heart was glad. "What friends we have won on this journey," said the warrior Giselher. "My wife's family will do us a great deal of good here. On my loyalty, I am glad the match was ever made."

"I do not know where you find your comfort," said the minstrel. "When did you ever see so many heroes come for the sake of peace with their helmets laced on and swords in their hands? Rüdiger means to earn his castles and his lands at our expense."

Before the fiddler had finished speaking, noble Rüdiger was seen in front of the hall. He set his good shield down in front of his feet; now he had to refuse his friends both service and greeting. The noble margrave called into the hall: "Bold Nibelungs, now defend yourselves on every side. You should have profited by me; now you pay for me. Once we were friends. I want to be free of that loyalty."

The hard-pressed men were dismayed at this news; none of them found any joy in it that the man they loved now meant to fight them. They had already endured great hardship from their enemies. "God in heaven forbid," said the warrior Gunther, "that you should turn from your kindness to us, and from the great loyalty that we truly counted on. I trust you will never do it."

"I cannot leave it undone," said the brave man. "I must fight you, for I have given my word. Now defend yourselves, bold heroes, as you love your lives. King Etzel's wife would not release me."

"You turn against us too late," said the noble king. "May God reward you, noble Rüdiger, for the loyalty and the love you have shown us — if only you would act with kindness to the end. We should always be in your debt, I and my kinsmen, for what you gave us, if you let us live: the splendid gifts you brought us here, in loyalty, into Etzel's land. Think of that, noble Rüdiger."

"How gladly I would grant you," said the warrior Rüdiger, "that I might give you my gifts in full measure, as freely as I once hoped to — if only I would never be blamed for it."

"Turn back, noble Rüdiger," said Gernot. "No host ever treated his guests as lovingly as you treated us. You will profit from it well, if we stay alive."

"Would to God, most noble Gernot," said Rüdiger, "that you were on the Rhine and I were dead with some honor, since I must fight you. No friends ever did worse by heroes."

"God reward you, Lord Rüdiger," Gernot answered, "for your rich gifts. Your death grieves me, if such goodness as yours is to perish. Here I carry your weapon, the one you gave me, good hero. It has never failed me in all this peril; under its edges lies many a knight dead. It is bright and true, splendid and good. I believe no warrior will ever give so rich a gift again. And if you will not turn back, if you mean to come at us, and you kill any of the friends I still have in here, then with your own sword I will take your life. Then I will be sorry for you, Rüdiger, and for your splendid wife."

"Would to God, Lord Gernot, that it could happen: that your whole will were done here, and your friends came out of it alive! Then both my daughter and my wife could trust you well."

Then young Giselher, beautiful Ute's son, spoke for Burgundy: "Why do you do this, Lord Rüdiger? Those who came with me all wish you well. You are setting about this badly; you want to make your beautiful daughter a widow too soon. When you and your warriors attack me in battle, how unfriendly you make it look — I who trust you above all other men, and that is why I took your daughter for my wife."

"Remember your loyalty, most noble king, and if God lets you leave this place," said Rüdiger, "do not let the young lady pay for me. For your own goodness' sake, be gracious to her."

"That I would do, and rightly," said young Giselher. "But if my high kinsmen who are still in here are to die at your hands, then my steadfast friendship for you, and for your daughter too, must be at an end."

"Now may God have mercy on us," said the brave man. Then they raised their shields, as if to go and fight the guests in Kriemhild's hall. But Hagen called down loudly from the top of the stairs: "Wait a while, most noble Rüdiger," said Hagen. "I and my lords want to say more, as our extremity forces us to. What good can the death of us exiles do Etzel? I stand in great trouble," Hagen went on. "The shield Lady Gotelind gave me to carry has been hacked to pieces in my hand by the Huns. I brought it in friendship into Etzel's land. If only God in heaven would grant that I might still carry a shield as good as the one in your hand, most noble Rüdiger! Then I would need no coat of mail in the fighting."

"Gladly would I serve you with my shield, if I dared offer it to you in front of Kriemhild. But take it, Hagen, and carry it on your arm. Ah, if only you could carry it home to the land of Burgundy!"

When he offered to give the shield so willingly, many eyes grew red with hot tears. It was the last gift that Rüdiger of Pöchlarn ever gave to any warrior. However grim Hagen was, and however hard his temper, the gift the good hero had made so near his last hour moved him. Many a noble knight began to grieve with him.

"God in heaven reward you, most noble Rüdiger. There will never be another like you, to give exiled warriors such splendid gifts. God grant that your goodness lives forever." "Alas for this news," said Hagen again. "We had so many other burdens to carry; must we fight with friends too? God be told of it." The margrave said: "It grieves me from my heart."

"God reward you for the gift, most noble Rüdiger. Whatever these proud warriors do toward you, my hand shall never touch you in this battle, even if you kill every one of them from the land of Burgundy."

Good Rüdiger bowed to him courteously. They wept on every side, that no one could part this heartbreak; it was a great misery. In Rüdiger the father of many virtues was about to die.

Then Volker the minstrel spoke from the hall: "Since my comrade Hagen has made peace with you, you shall have it just as steadfastly from my hand. You earned it well when we came into this land. Noble margrave, you shall be my messenger. The margravine gave me these red arm-rings, to wear here at the feast. See them for yourself, so that you can be my witness to it."

"Would to God in heaven," said Rüdiger, "that the margravine could give you more! I will gladly tell my dear love the news, if I see her alive again. Of that you may have no doubt."

When he had promised him that, Rüdiger raised his shield. His heart raged; he waited no longer. He ran at the guests like a true warrior, and the mighty margrave struck many a swift blow. The two of them, Volker and Hagen, stepped higher up the stairs, for the bold warriors had promised him that. But he still found men bold enough standing by the tower, so that Rüdiger began the fight with heavy care.

Bent on deadly revenge, Gunther and Gernot let him in; they had heroes' hearts. Giselher stood back higher up, and truly it grieved him. He still hoped to live, and so he kept clear of Rüdiger.

The margrave's men sprang at their enemies; they were seen following their lord like true warriors. They carried their cutting swords in their hands, and many helmets and many a splendid shield-rim broke under them. The weary men struck the men of Pöchlarn many a hard blow that cut clean and deep through the bright mail rings, right down to the life. They did splendid work in that storm of battle.

The noble company had now come all the way in. Volker and Hagen sprang into it at once; they gave quarter to no one but the one man. Blood ran down out of the helmets from the hands of both of them. How grimly the swords rang in there! The shield-plates burst from their fastenings under the blows, and the jewels hewn from the shields fell into the blood. They fought so grimly that no one will ever do the like again.

The lord of Pöchlarn went back and forth, as a man does who knows how to work with courage in battle. That day Rüdiger showed himself every inch a warrior, bold and worthy of praise. Here stood the warriors Gunther and Gernot; they struck many a hero dead in the fight. Giselher and Dankwart, the two of them, made light of it; they sent many a man to his last day.

Rüdiger showed well that he was strong enough, bold and well armed. Hey, what heroes he killed! A Burgundian saw it, and anger drove him; and from that, noble Rüdiger's death began to draw near.

Strong Gernot called out to the hero. He said to the margrave: "You will not leave me any of my men alive, most noble Rüdiger. That grieves me beyond measure; I can watch it no longer. Now your gift may well do you harm, since you have taken so many of my friends from me. Turn this way, noble, brave man: your gift shall be paid for as best I can."

Before the margrave could reach him, bright mail rings had to lose their color. Then the two men, hungry for honor, sprang at each other, and each began to guard himself against deep wounds. Their swords were so sharp that nothing could stand against them. Then the warrior Rüdiger struck Gernot through his flint-hard helmet, so that the blood flowed down. The bold good knight soon paid him back. He swung Rüdiger's gift high in his hand, and wounded to death as he was, he struck him one blow through his good shield, down to the helmet strap. From that, beautiful Gotelind's husband had to die.

Truly, so rich a gift was never worse repaid. They fell, both killed, Gernot and Rüdiger, alike in the fight, each by the other's hand. Only now did Hagen grow angry, when he saw the great loss.

The hero of Tronje said: "This has gone badly for us. In these two men we have suffered a loss so great that their people and their lands will never get over it. Rüdiger's heroes are forfeit to us exiles. Alas for my brother, who has been killed here! What grievous news comes to me all the time! And noble Rüdiger too I must mourn forever. The loss and the grievous wounds are on both sides."

When Lord Giselher saw his father-in-law dead, those still inside had to suffer for it. Death went hunting hard through Rüdiger's company; not one of the men of Pöchlarn was left alive.

Then Gunther and Giselher, and Hagen too, Dankwart and Volker, the good warriors, went to where they found the two men lying, and there the heroes wept in grief. "Death is robbing us cruelly," said young Giselher. "Now stop your weeping and let us go out to the air, so that the wind cools the mail on us battle-weary men. Truly, I think God in heaven does not grant us much longer to live."

Here one warrior sat and there another leaned, many of them; they were idle again, for Rüdiger's heroes lay dead. The din had died away. The silence lasted so long that it troubled Etzel.

"Alas for such service," said the king's wife. "It is not loyal enough to make our enemies pay with their lives at Rüdiger's hand. He means to bring them back to the land of Burgundy. What use is it, King Etzel, that we shared with him whatever he wanted? The hero has done wrong. The man who should avenge us is making peace." Volker, the fine warrior, answered her:

"The truth is not so, alas, most noble queen. If I dared to call so noble a lady a liar, I would say you have lied like a devil against Rüdiger. He and his warriors are quite cheated of any peace. He did what the king commanded so completely that he and his household lie dead here. Now look around you, Kriemhild, for someone else to command. The hero Rüdiger has served you to the end. If you will not believe it, we will let you see." To her heart's grief it was done: they carried the hewn hero to where the king could see him. Nothing so sorrowful had ever happened to Etzel's warriors.

When they saw the margrave carried in dead, no scribe could write or tell of the frenzied grief of women and men that began to show itself there, from the sorrow of their hearts. Etzel's grief grew so great that the mighty king's voice rang out like a lion's roar in heartbroken lament; and so did his wife's. Beyond all measure, they mourned good Rüdiger.

Chapter 38: How All of Lord Dietrich's Warriors Were Killed

On every side you could hear grief so great that the hall and the towers rang with the wailing. One of Dietrich's men from Bern heard it too, and how fast he hurried off with this heavy news! He said to his prince: "Listen, my lord Dietrich. In all my life until now I have never heard a lament so impossible to bear as the one I have just heard. I think King Etzel himself has come to harm. How else could they all be in such distress? One of the two, the king or Kriemhild, lies dead, struck down by the bold strangers out of hatred. Many a fine warrior is weeping beyond all measure."

The hero of Bern said: "My dear men, do not be in too great a hurry. Whatever the warriors far from home have done here, they are in great danger now; let them have the benefit of the peace I offered them."

Bold Wolfhart said: "I will go over there and ask what they have done, and then I will tell you, my dear lord, just as I find it out, what this lament may be about."

Lord Dietrich said: "Where men expect anger, and rude questions are put to them on top of it, that can easily spoil good warriors' high spirits. No, Wolfhart, I do not want you to be the one who questions them."

He asked Helfrich to go over quickly and told him to find out, from Etzel's men or from the guests themselves, what had happened there; for no one had ever seen such grief among people. The messenger asked: "What has been done here?" One of them answered: "Whatever joy we had in the land of the Huns is utterly gone. Here lies Rüdiger, killed by Burgundian hands, and of those who came in with him not one has survived."

Helfrich could never have been sorrier. Never had he carried news so unwillingly; the messenger went back to Dietrich weeping bitterly. "What have you found out for us?" Dietrich said. "Why are you weeping so hard, warrior Helfrich?" The noble warrior said: "I have good reason to grieve. The Burgundians have killed good Rüdiger."

The hero of Bern said: "God forbid! That would be a terrible revenge, and the devil's own mockery. What has Rüdiger ever done to deserve that from them? I know very well that he is a friend to the strangers."

Wolfhart answered: "If they have done it, it must cost them all their lives. If we let it pass, we would be disgraced; good Rüdiger's hand has served us many a time."

The lord of the Amelungs ordered the matter looked into more carefully. In bitter grief he sat down in a window, and he asked Hildebrand to go to the guests and find out from them what had been done there. Master Hildebrand, that warrior bold in any storm of battle, carried neither shield nor weapon in his hand: he meant to go to the guests in the courteous way. For that his sister's son rebuked him. Grim Wolfhart said: "If you go over there unarmed, you will never get away without abuse; you will have to come back in disgrace. But if you go armed, every one of them will think twice about that."

So the wise man armed himself on the young man's advice. Before he knew it, all of Dietrich's warriors had put on their armor and stood with swords in their hands. The hero was sorry to see it; he would gladly have turned them back. He asked them where they meant to go. "We are coming with you. Then perhaps Hagen of Tronje will think twice before speaking to you with scorn, which he knows so well how to do." When he heard that, the warrior let them come.

Bold Volker saw the warriors of Bern, Dietrich's men, coming well armed, swords belted on and shields in their hands. He told his lords from the land of Burgundy. The fiddler said: "I see Dietrich's men coming over there in a very hostile way, armed and helmeted. They mean to attack us. I think it is going to go badly for us strangers."

At the same moment Hildebrand arrived. He set the rim of his shield down in front of his feet and asked Gunther's men: "Alas, you good heroes, what had Rüdiger done to you? My lord Dietrich has sent me here to you: if the hand of any of you has killed the noble margrave, as we have been told, we could never get over so great a sorrow."

Hagen of Tronje said: "The news is no lie. I would gladly wish for your sake that the messenger had deceived you, for love of Rüdiger, and that he were still alive, a man whom men and women alike may weep for forever."

When they heard for certain that he was dead, the warriors mourned him, as their loyalty required of them. You could see the tears running down over the beards and chins of Dietrich's warriors. Great sorrow had been done to them.

Sigestab, the duke of Bern, said: "Now there is an end to the comfort Rüdiger gave us after our days of sorrow. The joy of a whole people far from home lies killed in Rüdiger."

Wolfwin, the warrior of the Amelungs, said: "If I saw my own father dead today, I could not be sorrier than I am for his life. Alas, who will comfort the noble margrave's wife now?"

The warrior Wolfhart said in an angry mood: "Who will lead the warriors now on so many a campaign, as the margrave so often did? Alas, noble Rüdiger, that we have lost you like this!"

Wolfbrand and Helfrich and Helmnot too, with all their kinsmen, mourned his death. Hildebrand could ask no more for sighing. He said: "Now, warriors, do what my lord has sent us here for. Give us Rüdiger's dead body out of the hall, the man in whom all our joy lies fallen in grief, and let us repay him for the great loyalty he always showed to us and to many another man. We are exiles too, as the warrior Rüdiger was. Why do you keep us waiting? Let us carry him away, so that we can still repay him after his death. We ought by rights to have done it while he was alive."

King Gunther said: "No service was ever as good as the one a friend does for a friend after his death. I call that steadfast loyalty, in whoever can do it. You are right to repay him; he has shown you much love."

"How long are we to go on begging?" said the warrior Wolfhart. "Since our best comfort lies dead by your hands, and we sadly can never have him again, let us carry him out of here so that we can bury the warrior."

Volker answered: "No one is going to give him to you. Take him from the house where the warrior lies fallen in the blood with his deep wounds. Then it will be a full service that you do for Rüdiger."

Bold Wolfhart said: "God knows, sir minstrel, you had better not provoke us; you have done us wrong enough. If I dared, in front of my lord, you would be in trouble. We have to let it be, because he has forbidden us to fight here."

The fiddler said: "A man has far too much fear if he gives up everything he is forbidden. I cannot call that a true hero's spirit." Hagen thought his comrade's words were well said.

"Do not let that trouble you," said Wolfhart. "I will put your strings so badly out of tune that you will have something to tell about it when you ride home to the Rhine. I cannot put up with your arrogance and keep my honor."

The fiddler said: "If you throw my strings off their good tune, the shine of your helmet will have to go very dull at my hand, however I ride home to the land of Burgundy."

Then he wanted to spring at him, but his uncle Hildebrand would not let him; he gripped him hard. "I think you would go mad with your foolish temper. You would have lost my lord's favor forever."

"Let the lion go, master; he is in such a fierce mood," said Volker the good warrior. "If he comes within reach of my hands, even if he had killed the whole world with his own hand, I will strike him so that he never tells the story afterward."

At that the men of Bern grew very angry. Wolfhart, a swift good warrior, snatched up his shield and ran at him in front of the others like a wild lion, and his friends came hard after him. But however great the strides he took toward the wall of the hall, old Hildebrand caught him before the steps; he did not want him to get into the fight ahead of himself. They found what they were looking for among the strangers soon enough.

Master Hildebrand sprang at Hagen. You could hear the swords ring in both their hands. They were in a fury; that was plain to see afterward, for a fire-red wind came off the two men's swords. They were parted in the press of the battle by the men of Bern, as their strength demanded. At once Hildebrand turned away from Hagen, and strong Wolfhart ran at bold Volker. He struck the fiddler on his good helmet so that the sword's edge bit through to the clasps. The bold minstrel repaid that with courage: he struck Wolfhart so that the sparks flew off him. They hewed plenty of fire from each other's mail, for each of them hated the other. Then Wolfwin, the warrior of Bern, parted them — if he were not a hero, he could never have done it.

Gunther the warrior received the famous heroes from the land of the Amelungs with a very willing hand. Lord Giselher made many a shining helmet red and wet with blood. Dankwart, Hagen's brother, was a grim man. Whatever he had done before to Etzel's warriors in battle was nothing but wind: now bold Aldrian's son fought in a rage. Ritschart and Gerbart, Helfrich and Wichart had very seldom spared themselves in many a battle, and they made Gunther's men well aware of it. Wolfbrand could be seen going splendidly through the fight. Old Hildebrand fought as if he were mad. Many good warriors had to fall dead into the blood at Wolfhart's hand, cut down by swords. So the bold good warriors avenged Rüdiger.

Lord Sigestab fought as his courage told him to. Hey, what good helmets of his enemies Dietrich's sister's son split in that fight! He could never have done better in battle. When strong Volker saw bold Sigestab hewing a stream of blood from the hard mail-rings, the hero grew angry. He sprang to meet him, and there Sigestab very quickly lost his life at the fiddler's hands: Volker gave him such a share of his art that he had to lie dead by his sword. Old Hildebrand avenged that, as his courage demanded.

"Alas for my dear lord," said Master Hildebrand, "who lies dead here at Volker's hand. Now the fiddler shall live no longer." How could bold Hildebrand ever have been fiercer? He struck Volker so that the helmet-clasps flew on every side to the walls of the hall, off the bold minstrel's helmet and shield. That was how strong Volker met his end.

Then Dietrich's men pressed into the fight. They struck so that mail-rings flew far away and you could see sword-points fly high through the air, and they drew a hot stream of blood out of the helmets. Hagen of Tronje saw Volker dead. That was the greatest sorrow he had suffered at this whole festival, in kinsman or in vassal. Alas, how fiercely Hagen began to avenge the hero! "Old Hildebrand shall not profit by this. My help lies killed by the hero's hand, the best comrade I ever won." He raised his shield higher and went off hewing.

Bold Helfrich killed Dankwart. Gunther and Giselher were sorry enough when they saw him fall in that great extremity; but he had well avenged his own death with his hands. Meanwhile Wolfhart went back and forth, hewing all the while at Gunther's men. He had come through the hall for the third time, and many a warrior fell at his hands.

Lord Giselher called out to Wolfhart: "Alas, that I ever won so grim an enemy! Noble bold knight, now turn and face me. I will help to end this; it cannot go on any longer."

Wolfhart turned to Giselher in the fight, and each struck the other many a wide wound. He drove at the king with such force that the blood under his feet sprayed up over his head. With swift grim blows beautiful Ute's son received bold Wolfhart. However strong the warrior was, he could not survive: no king so young could ever have been bolder. He struck Wolfhart through his good coat of mail so that the blood ran down from the wound; he wounded Dietrich's man to the death. Truly, no one but a hero could have done it.

When bold Wolfhart felt the wound, he let his shield fall and lifted a strong weapon higher in his hand — it was strong enough — and the hero struck Giselher through helmet and mail-rings. They had each dealt the other a grim death. Now not one of Dietrich's men was left alive. Old Hildebrand saw Wolfhart fall; I think nothing so painful had ever happened to him in his life.

Now Gunther's men were all dead, and Dietrich's too. Hildebrand had gone to where Wolfhart had fallen down into the blood, and he took the bold good warrior in his arms. He wanted to carry him out of the house, but he was a little too heavy, and he had to let him lie. Then the stricken man looked up out of the blood. He saw very well that his uncle would gladly have helped him away.

The mortally wounded man said: "My dear uncle, you cannot be of any use to me now. Guard yourself against Hagen; that seems good advice to me. He carries a grim spirit in his heart. And if my kinsmen want to mourn me after my death, tell the nearest and the best of them from me not to weep for me; there is no need for that. I lie here gloriously dead at a king's hands. And I have paid for my life in here so dearly that the wives of good knights may well weep for it. If anyone asks you about it, you can say straight out: a good hundred lie killed by my hand alone."

Then Hagen too remembered the minstrel, whose life bold Hildebrand had taken. He said to the warrior: "You will pay for my sorrows. You have robbed us here of many a fine warrior." He struck at Hildebrand so that you could plainly hear Balmung ring — the sword bold Hagen had taken from Siegfried when he killed the hero. The old man defended himself; truly, he was bold enough. Dietrich's warrior swung a broad weapon, one that cut terribly, at the hero of Tronje, but he could not wound Gunther's man. Then Hagen struck him through his well-made coat of mail. When old Hildebrand felt the wound, he feared more harm from Hagen's hand. Dietrich's man threw his shield over his back, and with his deep wound the hero escaped from Hagen.

Now none of all those warriors was alive except the two alone, Gunther and Hagen. Old Hildebrand went streaming with blood to where he found Dietrich, and brought him the painful news. He saw the man sitting there in sorrow; and now the prince gained far more sorrow still. He saw Hildebrand in his red coat of mail, and he asked for the news, as his fear drove him to.

"Now tell me, Master Hildebrand, how do you come to be so wet with lifeblood? Who did that to you? I think you have been fighting with the guests in the hall. I forbade you so strictly; you should rightly have kept away from it."

He told his lord: "Hagen did it. He gave me this wound in the hall as I was trying to turn away from the warrior. I barely escaped from the devil with my life."

The lord of Bern said: "It serves you right. You heard me promise those warriors friendship, and you broke the peace I had given them. If it were not an everlasting shame to me, you would lose your life for it."

"Do not be so angry, my lord Dietrich. The harm to me and my kinsmen is far too great already. We wanted to carry Rüdiger away, and King Gunther's men would not allow it."

"Oh, what grief is mine! Is Rüdiger really dead? That is a sorrow to me beyond all my other distress. Noble Gotelind is my aunt's child. Alas for the poor orphans there at Pöchlarn!" His death reminded him of loyalty and sorrow, and he began to weep hard; the hero had cause to. "Alas for the loyal hero I have lost! Truly I shall never get over the loss of King Etzel's vassal. Can you tell me the truth, Master Hildebrand: who was the warrior who killed him?" He said: "Strong Gernot did it, with all his might. And the hero fell dead at Rüdiger's hands too."

He said to Hildebrand: "Now tell my men to arm themselves quickly, because I mean to go over there, and have my bright battle gear brought to me. I will question the heroes from the land of Burgundy myself."

Master Hildebrand said: "Who is going to go with you? All the living men you have, you see standing here beside you. That is I, alone. The others are dead." Dietrich was shaken by the news, and well he might be, for he had never known such sorrow in all the world. He said: "If all my men are dead, then God has forgotten me, poor Dietrich. I was a mighty king, powerful and rich.

"How could it come about," Lord Dietrich said, "that they are all dead, those praiseworthy heroes, at the hands of men worn out by battle, who were themselves in such peril? If it were not for my ill luck, death would still be a stranger to them. Since my misfortune would not let it be otherwise, tell me: is any of the guests still alive?" Master Hildebrand said: "God knows, no one but Hagen alone, and Gunther the noble king."

"Alas, dear Wolfhart, if I have lost you, I may well be sorry I was ever born. Sigestab and Wolfwin and Wolfbrand too! Who will help me now to the land of the Amelungs? Helfrich the bold — is he killed too, and Gerbart and Wichart? How could I ever mourn them enough? This is the last day of my joys. Alas, that no one can die of grief!"

Chapter 39: How Lord Dietrich Overcame Gunther and Hagen

Then Lord Dietrich fetched his own armor, and Master Hildebrand helped him put it on. The powerful man lamented so loudly that the whole house rang with the force of his voice. Then he recovered a true hero's spirit. The good hero was armed now, in grim earnest; he took a strong shield in his hand, and he and Master Hildebrand went quickly from there.

Hagen of Tronje said: "I see Lord Dietrich coming this way. He means to fight us, after the great sorrow that has befallen him here. Today we shall find out who deserves to be called the best. However strong of body Lord Dietrich of Bern thinks himself, and however terrible, if he means to take revenge on us for what has been done to him" — so Hagen said — "I am quite ready to stand against him."

Dietrich and Hildebrand heard this. He came to where he found the two warriors standing outside the house, leaning against the hall, and Dietrich set his good shield down on the ground. In grief and sorrow he said: "Why have you done this, Gunther, mighty king, to me, an exile? What had I done to you? Of all my comfort I am left here alone. It did not seem enough to you, in that great extremity, when you killed the hero Rüdiger; now you have robbed me of every one of my men. Truly, I never did you heroes such a wrong. Think of yourselves and of your own grief, the death of your kinsmen and all the hardship — does it not weigh on the hearts of you good warriors at all? Oh, how heavily Rüdiger's death falls on me! Nothing so painful ever happened to any man in this world. You gave little thought to my suffering or to your own. Whatever kinsmen I had, you have killed them. Truly, I can never mourn my kinsmen enough."

"We are not as guilty as that," said Hagen. "Your warriors came to this house fully armed, in a great company. I think the story has not been told to you correctly."

"What else am I to believe? Hildebrand told me that when my warriors from the Amelung land asked you to give Rüdiger out of the hall, you offered the bold heroes nothing but mockery from up there on the stairs."

The king of the Rhine said: "They said they wanted to carry Rüdiger away. I ordered that refused — to spite Etzel, not your men — until Wolfhart began to hurl insults about it."

The hero of Bern said: "Then it had to be so. Gunther, noble king, by your good breeding, make up to me for the wrongs you have done me, and make such amends, bold knight, that I can give you credit for it. Give yourself up to me as a hostage, you and your man, and I will protect you as best I can, so that no one here among the Huns does you any harm. You will find nothing in me but loyalty and good faith."

"God in heaven forbid," said Hagen, "that two warriors should surrender to you who still stand armed and ready to fight you, and still walk so freely in front of their enemies."

"You should not refuse it," Dietrich said. "Gunther and Hagen, you have both so burdened my heart and my spirit that it would be only right for you to make it up to me. I give you my loyalty, and my hand on it: I will ride home with you to your own land. I will lead you there with honor, or lie dead myself, and for your sakes I will forget my own great suffering."

"Ask it no more," said Hagen. "It would not be a good story to tell of us, that two such bold men surrendered to you. And now there is no one to be seen beside you but Hildebrand alone."

Master Hildebrand said: "God knows, Sir Hagen, the hour is coming when you might be glad to take peace from a man who offers to keep it with you. You could well let my lord's terms content you."

"Yes, I would take the terms," said Hagen, "before I would run so shamefully out of a hall, Master Hildebrand, as you did here. I thought you could stand up to enemies better than that."

Hildebrand answered: "Why throw that at me? Who was it sat on his shield in front of the Waskenstein, in the Vosges, while Walther of Spain killed so many of his kinsmen? You have plenty to show on your own account."

Lord Dietrich said: "It does not suit heroes to bicker like old women. I forbid you, Hildebrand, to say another word. I am an exile, and great sorrows press on me. Let me hear, warrior Hagen," said Dietrich, "what you two swift fighters were saying when you saw me coming toward you armed. You said that you alone would stand against me in a fight."

"No one will deny you that," said Hagen the warrior. "I am willing to try it here with hard blows, unless Nibelung's sword breaks in my hand. It makes me angry that we two have been asked for as hostages."

When Dietrich heard Hagen's grim mood, the swift good warrior quickly snatched up his shield. How fast Hagen sprang at him from the stairs! Nibelung's good sword rang out loudly on Dietrich. Then Lord Dietrich knew well that the bold man was in a grim mood; the lord of Bern began to guard himself against the fearful blows. He knew Hagen well, that splendid warrior, and he feared Balmung too, a weapon strong enough. From time to time Dietrich struck back with cunning, until at last he overcame Hagen in the fight. He gave him a wound that was deep and long.

Then Lord Dietrich thought: "You are worn out by your ordeal. I would have little honor if you lay dead before me. I will try instead whether I can force you to be my hostage." That was done at his peril. He let his shield fall — his strength was great — and locked Hagen of Tronje in his arms. So the bold man was overpowered by him. Noble Gunther began to grieve at that.

Dietrich bound Hagen and led him to where he found the noble queen, and gave into her hand the boldest warrior who ever carried a sword. After her great sorrow she was glad enough. Etzel's wife bowed to the warrior for joy. "May your heart and body be forever blessed. You have made up to me for all my distress. I will serve you for it always, unless death prevents me."

Lord Dietrich said: "You must let him live, noble queen; and it may yet come about that he makes up to you for what he has done to you. He must not suffer for it that you see him standing here bound."

Then she had Hagen taken to a prison where he lay locked up and no one saw him. Gunther, the noble king, began to shout: "Where has the hero of Bern gone? He has done me wrong."

Lord Dietrich went out to meet him. Gunther's courage was worthy of praise: he waited no longer but ran out in front of the hall, and a tremendous clash rose from the swords of the two of them. However long Lord Dietrich had been praised, Gunther was so angry and so wild — after his great sorrow he was his deadly enemy — that it is still told as a wonder that Lord Dietrich came out of it alive. Both their courage and their strength were great. The great hall and the towers rang with the blows as they hewed with their swords at the good helmets. King Gunther had a lordly spirit. But the man of Bern overcame him, as had happened to Hagen before. The hero's blood could be seen flowing through the rings of his mail, from a sharp sword that Dietrich carried. Lord Gunther, for all his weariness, had defended himself with honor.

The lord was bound by Dietrich's hand, though kings should never suffer such bonds. Dietrich thought that if he let the king and his man go free, everyone they met would have to die at their hands. Dietrich of Bern took him by the hand and led him bound to where he found Kriemhild. With his suffering, much of her sorrow was over. She said: "Welcome, Gunther from the land of Burgundy."

He said: "I would bow to you, my dear sister, if your greeting could be kinder. I know you, queen, to be so angry in your heart that you give me and Hagen a very poor welcome."

The hero of Bern said: "Most noble king's wife, never were such good knights given as hostages as I have given you here, high lady. Now, for my sake, treat the exiles well." She said she would do so gladly. Then Lord Dietrich went away from the praiseworthy heroes with tears in his eyes. Later Etzel's wife took grim revenge: she took the lives of both those chosen warriors.

To make their captivity worse she kept them apart, so that neither saw the other again until she carried her brother's head to Hagen. Kriemhild's revenge was enough on both of them.

The queen went to where she saw Hagen. How full of hatred she was when she spoke to the hero: "If you will give me back what you have taken from me, you may still go home alive to the Burgundians."

Grim Hagen said: "You are wasting your words, most noble queen. I have sworn that I will never show the treasure as long as any of my lords is alive; so I will give it to no one."

"Then I will bring it to an end," said the noble woman. She ordered her brother's life taken. They struck off his head, and she carried it by the hair before the hero of Tronje. It was grief enough to him.

When the unhappy man saw his lord's head, the warrior said to Kriemhild: "You have brought it to an end exactly as you wanted, and it has turned out just as I thought it would. Now the noble king of Burgundy is dead, and young Giselher, and Lord Gernot too. No one knows where the treasure is now but God and me — and from you, she-devil, it will stay hidden forever."

She said: "Then you have paid me badly. So I will keep Siegfried's sword at least. My dear love carried it when I last saw him — the man whose loss broke my heart, and the fault was yours."

She drew it from the scabbard; he could not stop her. She meant to take the warrior's life. She lifted it with her hands and struck off his head. King Etzel saw it, and it was grief enough to him.

"Alas!" said the prince. "Now the very best warrior who ever went into battle or carried a shield lies dead at a woman's hands. However much I was his enemy, it grieves me deeply."

Old Hildebrand said: "She shall gain nothing by daring to kill him. Whatever comes to me of it — and though he himself brought me into deadly peril — still I will avenge the bold man of Tronje's death."

Hildebrand sprang at Kriemhild in a rage. He struck the queen a heavy swing of his sword. Her fear of Hildebrand gave her pain; what could it help her that she screamed so terribly? Now all those who were doomed lay dead there. The noble woman was hewn to pieces. Dietrich and Etzel began to weep; they mourned from the heart, both kinsmen and vassals.

Great honor lay there dead. All the people had grief and distress. The king's festival had ended in sorrow, as joy always turns to sorrow in the end. I cannot tell you what happened there after that, except that knights and ladies could be seen weeping, and the noble squires too, for the deaths of their dear kinsmen. Here the story ends: this is the fall of the Nibelungs.
